\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

Because the middle and right vertical homomorphisms are bijective by
Proposition

A.7
, we see that
{\mathrm{Ker}}(\tau_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{E}})\rightarrow{\mathrm{Ker}}(\tau){\,\widehat{\otimes}_{K}\,}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{E}
is bijective as well.
If we consider
\tilde{f}
as an element of
{\mathrm{Ker}}(\tau){\,\widehat{\otimes}_{K}\,}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{E}
and apply the pairing (
1.16
) to it, we find that
\mu(\tilde{f})=0
, for all
\mu\in D_{e}(G)
, as (cf.
[
11
, IV. §4.1 Prop. 2]
)

D_{e}(G)=\left\{\mu\in D(G)\,\middle|\,\mu({\mathrm{Ker}}(\tau))=0\right\}.

The separate continuity of (
1.26
) and the non-degeneracy in
D_{e}(G)
follow from the respective statements for (
1.15
).
Moreover, the description (
1.27
) follows from (
1.16
).

It remains to show the non-degeneracy in
C^{\mathrm{la}}_{e}(G,V)
.
To this end, let
f\in C^{\mathrm{la}}_{e}(G,V)
such that
\mu(f)=0
, for all
\mu\in D_{e}(G)
.
We have to show that
f=0
.
Let
U
be a compact open chart around
e
and
E\subset V
a BH-subspace such that
f\in C^{\mathrm{rig}}(U,\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{E})
.
Then we have the topological isomorphism
C^{\mathrm{rig}}(U,\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{E})\cong C^{\mathrm{rig}}(U,K){\,\widehat{\otimes}_{K}\,}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{E}
, and
C^{\mathrm{rig}}(U,K){\,\otimes_{K}\,}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{E}
is a dense subspace thereof.
Hence we find sequences
(f_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}\subset C^{\mathrm{rig}}(U,K)
,
(a_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}\subset\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{E}
, both converging to
0
, such that
f=\sum_{n\geq 1}f_{n}\otimes a_{n}
.
It follows from our assumption and the description (
1.27
) that

0=\mu(f)=\sum_{n\geq 1}\mu(f_{n})a_{n}\quad\text{, for all $\mu\in D_{e}(G)$.}

This implies that

(1.28)

\sum_{n\geq 1}\lambda(f_{n})a_{n}=0\quad\text{, for all $\lambda\in C^{\mathrm{rig}}(U,K)^{\prime}$,}

since the homomorphism
D_{e}(G)\rightarrow C^{\mathrm{rig}}(U,K)^{\prime}
induced by the duality pairing between
C^{\mathrm{la}}_{e}(G,K)
and
D_{e}(G)
is surjective.

From now on, we identify
C^{\mathrm{rig}}(U,K)
as a locally convex
K
-vector space with the space of sequences tending to
0

c_{0}({\mathbb{N}})\vcentcolon=\left\{(a_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}\subset K^{\mathbb{N}}\,\middle|\,a_{n}\rightarrow 0\text{ as }n\rightarrow\infty\right\}

endowed with the supremum norm.
Our further reasoning uses that
c_{0}({\mathbb{N}})
is a
K
-Banach space which has the so called approximation property, and essentially is a special case of the statement
[
62
, Prop. 4.6]
in the archimedean setting.
Nevertheless, we want to present a streamlined but detailed account.
To prove that
f=0
in
c_{0}({\mathbb{N}}){\,\widehat{\otimes}_{K}\,}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{E}
, we will show that

T(f)=0\quad\text{, for all $T\in\big(c_{0}({\mathbb{N}}){\,\widehat{\otimes}_{K}\,}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{E}\big)^{\prime}$ with $T\neq 0$.}

We fix such
T
, and note that we have an isomorphism of
K
-vector spaces

(1.29)

\big(c_{0}({\mathbb{N}}){\,\widehat{\otimes}_{K}\,}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{E}\big)^{\prime}\overset{\cong}{\longrightarrow}{\mathcal{L}}\big(c_{0}({\mathbb{N}}),\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{E}^{\prime}\big)\,,\quad S\longmapsto\big[(a_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}\mapsto[v\mapsto S((a_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}\otimes v)]\big],

by
[
67
, Rmk. 20.12]
.
Set
C\vcentcolon=\lVert T\rVert_{{\mathcal{L}}(c_{0}({\mathbb{N}}),\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{E}^{\prime})}^{-1}
, and let
\varepsilon>0
.
We now consider the projections

P_{m}\colon c_{0}({\mathbb{N}})\longrightarrow c_{0}({\mathbb{N}})\,,\quad(x_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}\longmapsto(x_{1},\ldots,x_{m},0,\ldots)\quad\text{, for $m\in{\mathbb{N}}$,}

which constitute continuous endomorphisms of finite rank.
As
f_{n}\rightarrow 0
in
c_{0}({\mathbb{N}})
, there exists some
N\in{\mathbb{N}}
such that
\lVert f_{n}\rVert_{c_{0}({\mathbb{N}})}<C\,\varepsilon
, for all
n\geq N
.
After enlarging
N
, we may assume that
\lVert f_{n}-P_{N}(f_{n})\rVert_{c_{0}({\mathbb{N}})}<C\,\varepsilon
, even for all
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
.

We set
S\vcentcolon=T\circ P_{N}\in{\mathcal{L}}\big(c_{0}({\mathbb{N}}),\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{E}^{\prime}\big)
.
Because
S
is of finite rank, there exist
r\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{r}\in c_{0}({\mathbb{N}})^{\prime}
, and
\ell_{1},\ldots,\ell_{r}\in\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{E}^{\prime}
such that
S=\big[x\mapsto\sum_{i=1}^{r}\lambda_{i}(x)\,\ell_{i}\big]
(cf.
[
67
, §18]
).
Hence, when we apply
S\in\big(c_{0}({\mathbb{N}}){\,\widehat{\otimes}_{K}\,}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{E}\big)^{\prime}
to
f
, using (
1.29
) we compute

\displaystyle S(f)=\sum_{n\geq 1}\sum_{i=1}^{r}\lambda_{i}(f_{n})\,\ell_{i}(a_{n})=\sum_{i=1}^{r}\ell_{i}\bigg(\sum_{n\geq 1}\lambda_{i}(f_{n})\,a_{n}\bigg)=0.

For the last equality, we have used (
1.28
) here.
Therefore

\displaystyle{\lvert{T(f)}\rvert}

\displaystyle\leq{\lvert{T(f)-S(f)}\rvert}+{\lvert{S(f)}\rvert}={\lvert{T(f)-S(f)}\rvert}=\bigg\lvert\sum_{n\geq 1}T(f_{n})(a_{n})-S(f_{n})(a_{n})\bigg\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\max_{n\geq 1}\Big(\lVert T(f_{n})-S(f_{n})\rVert_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{E}^{\prime}}\cdot\lVert a_{n}\rVert_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{E}}\Big)=\max_{n\geq 1}\Big(\big\lVert T\big(f_{n}-P_{N}(f_{n})\big)\big\rVert_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{E}^{\prime}}\cdot\lVert a_{n}\rVert_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{E}}\Big)

\displaystyle\leq\max_{n\geq 1}\Big(\lVert T\rVert_{{\mathcal{L}}(c_{0}({\mathbb{N}}),\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{E}^{\prime})}\cdot\lVert f_{n}-P_{N}(f_{n})\rVert_{c_{0}({\mathbb{N}})}\cdot\lVert a_{n}\rVert_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{E}}\Big)<\varepsilon\cdot\max_{n\geq 1}\,\lVert a_{n}\rVert_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{E}}.

For
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0
, this shows that
T(f)=0
.
Since
T\in\big(c_{0}({\mathbb{N}}){\,\widehat{\otimes}_{K}\,}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{E}\big)^{\prime}
with
T\neq 0
was arbitrary, we can conclude that
f=0
.
∎

Corollary 1.6.13
.

Let
G
be a locally
L
-analytic Lie group.
Then
{\mathrm{hy}}(G)\subset D_{e}(G)
is a dense
K
-subalgebra.
In particular, for any Hausdorff locally convex
K
-vector space
V\neq(0)
, the
K
-bilinear pairing

{\mathrm{hy}}(G)\times C^{\mathrm{la}}_{e}(G,V)\longrightarrow V

induced from (
1.26
) is non-degenerate
5
5

5

In
[
30
, Kor. 4.7.4]
Féaux de Lacroix shows that the pairing
U({\mathfrak{g}})\otimes_{L}K\times C^{\mathrm{la}}_{e}(G,V)\rightarrow V
is non-degenerate for the case
{\rm char}(L)=0
. His proof uses differentiation with respect to elements of
{\mathfrak{g}}
which is why we pursue a different method here.
.

Proof.

As a first step, we show that the above pairing is non-degenerate when
V=K
.
In
{\mathrm{hy}}(G)
this is clear.
On the other hand, let
f\in C^{\mathrm{la}}_{e}(G,K)
such that
\mu(f)=0
, for all
\mu\in{\mathrm{hy}}(G)
.
For any
n\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
, it follows that
f\in{\mathrm{hy}}(G)_{n}^{\perp}
where we consider the orthogonal under (
1.26
)

{\mathrm{hy}}(G)_{n}^{\perp}=\left\{f^{\prime}\in C^{\mathrm{la}}_{e}(G,K)\,\middle|\,\forall\mu\in{\mathrm{hy}}(G)_{n}:\mu(f^{\prime})=0\right\}.

But we have
{\mathrm{hy}}(G)_{n}=({\mathfrak{m}}_{e}^{n+1})^{\perp}
by definition, and
\big(({\mathfrak{m}}_{e}^{n+1})^{\perp}\big)^{\perp}={\mathfrak{m}}_{e}^{n+1}
by
[
11
, II. §6.3 Cor. 3]
.
Hence
f\in\bigcap_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}}{\mathfrak{m}}_{e}^{n+1}
, and we apply Krull’s intersection theorem to conclude that
f=0
.

To show the density of
{\mathrm{hy}}(G)\subset D_{e}(G)
, assume, for the sake of contradiction, that there is
\delta\in D_{e}(G)\setminus\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}
.
By the Hahn–Banach theorem
[
67
, Cor. 9.3]
there exists
f\in C^{\mathrm{la}}_{e}(G,K)
such that
\mu(f)=0
, for all
\mu\in\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}
, and
\delta(f)=1
.
But as we have just seen, this implies
f=0
which is a contradiction.

The non-degeneracy of the pairing between
{\mathrm{hy}}(G)
and
C^{\mathrm{la}}_{e}(G,V)
now follows from
{\mathrm{hy}}(G)
being a dense subspace of
D_{e}(G)
and
Proposition

1.6.12
.
∎

Proposition 1.6.14
.

Let
G
be a compact locally
L
-analytic Lie group, and
V
a Hausdorff locally convex
K
-vector space.
If we consider
C^{\mathrm{la}}(G,V)
with the left regular
G
-representation, then we have in
V
the equality

(\dot{\mu}\ast f)(e)=\mu(f)\quad\text{, for all $\mu\in D(G)$, $f\in C^{\mathrm{la}}(G,V)$.}

Proof.

Let

o_{C^{\mathrm{la}}(G,V)}\colon C^{\mathrm{la}}(G,V)\longrightarrow C^{\mathrm{la}}\big(G,C^{\mathrm{la}}(G,V)\big)\,,\quad f\longmapsto\rho_{f},

denote the orbit homomorphism that sends a function
f
to its orbit map under the left regular
G
-representation.
Moreover, let
I_{W}
denote the integration map (
1.13
) associated with a Hausdorff locally convex
K
-vector space
W
.
Now fix
\mu\in D(G)
, and consider the following commutative diagram using that
I_{W}
is natural:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{\mathrm{la}}\big(G,C^{\mathrm{la}}(G,V)\big)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{L}}_{b}\big(D(G),C^{\mathrm{la}}(G,K)\big)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{\mathrm{la}}(G,V)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{\mathrm{la}}\big(G,C^{\mathrm{la}}(G,V)\big)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{L}}_{b}\big(D(G),C^{\mathrm{la}}(G,V)\big)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{\mathrm{la}}(G,V)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{\mathrm{la}}(G,V)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{L}}_{b}(D(G),V)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I_{C^{\mathrm{la}}(G,V)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{inv}}^{\sharp}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{ev}_{\dot{\mu}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dot{({\,\_\,})}^{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces o_{C^{\mathrm{la}}(G,V)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{id}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I_{C^{\mathrm{la}}(G,V)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathrm{ev}_{e})_{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{ev}_{\mu}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathrm{ev}_{e})_{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{ev}_{e}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I_{V}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{ev}_{\mu}}

Taking the image of a function
f\in C^{\mathrm{la}}(G,V)
under the homomorphisms of the top path to
V
then yields
\big(I_{C^{\mathrm{la}}(G,V)}(\rho_{f})(\dot{\mu})\big)(e)=(\dot{\mu}\ast f)(e)
.
Taking the image via going the bottom path yields
I_{V}(f)(\mu)=\mu(f)
.
Hence the claim follows from the commutativity of the above diagram.
∎

Proposition 1.6.15
.

Let
G
be a locally
L
-analytic Lie group.
For every Hausdorff locally convex
K
-vector space
V
, the
G
-representation

(1.30)

G\times C^{\mathrm{la}}_{e}(G,V)\longrightarrow C^{\mathrm{la}}_{e}(G,V)\,,\quad(g,f)\longmapsto f(g^{-1}{\,\_\,}g),

is locally analytic.

The adjoint representation, for every
n\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
,

{\mathrm{Ad}}_{n}\colon G\times{\mathrm{hy}}(G)_{n}\longrightarrow{\mathrm{hy}}(G)_{n}\,,\quad(g,\mu)\longmapsto{\mathrm{Ad}}_{n}(g)(\mu)\vcentcolon=\left[f\mapsto\mu\big(f(g{\,\_\,}g^{-1})\big)\right],

is locally analytic.

Proof.

For (i), let
H\subset G
be a compact open subgroup.
By
Example

1.3.7
(ii),
C^{\mathrm{la}}(H,V)
with the
H
-action by conjugation is a locally analytic
H
-representation.
Since the strict epimorphism
C^{\mathrm{la}}(H,V)\rightarrow C^{\mathrm{la}}_{e}(G,V)
is
H
-equivariant with respect to the action by conjugation,
C^{\mathrm{la}}_{e}(G,V)
is a locally analytic
H
-representation by
Proposition

1.3.8
(i).
Moreover, the functoriality from
Proposition

1.6.4
shows that
G
acts on
C^{\mathrm{la}}_{e}(G,V)
by topological endomorphisms.
Hence
Proposition

1.3.9
implies that
C^{\mathrm{la}}_{e}(G,V)
is a locally analytic
G
-representation.

For the second statement, let
V=K
, and consider the locally analytic
G
-representation
C^{\mathrm{la}}_{e}(G,K)
with the
G
-action of (i).
As this representation is of compact type,
D_{e}(G)
is a separately continuous
D(G)
-module with respect to the structure induced from (
1.30
) by
Proposition

1.4.10
(ii).
We claim that
{\mathrm{hy}}(G)_{n}\subset D_{e}(G)
, for
n\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
, is a
D(G)
-submodule.
Indeed, if
f\in{\mathfrak{m}}_{e}\subset C^{\mathrm{la}}_{e}(G,K)
then

\mathrm{ev}_{e}\big(f(g^{-1}{\,\_\,}g)\big)=f(g^{-1}eg)=f(e)=0,

which shows that
f(g^{-1}{\,\_\,}g)\in{\mathfrak{m}}_{e}
, for all
g\in G
.
Moreover, in (
1.30
)
G
acts by
K
-algebra homomorphisms.
Hence
{\mathfrak{m}}_{e}^{n+1}\subset C^{\mathrm{la}}_{e}(G,K)
is a locally analytic
G
-subrepresentation.
It follows that
{\mathrm{hy}}(G)_{n}\subset D_{e}(G)
is a
D(G)
-submodule.
Since
{\mathrm{hy}}(G)_{n}
is finite dimensional, in particular it is of LB-type.
Therefore, the equivalence of
Proposition

1.4.10
(i) shows that
{\mathrm{hy}}(G)_{n}
carries the structure of a locally analytic
G
-representation and this is given by
{\mathrm{Ad}}_{n}
.
∎

Definition and Proposition 1.6.16

(cf.
[
58
, Prop. 3.5]
)
.

Assume that
K
is a finite extension of
L
.
Let
G
be a locally
L
-analytic Lie group, and
H\subset G
a locally
L
-analytic subgroup.
Then every element of the
K
-subalgebra of
D(G,K)
generated by
{\mathrm{hy}}(G,K)
and
D(H,K)
is a finite sum of elements of the form
\mu\ast\delta
, for
\mu\in{\mathrm{hy}}(G,K)
,
\delta\in D(H,K)
.

We denote this subalgebra by
D({\mathfrak{g}},H,K)\subset D(G,K)
, or
D({\mathfrak{g}},H)
when the coefficient field is implied.

Proof.

With the appropriate adjustments we proceed analogously to the proof of
[
58
, Prop. 3.5]
.
It suffices to show that, for all
\delta\in D(H)
,
\mu\in{\mathrm{hy}}(G)
,
\delta\ast\mu
is a finite sum of elements
\mu^{\prime}\ast\delta^{\prime}
, for
\mu^{\prime}\in{\mathrm{hy}}(G)
,
\delta^{\prime}\in D(H)
.
We fix such
\delta
and
\mu
, and may assume that
\mu\in{\mathrm{hy}}(G)_{n}
, for some
n\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
.
By
Proposition

1.3.10
the adjoint representation
{\mathrm{Ad}}_{n}
on
{\mathrm{hy}}(G)_{n}
is given by a locally
L
-analytic map of locally
L
-analytic Lie groups
G\rightarrow{\mathrm{GL}}\big({\mathrm{hy}}(G)_{n}\big)
.
Hence, for an
L
-Basis
\mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{r}
of
{\mathrm{hy}}(G)_{n}
, there exist
c_{1},\ldots,c_{r}\in C^{\mathrm{la}}(G,K)
such that

{\mathrm{Ad}}_{n}(g)(\mu)=\sum_{i=1}^{r}c_{i}(g)\,\mu_{i}\quad\text{, for all $g\in G$.}

We define
\delta_{i}\in D(H)
, for
i=1,\ldots,r
, by

\delta_{i}(f)\vcentcolon=\delta\big[h\mapsto c_{i}(h)\,f(h)\big]\quad\text{, for $f\in C^{\mathrm{la}}(H,K)$.}

Then, for
f\in C^{\mathrm{la}}(G,K)
, we compute

\displaystyle(\delta\ast\mu)(f)

\displaystyle=\delta\Big[h\mapsto\mu\big[g\mapsto f(hg)\big]\Big]=\delta\Big[h\mapsto{\mathrm{Ad}}_{n}(h)(\mu)\big[g\mapsto f(gh)\big]\Big]

\displaystyle=\delta\Bigg[h\mapsto\bigg(\sum_{i=1}^{r}c_{i}(h)\,\mu_{i}\bigg)\big[g\mapsto f(gh)\big]\Bigg]=\sum_{i=1}^{r}\delta\Big[h\mapsto c_{i}(h)\,\mu_{i}\big[g\mapsto f(gh)\big]\Big]

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{r}\delta\Big[h\mapsto\mu_{i}\big[g\mapsto c_{i}(h)\,f(gh)\big]\Big]=\sum_{i=1}^{r}\mu_{i}\Big[g\mapsto\delta\big[h\mapsto c_{i}(h)\,f(gh)\big]\Big]

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{r}\mu_{i}\Big[g\mapsto\delta_{i}\big[h\mapsto f(gh)\big]\Big]=\sum_{i=1}^{r}(\mu_{i}\ast\delta_{i})(f).

using
Corollary

1.4.5
at several instances.
Hence, we see that
\delta\ast\mu=\sum_{i=1}^{r}\mu_{i}\ast\delta_{i}
.
∎

Corollary 1.6.17
.

Suppose that
K
is a finite extension of
L
, and let
V
be a locally analytic representation of a locally
L
-analytic Lie group
G
.
Then we have

g.(\mu\ast v)={\mathrm{Ad}}_{n}(g)(\mu)\ast(g.v)\quad\text{, for all $g\in G$, $\mu\in{\mathrm{hy}}(G)$, $v\in V$,}

where
n\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
such that
\mu\in{\mathrm{hy}}(G)_{n}
.

Proof.

For
g\in G
and
\mu\in{\mathrm{hy}}(G)_{n}
, we find
\mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{r}\in{\mathrm{hy}}(G)_{n}
and
c_{1},\ldots,c_{r}\in C^{\mathrm{la}}(G,K)
such that

{\mathrm{Ad}}_{n}(g)(\mu)=\sum_{i=1}^{r}c_{i}(g)\,\mu_{i}

and consequently
\delta_{g}\ast\mu=\sum_{i=1}^{r}c_{i}(g)\,\mu_{i}\ast\delta_{g}
like in the proof of the above proposition.
We then compute, for
v\in V
,

\displaystyle{\mathrm{Ad}}_{n}(g)(\mu)\ast(g.v)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{r}c_{i}(g)\,\mu_{i}\ast(g.v)=\sum_{i=1}^{r}c_{i}(g)\,\mu_{i}\ast(\delta_{g}\ast v)

\displaystyle=\bigg(\sum_{i=1}^{r}c_{i}(g)\,\mu_{i}\ast\delta_{g}\bigg)\ast v=(\delta_{g}\ast\mu)\ast v=g.(\mu\ast v).

∎

We want to characterize modules over the
K
-algebras
D({\mathfrak{g}},H)
analogously to the
p
-adic situation considered by Agrawal and Strauch in
[
1
]
.

Definition 1.6.18

(cf.
[
1
, Def. 7.4.1]
)
.

Assume that
K
is a finite extension of
L
, and let
G
be a locally
L
-analytic Lie group with a locally
L
-analytic subgroup
H\subset G
.
We call a locally analytic
H
-representation
V
which simultaneously is a
{\mathrm{hy}}(G)
-module a
locally analytic
({\mathrm{hy}}(G),H)
-module
if the scalar multiplication map
{\mathrm{hy}}(G)\times V\rightarrow V
is separately continuous when
{\mathrm{hy}}(G)
is endowed with the subspace topology of
{\mathrm{hy}}(G)\subset D(G)
and the following two compatibly conditions hold:
The action of
{\mathrm{hy}}(H)
as a
K
-subalgebra of
{\mathrm{hy}}(G)
agrees with the action induced from
Proposition

1.4.9
(i) of
{\mathrm{hy}}(H)
as a
K
-subalgebra of
D(H)
.

For all
h\in H
,
\mu\in{\mathrm{hy}}(G)
,
v\in V
, and
n\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
with
\mu\in{\mathrm{hy}}(G)_{n}
, we have

h.(\mu\ast v)={\mathrm{Ad}}_{n}(h)(\mu)\ast(h.v).

Remark 1.6.19
.

It follows from
Corollary

1.6.17
that a locally analytic
G
-representation canonically carries the structure of a locally analytic
({\mathrm{hy}}(G),H)
-module, for any locally
L
-analytic subgroup
H\subset G
.

Corollary 1.6.20

(cf.
[
1
, Lemma 7.4.2]
)
.

Giving a locally analytic
({\mathrm{hy}}(G),H)
-module structure is naturally equivalent to giving a separately continuous
D({\mathfrak{g}},H)
-module structure.
Moreover, passing to the strong dual space yields an anti-equivalence of categories

\left(\begin{subarray}{c}\text{\small locally analytic $({\mathrm{hy}}(G),H)$-modules}\\
\text{\small on locally convex $K$-vector spaces}\\
\text{\small of compact type with continuous $H$- }\\
\text{\small and ${\mathrm{hy}}(G)$-equivariant homomorphisms}\end{subarray}\right)\longrightarrow\left(\begin{subarray}{c}\text{\small separately continuous $D({\mathfrak{g}},H)$-modules}\\
\text{\small on nuclear $K$-Fr\'{e}chet spaces}\\
\text{\small with continuous $D({\mathfrak{g}},H)$-module maps}\end{subarray}\right).

Proof.

A locally analytic
({\mathrm{hy}}(G),H)
-module
V
naturally comes with a separately continuous
D(H)
-module structure by
Proposition

1.4.10
(i).
Via setting

(\mu\ast\delta)\ast v\vcentcolon=\mu\ast(\delta\ast v)\quad\text{, for $\mu\in{\mathrm{hy}}(G)$, $\delta\in D(H)$, $v\in V$,}

and
K
-linear extension, we obtain a separately continuous homomorphism

D({\mathfrak{g}},H)\times V\longrightarrow V

which is well defined by condition (1).
To see that this defines a
D({\mathfrak{g}},H)
-module structure, the only non-trivial assertion to verify is the associativity.
Utilizing the associativity of the
{\mathrm{hy}}(G)
- and
D(H)
-actions and the density of the Dirac distributions, it suffices to show that

(\delta_{h}\ast\mu)\ast v=\delta_{h}\ast(\mu\ast v)\quad\text{, for all $h\in H$, $\mu\in{\mathrm{hy}}(G)$, and $v\in V$.}

But like in the proof of
Corollary

1.6.17
we see that

(\delta_{h}\ast\mu)\ast v={\mathrm{Ad}}_{n}(h)(\mu)\ast(h.v).

Therefore the associativity follows from condition (2).
This also shows that conversely we obtain a locally analytic
({\mathrm{hy}}(G),H)
-module structure on a separately continuous
D({\mathfrak{g}},H)
-module.

For the anti-equivalence of categories, one argues analogously to the proof of
Proposition

1.4.10
(ii).
∎

1.7.
Non-Archimedean Manifolds Arising from Rigid Analytic Spaces

Here we want to associate locally analytic manifolds to rigid analytic spaces and schemes satisfying some assumptions.
When applied to a smooth algebraic group
{\mathrm{\bf G}}
this allows us to relate the hyperalgebra of the locally analytic Lie group associated with
{\mathrm{\bf G}}
to the (algebraic) distribution algebra
{\rm Dist}({\mathrm{\bf G}})
as defined in
[
41
, I. §7.7]
.
For the moment, let
L
be a complete non-archimedean field with non-trivial absolute value
{\lvert{{\,\_\,}}\rvert}
.

Let
X
be a rigid analytic
L
-space and let
{\mathfrak{m}}_{x}
denote the maximal ideal of
{\mathcal{O}}_{X,x}
, for
x\in X
.
We consider on the set of
L
-valued points of
X

X(L)=\left\{x\in X\,\middle|\,{\mathcal{O}}_{X,x}/{\mathfrak{m}}_{x}=L\right\}

the topology generated by
U(L)\subset X(L)
, for all affinoid subdomains
U\subset X
.

Lemma 1.7.1
.

For the affinoid unit ball
{\mathbb{B}}^{n}={\mathrm{Sp}}\,K\langle T_{1},\ldots,T_{n}\rangle
, for
n\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
, the topology defined on
{\mathbb{B}}^{n}(L)=B_{1}^{n}(0)
as above agrees with the “euclidean” topology given via
B_{1}^{n}(0)\subset L^{n}
.

Proof.

By
[
7
, 7.2.5 Cor. 4]
the affinoid subdomains
U\subset{\mathbb{B}}^{n}
form a basis of the canonical topology of
{\mathbb{B}}^{n}
.
Moreover, for any
x=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})\in{\mathbb{B}}^{n}
, the Weierstraß domains

{\mathbb{B}}^{n}(f_{1},\ldots,f_{r})\vcentcolon=\big\{y\in{\mathbb{B}}^{n}\,\big|\,{\lvert{f_{1}(y)}\rvert}\leq 1,\ldots,{\lvert{f_{r}(y)}\rvert}\leq 1\big\}={\mathbb{B}}^{n}(f_{1})\cap\ldots\cap{\mathbb{B}}^{n}(f_{r}),

for
f_{1},\ldots,f_{r}\in{\mathfrak{m}}_{x}
, form a basis of neighbourhoods of
x
in the canonical topology
[
7
, 7.2.1, Prop. 3 (ii)]
.

For
L
-valued
x\in{\mathbb{B}}^{n}(L)
and
f\in{\mathfrak{m}}_{x}=(T_{1}-x_{1},\ldots,T_{n}-x_{n})
, let
f^{\prime}_{i}\in L\langle T_{1},\ldots,T_{n}\rangle
such that
f=f^{\prime}_{1}\,(T_{1}-x_{1})+\ldots+f^{\prime}_{n}\,(T_{n}-x_{n})
.
We moreover find
c\in L^{\times}
such that
{\lvert{c}\rvert}=\max_{i=1}^{n}{\lvert{f^{\prime}_{i}}\rvert}_{\mathrm{sup}}=\vcentcolon\varepsilon^{-1}
.
Then, for
y\in{\mathbb{B}}^{n}_{{\varepsilon},x}\vcentcolon={\mathbb{B}}^{n}\big(c\,(T_{1}-x_{1}),\ldots,c\,(T_{n}-x_{n})\big)
, i.e.
y\in{\mathbb{B}}^{n}
satisfying
\max_{i=0}^{n}{\lvert{y_{i}-x_{i}}\rvert}\leq{\varepsilon}
, we have

{\lvert{f(y)}\rvert}\leq\max_{i=1}^{n}{\lvert{f^{\prime}_{i}(y)}\rvert}{\lvert{y_{i}-x_{i}}\rvert}\leq\varepsilon^{-1}\max_{i=1}^{n}{\lvert{y_{i}-x_{i}}\rvert}\leq 1,

and therefore
{\mathbb{B}}^{n}_{{\varepsilon},x}\subset{\mathbb{B}}^{n}(f)
.
We conclude that the
{\mathbb{B}}^{n}_{{\varepsilon},x}
, for
\varepsilon\in{\lvert{L^{\times}}\rvert}
, constitute a neighbourhood basis of
x
for the canonical topology.
But we also have
{\mathbb{B}}^{n}_{{\varepsilon},x}(L)=B^{n}_{\varepsilon}(x)
which shows that the “euclidean” topology on
{\mathbb{B}}^{n}(L)
is finer than the topology defined via the affinoid subdomains of
{\mathbb{B}}^{n}
.

That the two topologies agree now easily follows from
B^{n}_{\varepsilon}(x)={\mathbb{B}}^{n}_{\varepsilon,x}(L)
and the fact that the
{\mathbb{B}}^{n}_{\varepsilon,x}
are affinoid subdomains, for
x\in{\mathbb{B}}^{n}(L)
and
\varepsilon\in{\lvert{L^{\times}}\rvert}
.
∎

For
X
with good properties, we now want to endow
X(L)
with the structure of a locally
L
-analytic manifold.
To define charts, we will use the following lemma.
Its statement is probably well-known but we include a proof since we could not find it in the literature.

Lemma 1.7.2
.

Let
X
be a rigid analytic
L
-variety, and
x\in X
a regular
L
-rational point of local dimension
n
.
Then there exists an open affinoid subdomain
U\subset X
with
x\in U
such that
U
is isomorphic to the
n
-dimensional unit ball
{\mathbb{B}}^{n}
.
Moreover, for a system of regular parameters
(f_{1},\ldots,f_{n})
of
{\mathfrak{m}}_{x}
, the isomorphism
\varphi\colon U\xrightarrow{\cong}{\mathbb{B}}^{n}
can be chosen in such a way that
\varphi(x)=0
and
T_{i}
is mapped to
f_{i}
under the induced
{\mathcal{A}}(L^{n},L)\cong{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{B}}^{n},0}\xrightarrow{\cong}{\mathcal{O}}_{X,x}
.

Proof.

We may assume that
X
is affinoid, say
X={\mathrm{Sp}}\,A
, for some affinoid
L
-algebra
A
.
Because
x\in X
is regular,
A_{{\mathfrak{m}}}
is a regular local ring (
[
7
, 7.3.2, Prop. 8(i)]
) where
{\mathfrak{m}}\subset A
denotes the maximal ideal corresponding to
x
.

Let
(\tilde{f}_{1},\ldots,\tilde{f}_{n})={\mathfrak{m}}A_{\mathfrak{m}}
denote a system of regular parameters, and let
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{f}_{i}\in{\mathcal{O}}_{X,x}
be the image of
\tilde{f}_{i}
under
A_{\mathfrak{m}}\rightarrow{\mathcal{O}}_{X,x}
, see
[
7
, 7.3.2, Prop. 3]
.
After shrinking
X
, we may assume that the
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{f}_{i}
lift to
f_{i}\in{\mathfrak{m}}
, so that
f_{i}
is mapped to
\tilde{f}_{i}
under the localization map
A\rightarrow A_{\mathfrak{m}}
.
Using that
{\mathfrak{m}}\subset A
is finitely generated, one verifies that there exists
s\in A\setminus{\mathfrak{m}}
such that
s\,{\mathfrak{m}}\subset(f_{1},\ldots,f_{n})
and
{\lvert{s(x)}\rvert}\geq 1
.
Via replacing
A
by the completed localization
A\langle s^{-1}\rangle
, we may assume that
{\mathfrak{m}}=(f_{1},\ldots,f_{n})
.
Furthermore, by scaling we may assume that
{\lvert{f_{i}}\rvert}_{\mathrm{sup}}\leq 1
or equivalently that the
f_{i}
are power-bounded, see
[
7
, 6.2.3, Prop. 1]
.
Therefore there exists a continuous homomorphism of
L
-algebras
[
7
, 6.1.1, Prop. 4]

\varphi^{\flat}\colon L\langle T_{1},\ldots,T_{n}\rangle\longrightarrow A\,,\quad T_{i}\longmapsto f_{i},

which induces a morphism
\varphi\colon X\rightarrow{\mathbb{B}}^{n}
of affinoid
L
-varieties.
It follows that
\varphi(x)=0\in{\mathbb{B}}^{n}
and that
x
is the only point of
X
which is mapped to
0
since
{\mathfrak{m}}=(f_{1},\ldots,f_{n})
.
Now
\varphi^{\flat}
induces a homomorphism of the completion of the local rings

\widehat{\varphi}^{\flat}_{x}\colon L\mkern-3.0mu\left[\mkern-2.5mu\left[T_{1},\ldots,T_{n}\right]\mkern-2.5mu\right]\cong\widehat{{\mathcal{O}}}_{{\mathbb{B}}^{n},0}\longrightarrow\widehat{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X,x}\,,\quad T_{i}\longmapsto\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{f}.

As the
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{f},\ldots,\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{f}
generate
{\mathfrak{m}}\widehat{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X,x}
and
\widehat{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X,x}/{\mathfrak{m}}\widehat{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X,x}\cong L
, we may apply
[
25
, Thm. 7.16 b.]
to conclude that
\widehat{\varphi}^{\flat}_{x}
is surjective.
By considering the dimensions of these rings it follows that
\widehat{\varphi}^{\flat}_{x}
is in fact an isomorphism.
Then
[
7
, 7.3.3, Prop. 5]
implies that there exists an affinoid subdomain
V\subset{\mathbb{B}}^{n}
containing
0
such that
\varphi\colon\varphi^{-1}(V)\rightarrow V
is an isomorphism.
But the Weierstraß domains
{\mathbb{B}}^{n}(c\,T_{1},\ldots,c\,T_{n})
, for
c\in L^{\times}
with
{\lvert{c}\rvert}\geq 1
, form a basis of neighbourhoods of
0\in{\mathbb{B}}^{n}
, cf. the proof of
Lemma

1.7.1
.
In this way we obtain the sought open affinoid subdomain

U\vcentcolon=\varphi^{-1}\big({\mathbb{B}}^{n}(c\,T_{1},\ldots,c\,T_{n})\big)\overset{\varphi}{\longrightarrow}{\mathbb{B}}^{n}(c\,T_{1},\ldots,c\,T_{n})\cong{\mathbb{B}}^{n}.

∎

Lemma 1.7.3
.

Let
{\mathbb{B}}^{n}\rightarrow{\mathbb{B}}^{m}
be a morphism of rigid analytic
L
-spaces, for
n,m\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
.
Then the induced map
B_{1}^{n}(0)\rightarrow B_{1}^{m}(0)
is given by convergent power series.

Proof.

By
[
7
, 6.1.1 Prop. 4]
we have

\displaystyle{\mathrm{Hom}}({\mathbb{B}}^{n},{\mathbb{B}}^{m})\cong{\mathrm{Hom}}_{L-alg}\big(L\langle Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{m}\rangle,L\langle X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}\rangle\big)\cong\big({\mathcal{O}}_{L}\langle X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}\rangle\big)^{m}.

where to
(f_{1},\ldots,f_{m})\in\big({\mathcal{O}}_{L}\langle X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}\rangle\big)^{m}
one associates the homomorphism which on
L
-valued points is given by

{\mathbb{B}}^{n}(L)\longrightarrow{\mathbb{B}}^{m}(L)\,,\quad z\longmapsto\big(f_{1}(z),\ldots,f_{m}(z)\big).

∎

Definition 1.7.4

(
[
43
, Def. 2.5.6]
)
.

A rigid analytic
L
-space is defined to be
of countable type
if there exists an admissible covering
X=\bigcup_{i\in I}U_{i}
by affinoid open subdomains
U_{i}\subset X
such that
I
is at most countable.

Remarks 1.7.5
.

Examples of such spaces include the rigid analytic
L
-space associated to a scheme of finite type over
L

[
43
, Rmk. 2.5.11]
.

When the field
L
contains a dense countable subfield (e.g. when
L
is a non-archimedean local field), any admissible open subset
U
of a rigid analytic
L
-space
X
of countable type is of countable type itself
6
6

6

We learned about this from
https://mathoverflow.net/q/155500
(version: 2014-01-23), and we follow the reasoning suggested there by the user “ACL” (
https://mathoverflow.net/users/10696/acl
) for a proof.
.

Proof of (ii).

Taking an at most countable admissible covering
X=\bigcup_{i\in I}U_{i}
by open affinoid subdomains
U_{i}\subset X
, and considering the admissible open
U\cap U_{i}\subset U_{i}
, we may assume that
X
is affinoid.

Hence, let
X={\mathrm{Sp}}\,A
, for some affinoid
L
-algebra
A
, and let
U=\bigcup_{i\in I}U_{i}
be some admissible covering by open affinoid subdomains
U_{i}\subset X
.
This admissible covering is an open covering with respect to the canonical topology on
X
.
By
[
5
, Prop. 2.1.15]
, we have a topological embedding
X\hookrightarrow{\mathcal{M}}(A)
where
{\mathcal{M}}(A)
denotes the Berkovich spectrum associated with
A
.
Moreover, using that
L
contains a dense countable subfield, one can find a topological embedding
{\mathcal{M}}(A)\hookrightarrow[0,1]^{\mathbb{N}}
, see
[
18
, p. 4]
.
Therefore
U
with its induced subspace topology has a countable basis
[
9
, Ch. IX. §2.8 Prop. 12]
.
But this implies that there already exists an at most countable subset
J\subset I
such that
U=\bigcup_{i\in J}U_{i}
is a covering
[
9
, Ch. IX. §2.8 Prop. 13]
.
∎

Definition and Proposition 1.7.6
.

Let
X
be a smooth, separated rigid analytic
L
-space of countable type.
Then
X(L)
with the topology generated by
U(L)\subset X(L)
, for all affinoid subdomains
U\subset X
, and the atlas with charts induced by the isomorphisms of
Lemma

1.7.2
is a locally
L
-analytic manifold.
We will denote it by
X^{\mathrm{la}}
.

Proof.

For each
x\in X(L)
, by
Lemma

1.7.2
there exist an affinoid subdomain
U_{x}\subset X
containing
x
and an isomorphism
\varphi_{x}\colon U_{x}\xrightarrow{\cong}{\mathbb{B}}^{n_{x}}
,
x\mapsto 0
, where
n_{x}
is the local dimension at
x
.
These isomorphisms yield charts

\varphi_{x}\colon U_{x}(L)\overset{\cong}{\longrightarrow}B_{1}^{n_{x}}(0)\subset L^{n_{x}}

which we want to show to be compatible.
For
x,y\in X(L)
we obtain an isomorphism of rigid analytic
L
-spaces

\varphi_{y}\circ\varphi_{x}^{-1}\colon\varphi_{x}(U_{x}\cap U_{y})\overset{\cong}{\longrightarrow}U_{x}\cap U_{y}\overset{\cong}{\longrightarrow}\varphi_{y}(U_{x}\cap U_{y}).

For
z\in\varphi_{x}(U_{x}\cap U_{y})(L)
, we find
c\in L^{\times}
such that

Y^{\prime}\vcentcolon=Y\big(c\,(T_{1}-z_{1}),\ldots,c\,(T_{n_{x}}-z_{n_{x}})\big)\subset Y\vcentcolon=\varphi_{x}(U_{x}\cap U_{y})

is an open affinoid subdomain with

\psi_{z}\colon Y^{\prime}\overset{\cong}{\longrightarrow}{\mathbb{B}}^{n_{x}}\,,\quad w\longmapsto c\,(w-z),

and
Y^{\prime}(L)=B_{{\lvert{c}\rvert}^{-1}}^{n_{x}}(z)
.
Applying
Lemma

1.7.3
to

\varphi_{y}\circ\varphi^{-1}_{x}\circ\psi^{-1}_{z}\colon{\mathbb{B}}^{n_{x}}\longrightarrow Y^{\prime}\longrightarrow\varphi_{x}^{-1}(Y^{\prime})\longrightarrow\varphi_{y}(\varphi_{x}^{-1}(Y^{\prime}))\lhook\joinrel\longrightarrow{\mathbb{B}}^{n_{y}}

we find that
f(w)\vcentcolon=(\varphi_{y}\circ\varphi^{-1}_{x}\circ\psi^{-1}_{z})(w)
is given by convergent power series, for
w\in B_{{\lvert{c}\rvert}^{-1}}^{n_{x}}(z)
.
Therefore
(\varphi_{y}\circ\varphi_{x}^{-1})(w)=f(c\,(w-z))
, for all
z\in Y(L)
and such
w
, shows that
\varphi_{y}\circ\varphi_{x}^{-1}
is locally
L
-analytic on
\varphi_{x}(U_{x}\cap U_{y})(L)
.
This shows that the charts
\varphi_{x}
, for
x\in X(L)
, are compatible, and we obtain a maximal atlas induced by them.

To see that
X(L)
is second countable let
X=\bigcup_{i\in I}X_{i}
be an admissible covering by open affinoid subdomains.
We may assume
I
to be at most countable by the assumption on
X
to be of countable type.
But each affinoid
X_{i}
is the a subspace of some
{\mathbb{B}}^{n_{i}}
with respect to the canonical topology.
Hence
X_{i}(L)\subset{\mathbb{B}}^{n_{i}}(L)\subset L^{n_{i}}
is second countable by
Lemma

1.7.1
.

By the assumption that
X
is separated, the diagonal morphism
\Delta\colon X\rightarrow X\times_{L}X
is a closed immersion.
It follows from
[
7
, 9.3.5 Lemma 3]
that
U\times_{L}U\subset X\times_{L}X
is an open affinoid subdomain, for every open affinoid subdomain
U\subset X
, and from
[
7
, 9.5.3 Prop. 2]
that the morphism
\Delta\colon U\rightarrow U\times_{L}U
is a closed immersion.
Hence
\Delta(U)\subset U\times_{L}U
is closed in the canonical topology.
Therefore we can deduce that on the level of
L
-valued points

\Delta\big(U(L)\big)\subset U(L)\times U(L)=(U\times_{L}U)(L)

is closed when
U(L)
is endowed with the topology generated by all open affinoid subdomains of
U
.
This shows that
X(L)
is Hausdorff.
Finally, since
X(L)
clearly is locally compact in addition to being second countable and Hausdorff, we can conclude that it is paracompact.
∎

Corollary 1.7.7
.

Via assigning

\displaystyle X

\displaystyle\longmapsto X^{\mathrm{la}},

\displaystyle[f\colon X\rightarrow Y]

\displaystyle\longmapsto f{\!\,\mid_{X^{\mathrm{la}}}}

we obtain a functor from the full subcategory of smooth, separated rigid analytic
L
-spaces of countable type to the category of locally
L
-analytic manifolds.

Proof.

Like in the proof of the previous proposition one shows that a morphism between rigid analytic
L
-spaces induces a locally
L
-analytic map between the associated manifolds.
∎

Remarks 1.7.8
.

For a smooth, separated
L
-scheme
X
of finite type, we may pass to the rigid analytification
X^{\mathrm{rig}}
which is of countable type by Remark
1.7.5
(i).
Since being separated and smooth carries over to
X^{\mathrm{rig}}
, we may associate with
X
the locally
L
-manifold
(X^{\mathrm{rig}})^{\mathrm{la}}
.
We denote the resulting functor by
({\,\_\,})^{\mathrm{la}}
.

In particular, if
{\mathrm{\bf G}}
is a smooth algebraic group over
L
, it is necessarily separated.
It follows from functoriality that the algebraic group structure of
{\mathrm{\bf G}}
endows
{\mathrm{\bf G}}^{\mathrm{la}}
with the structure of a locally
L
-analytic Lie group.

We now assume that
L
is a locally compact complete non-archimedean field and let
K
be a finite extension of
L
.

Proposition 1.7.9
.

Let
X
be a smooth, separated rigid analytic
L
-space of countable type,
x\in X(L)
.
Then there is a isomorphism of local
K
-algebras

(1.31)

{\mathcal{O}}_{X,x}{\,\otimes_{L}\,}K\overset{\cong}{\longrightarrow}C^{\mathrm{la}}_{x}(X^{\mathrm{la}},K)\,,\quad f\otimes\lambda\longmapsto\lambda f{\!\,\mid_{X^{\mathrm{la}}}}.

Proof.

Employing
Lemma

1.7.2
, we find an affinoid subdomain
U\subset X
containing
x
and an isomorphism
\varphi\colon U\xrightarrow{\cong}{\mathbb{B}}^{n}
, for some
n\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
, with
\varphi(x)=0
.
For
\varepsilon\in{\lvert{{\mathcal{O}}_{L}\setminus\{0\}}\rvert}
we consider the Weierstraß domain
{\mathbb{B}}^{n}_{\varepsilon}\vcentcolon={\mathbb{B}}^{n}(c\,T_{1},\ldots,c\,T_{n})\subset{\mathbb{B}}^{n}
where
c\in L
such that
{\lvert{c}\rvert}=\varepsilon^{-1}
.
Then the set of affinoid subdomains
U_{\varepsilon}\vcentcolon=\varphi^{-1}({\mathbb{B}}^{n}_{\varepsilon})
is cofinal in the family of affinoid subdomains of
X
containing
x
.
Therefore we find that

{\mathcal{O}}_{X,x}{\,\otimes_{L}\,}K\cong\Big(\varinjlim_{\varepsilon\in{\lvert{{\mathcal{O}}_{L}\setminus\{0\}}\rvert}}{\mathcal{O}}(U_{\varepsilon})\Big){\,\otimes_{L}\,}K\cong\varinjlim_{\varepsilon\in{\lvert{{\mathcal{O}}_{L}\setminus\{0\}}\rvert}}{\mathcal{O}}(U_{\varepsilon}){\,\otimes_{L}\,}K.

On the other hand the induced charts
U_{\varepsilon}(L)\rightarrow B_{\varepsilon}^{n}(0)
form a cofinal subset in the family of analytic charts of
X^{\mathrm{la}}
centred at
x
.
Hence we have by
Lemma

1.6.2
(ii)

C^{\mathrm{la}}_{x}(X^{\mathrm{la}},K)\cong\varinjlim_{\varepsilon\in{\lvert{{\mathcal{O}}_{L}\setminus\{0\}}\rvert}}C^{\mathrm{rig}}(U_{\varepsilon}(L),K).

But there are compatible isomorphisms of
L
-algebras

(1.32)

{\mathcal{O}}(U_{\varepsilon})\longrightarrow C^{\mathrm{rig}}(U_{\varepsilon}(L),L)\,,\quad f\longmapsto f{\!\,\mid_{U_{\varepsilon}(L)}}\,,\quad\text{ for all $\varepsilon\in{\lvert{{\mathcal{O}}_{L}\setminus\{0\}}\rvert}$.}

Indeed, an inverse is given as follows:
For
f\in C^{\mathrm{rig}}(U_{\varepsilon}(L),L)
, let
g\in{\mathcal{A}}_{\varepsilon}(L^{n},L)
be a convergent power series such that
f(z)=g(\varphi(z))
, for all
z\in U_{\varepsilon}(L)
.
As
{\mathcal{A}}_{\varepsilon}(L^{n},L)={\mathcal{O}}({\mathbb{B}}^{n}_{\varepsilon})
, we map
f
to
\varphi^{\flat}(g)
where
\varphi^{\flat}\colon{\mathcal{O}}({\mathbb{B}}^{n}_{\varepsilon})\xrightarrow{\cong}{\mathcal{O}}(U_{\varepsilon})
is the isomorphism of affinoid
L
-algebras corresponding to
\varphi{\!\,\mid_{U_{\varepsilon}}}
.
Passing to the tensor product of (
1.32
) with
K
then yields (cf.
[
27
, §2.3]
)

{\mathcal{O}}(U_{\varepsilon}){\,\otimes_{L}\,}K\cong C^{\mathrm{rig}}(U_{\varepsilon}(L),L){\,\otimes_{L}\,}K\cong C^{\mathrm{rig}}(U_{\varepsilon}(L),K)

Taking the direct limit over these isomorphisms gives (
1.31
).

Furthermore we note that the isomorphisms (
1.32
) preserve the maximal ideals of functions vanishing at
x
so that (
1.31
) is an isomorphism of local
K
-algebras. ∎

Corollary 1.7.10
.

Let
{\mathrm{\bf G}}
be a smooth algebraic group over
L
.
Then the isomorphism (
1.31
) for
{\mathrm{\bf G}}^{\mathrm{rig}}
induces a canonical isomorphism of
K
-Hopf algebras

{\mathrm{hy}}({\mathrm{\bf G}}^{\mathrm{la}},K)\overset{\cong}{\longrightarrow}{\rm Dist}({\mathrm{\bf G}}){\,\otimes_{L}\,}K

where
{\rm Dist}({\mathrm{\bf G}})
denotes the distribution algebra of
{\mathrm{\bf G}}
, cf.
[
41
, I. §7.7]
.
In particular, if
{\rm char}(L)=0
, then
{\mathrm{hy}}({\mathrm{\bf G}}^{\mathrm{la}},K)\cong U({\rm Lie}\,{\mathrm{\bf G}}){\,\otimes_{L}\,}K
.

Proof.

As noted in Remark
1.7.8
(ii), we may apply
Proposition

1.7.9
to obtain an isomorphism of local
K
-algebras

\alpha\colon{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathrm{\bf G}}^{\mathrm{rig}},e}{\,\otimes_{L}\,}K\overset{\cong}{\longrightarrow}C^{\mathrm{la}}_{e}({\mathrm{\bf G}}^{\mathrm{la}},K).

For every
n\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
, we thus have a homomorphism

(1.33)

{\mathrm{hy}}({\mathrm{\bf G}}^{\mathrm{la}},K)_{n}\longrightarrow\left\{\ell\in({\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathrm{\bf G}}^{\mathrm{rig}},e}{\,\otimes_{L}\,}K)^{\ast}\,\middle|\,\ell({\mathfrak{m}}_{e}^{n+1}{\,\otimes_{L}\,}K)=0\right\}\,,\quad\mu\longmapsto\mu\circ\alpha.

Let
{\mathfrak{M}}_{e}={\mathrm{Ker}}({\mathrm{ev}}_{e})\subset C^{\mathrm{la}}_{e}({\mathrm{\bf G}}^{\mathrm{la}},K)
denote the maximal ideal.
By
Proposition

1.6.5
(iii) every
\mu\in C^{\mathrm{la}}_{e}({\mathrm{\bf G}}^{\mathrm{la}},K)^{\ast}
with
\mu({\mathfrak{M}}_{e}^{n+1})=0
factors over a finite-dimensional quotient of
C^{\mathrm{la}}_{e}({\mathrm{\bf G}}^{\mathrm{la}},K)
.
Hence every such
\mu
already is continuous, and it follows that (
1.33
) an isomorphism of
K
-vector spaces.

Moreover, let
\widehat{{\mathcal{O}}}_{{\mathrm{\bf G}},e}
and
\widehat{{\mathcal{O}}}_{{\mathrm{\bf G}}^{\mathrm{rig}},e}
denote the respective
{\mathfrak{m}}_{e}
-adic completions.
As stated in
[
6
, p. 113]
these completions are canonically isomorphic
7
7

7

In
[
6
]
Bosch refers to
[
48
, Satz 2.1]
, but this paper was not available to me.
.
The homomorphisms

\big({\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathrm{\bf G}}^{\mathrm{rig}},e}{\,\otimes_{L}\,}K\big)^{\ast}\longleftarrow\big(\widehat{{\mathcal{O}}}_{{\mathrm{\bf G}}^{\mathrm{rig}},e}{\,\otimes_{L}\,}K\big)^{\ast}\cong\big(\widehat{{\mathcal{O}}}_{{\mathrm{\bf G}},e}{\,\otimes_{L}\,}K\big)^{\ast}\longrightarrow\big({\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathrm{\bf G}},e}{\,\otimes_{L}\,}K\big)^{\ast}

thus restrict to yield an isomorphism

\displaystyle\left\{\ell\in({\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathrm{\bf G}}^{\mathrm{rig}},e}{\,\otimes_{L}\,}K)^{\ast}\,\middle|\,\ell({\mathfrak{m}}_{e}^{n+1}{\,\otimes_{L}\,}K)=0\right\}

\displaystyle\cong\left\{\ell\in({\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathrm{\bf G}},e}{\,\otimes_{L}\,}K)^{\ast}\,\middle|\,\ell({\mathfrak{m}}_{e}^{n+1}{\,\otimes_{L}\,}K)=0\right\}

\displaystyle\cong{\rm Dist}_{n}({\mathrm{\bf G}}){\,\otimes_{L}\,}K.

That the resulting
\beta\colon{\mathrm{hy}}({\mathrm{\bf G}}^{\mathrm{la}},K)\xrightarrow{\cong}{\rm Dist}({\mathrm{\bf G}}){\,\otimes_{L}\,}K
is an isomorphism of
K
-Hopf algebras follows from the commutativity of the following diagram, for
\mu,\nu\in{\mathrm{hy}}({\mathrm{\bf G}}^{\mathrm{la}},K)
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathrm{\bf G}},e}{\,\otimes_{L}\,}K}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\big({\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathrm{\bf G}},e}{\,\otimes_{L}\,}K\big){\,\otimes_{K}\,}\big({\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathrm{\bf G}},e}{\,\otimes_{L}\,}K\big)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathrm{\bf G}}^{\mathrm{rig}},e}{\,\otimes_{L}\,}K}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\big({\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathrm{\bf G}}^{\mathrm{rig}},e}{\,\otimes_{L}\,}K\big){\,\widehat{\otimes}_{K}\,}\big({\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathrm{\bf G}}^{\mathrm{rig}},e}{\,\otimes_{L}\,}K\big)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{\mathrm{la}}_{e}({\mathrm{\bf G}}^{\mathrm{la}},K)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{\mathrm{la}}_{e}({\mathrm{\bf G}}^{\mathrm{la}},K){\,\widehat{\otimes}_{K}\,}C^{\mathrm{la}}_{e}({\mathrm{\bf G}}^{\mathrm{la}},K)}
.
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta(\mu)\otimes\beta(\nu)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha\otimes\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu\otimes\nu}

∎

2.
H^{0}({\mathcal{X}},{\mathcal{E}})^{\prime}_{b}
and Local Cohomology Groups as Locally Analytic Representations

Let
K
be a complete non-archimedean field with non-trivial absolute value
{\lvert{{\,\_\,}}\rvert}
.

2.1.
Topologies on the Coherent and Local Cohomology of Rigid Analytic Spaces

In this section we want to consider a more general situation than the one we later need for the Drinfeld upper half space.
Let
X
be a rigid analytic
K
-space and
{\mathcal{E}}
a coherent
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}
-module.
Following
[
61
, 1.6]
we want to recall how the sections and the coherent cohomology groups of
{\mathcal{E}}
on admissible open subsets can canonically be endowed with locally convex topologies.

Let
U={\mathrm{Sp}}\,A\subset X
be an affinoid subdomain, for some affinoid
K
-algebra
A
.
By Kiehl’s theorem
[
7
, 9.4.3 Thm. 3]
,
{\mathcal{E}}(U)
is a finite
A
-module, and hence carries the structure of a complete normed
A
-module, unique up to equivalence of norms
[
7
, 3.7.3 Prop. 3]
.
By fixing such a norm
{\mathcal{E}}(U)
becomes a
K
-Banach space.
For another affinoid subdomain
U^{\prime}\subset U\subset X
, the induced restriction homomorphism
{\mathcal{E}}(U)\rightarrow{\mathcal{E}}(U^{\prime})
is continuous.

Next, we want to look at an admissible open subset
U\subset X
.
For an admissible covering
U=\bigcup_{i\in I}U_{i}
by affinoid open subdomains
U_{i}\subset X
, the intersections
U_{i}\cap U_{j}
, for
i,j\in I
, are admissible open again, and we can find admissible coverings
U_{i}\cap U_{j}=\bigcup_{k\in I_{ij}}V_{ijk}
by affinoid open subdomains
V_{ijk}\subset X
.
Because the
{\mathcal{E}}(U_{i}\cap U_{j})\rightarrow\prod_{k\in I_{ij}}{\mathcal{E}}(V_{ijk})
are monomorphisms of
K
-vector spaces, we have an exact sequence

(2.1)

0\longrightarrow{\mathcal{E}}(U)\longrightarrow\prod_{i\in I}{\mathcal{E}}(U_{i})\longrightarrow\prod_{i,j\in I}\prod_{k\in I_{ij}}{\mathcal{E}}(V_{ijk})

of
K
-vector spaces.
We would like to use this exact sequence to endow
{\mathcal{E}}(U)
with a locally convex topology.
However in order to do this independently of the admissible coverings, we restrict ourselves to the situation that the involved coverings are at most countable (e.g. when
U
is of countable type, see
Definition

1.7.4
).
In this case the products in (
2.1
) are at most countable, and therefore are
K
-Fréchet spaces.
It follows that
{\mathcal{E}}(U)
with the subspace topology is a
K
-Fréchet space as well.
To see that this topology is independent of the (at most countable) admissible coverings, it suffices to consider the situation of a refinement of such coverings.
It is a classical result that the induced homomorphism of complexes is a quasi-isomorphism algebraically
[
6
, 6.2 Thm. 5]
.
Then one argues analogously to the case of complexes of complex Fréchet spaces in
[
4
, VII. Lemma 1.32]
to deduce that the induced isomorphisms between the cohomology groups are topological isomorphisms even.
Note that the restriction homomorphisms
{\mathcal{E}}(U)\rightarrow{\mathcal{E}}(U^{\prime})
, for admissible open
U^{\prime}\subset U
which allow an at most countable admissible covering, are continuous.

Now consider an admissible open subset
U\subset X
with an at most countable admissible covering
U=\bigcup_{i\in I}U_{i}
by admissible open subsets that each allow an at most countable admissible covering by affinoid subdomains.
Moreover, assume that the covering of
U
is
{\mathcal{E}}
-acyclic, i.e. all higher cohomology groups of
{\mathcal{E}}
on the intersections of the
U_{i}
vanish.
For instance, when
X
is separated, this is fulfilled if the
U_{i}
are affinoid or quasi-Stein
[
61
, 1.6]
.
Then the associated Čech complex

(2.2)

\displaystyle\prod_{i\in I}{\mathcal{E}}(U_{i})\longrightarrow\prod_{i_{0},i_{1}\in I}{\mathcal{E}}(U_{i_{0}}\cap U_{i_{1}})\longrightarrow\ldots

computes
H^{k}(U,{\mathcal{E}})
on the level of
K
-vector spaces
[
6
, 6.2 Thm. 5]
, and its differentials are continuous.
Note that (
2.2
) consists of
K
-Fréchet spaces because all of the above products are countable.
We endow
H^{k}(U,{\mathcal{E}})
with the locally convex topology that is induced from being a subquotient of a term of (
2.2
).

By the open mapping theorem
[
67
, Prop. 8.6]
this topology is Hausdorff if and only if the differentials of (
2.2
) are strict.
In general however, this is not the case.
Like before one shows that the topology on
H^{k}(U,{\mathcal{E}})
does not depend on the choice of the at most countable covering.

Furthermore, we want to consider the local cohomology groups of
{\mathcal{E}}
with respect to the set theoretical complement
Z\vcentcolon=X\setminus U
.
We now suppose that
X
possesses an
{\mathcal{E}}
-acyclic, at most countable, admissible covering
X=\bigcup_{j\in J}V_{j}
by admissible open subsets which each have an at most countable, admissible covering by affinoid open subdomains.
We also suppose that the intersections
U_{i}\cap V_{j}
admit an at most countable, admissible covering by affinoid open subdomains.
For example, this latter assumption is fulfilled if
X
is quasi-separated.
In this setting, we may assume that the covering
U=\bigcup_{i\in I}U_{i}
is a refinement of
U=\bigcup_{j\in J}V_{j}\cap U
.
Hence, we have a continuous homomorphism of the Čech complexes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod\limits_{j\in J}{\mathcal{E}}(V_{j})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod\limits_{j_{0},j_{1}\in J}{\mathcal{E}}(V_{j_{0}}\cap V_{j_{1}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ldots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod\limits_{i\in I}{\mathcal{E}}(U_{i})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod\limits_{i_{0},i_{1}\in I}{\mathcal{E}}(U_{i_{0}}\cap U_{i_{1}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ldots}

which in turn induces continuous homomorphisms
H^{k}(X,{\mathcal{E}})\rightarrow H^{k}(U,{\mathcal{E}})
,
k\geq 0
, by the usual diagram chase.

As
K
-vector spaces the local cohomology groups
H^{k}_{Z}(X,{\mathcal{E}})
are defined via the right derived functors
H^{k}_{Z}(X,{\,\_\,})
of the functor
{\mathrm{Ker}}\big(\Gamma(X,{\,\_\,})\rightarrow\Gamma(U,{\,\_\,})\big)

[
33
, Exp. 2, Def. 2.1]
.
They fit into the long exact cohomology sequence of
K
-vector spaces
[
33
, Exp. 2, Cor. 2.9]
:

(2.3)

\displaystyle\ldots\longrightarrow H^{k-1}_{Z}(X,{\mathcal{E}})\longrightarrow H^{k-1}(X,{\mathcal{E}})\longrightarrow H^{k-1}(U,{\mathcal{E}})\overset{\partial^{k-1}}{\longrightarrow}H^{k}_{Z}(X,{\mathcal{E}})\longrightarrow\ldots.

We endow
H^{k}_{Z}(X,{\mathcal{E}})
with the locally convex final topology with respect to
\partial^{k-1}
, i.e. the finest locally convex topology such that
\partial^{k-1}
is continuous.

Remark 2.1.1
.

It follows from
[
67
, Lemma 5.1 (i)]
that with this choice of topology on
H^{k}_{Z}(X,{\mathcal{E}})
the homomorphism
H^{k}_{Z}(X,{\mathcal{E}})\rightarrow H^{k}(X,{\mathcal{E}})
is continuous as well.
Moreover, then
\partial^{k-1}
even is a strict homomorphism by
Lemma

A.12
.

2.2.
Coherent (Local) Cohomology of Equivariant Vector Bundles

Now consider a rigid analytic group variety
G
over
K
with multiplication morphism
m\colon G\times_{K}G\rightarrow G
, and a rigid analytic
K
-variety
X
with an action
\sigma\colon G\times_{K}X\rightarrow X
by this rigid analytic group variety.
Moreover, let
{\mathcal{E}}
be a
G
-equivariant coherent
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}
-module, i.e. a coherent
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}
-module with an isomorphism

\Phi\colon\sigma^{\ast}{\mathcal{E}}\overset{\cong}{\longrightarrow}{\mathrm{pr}}_{2}^{\ast}{\mathcal{E}}

of
{\mathcal{O}}_{G\times_{K}X}
-modules which satisfies the cocycle condition on
G\times_{K}G\times_{K}X
:

{\mathrm{pr}}_{23}^{\ast}\Phi\circ(\mathrm{id}_{G}\times\sigma)^{\ast}\Phi=(m\times\mathrm{id}_{X})^{\ast}\Phi.

For any
g\in G(K)
,
g\colon{\mathrm{Sp}}\,K\rightarrow G
, by a slight abuse of notation we also let
g
denote the induced automorphism
X\xrightarrow{g\times{\mathrm{id}}_{X}}G\times_{K}X\xrightarrow{\sigma}X
.
We thus obtain an isomorphism

\Phi_{g}\colon g^{\ast}{\mathcal{E}}=(g\times\mathrm{id}_{X})^{\ast}\sigma^{\ast}{\mathcal{E}}\xrightarrow{(g\times\mathrm{id}_{X})^{\ast}\Phi}(g\times\mathrm{id}_{X})^{\ast}{\mathrm{pr}}_{2}^{\ast}{\mathcal{E}}={\mathcal{E}}

of
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}
-modules, for each
g\in G(K)
.

Fix
g\in G(K)
, and let
U\subset X
be an admissible open subset.
In the following, we keep the assumptions from the previous section, i.e. that
U
admits an at most countable admissible,
{\mathcal{E}}
-acyclic covering by open subsets which in turn admit at most countable admissible coverings by affinoid open subdomains.
We get an induced isomorphism of
K
-vector spaces on the cohomology groups

(2.4)

\displaystyle\varphi_{g}\colon H^{k}(U,{\mathcal{E}})\longrightarrow H^{k}(U,g_{\ast}g^{\ast}{\mathcal{E}})\cong H^{k}(g^{-1}(U),g^{\ast}{\mathcal{E}})\xrightarrow{\Phi_{g}(g^{-1}(U))}H^{k}(g^{-1}(U),{\mathcal{E}}).

In particular, we obtain an automorphism of
H^{k}(U,{\mathcal{E}})
if
g
stabilizes
U
.

For an affinoid subdomain
V\subset U
, we denote the continuous homomorphism of affinoid
K
-algebras corresponding to
g\colon g^{-1}(V)\rightarrow V
by
g^{\flat}_{V}\colon{\mathcal{O}}(V)\rightarrow{\mathcal{O}}(g^{-1}(V))
.
Then

\displaystyle\varphi_{g,V}\colon{\mathcal{E}}(V)\longrightarrow g_{\ast}g^{\ast}{\mathcal{E}}(V)=g^{\ast}{\mathcal{E}}\big(g^{-1}(V)\big)\xrightarrow{\Phi_{g}(g^{-1}(V))}{\mathcal{E}}\big(g^{-1}(V)\big)

is
g^{\flat}_{V}
-semilinear, i.e.
\varphi_{g,V}(ae)=g^{\flat}_{V}(a)\varphi_{g,V}(e)
, for all
a\in{\mathcal{O}}(V),e\in{\mathcal{E}}(V)
.
Using that
{\mathcal{E}}(V)
and
{\mathcal{E}}(g^{-1}(V))
are finite
{\mathcal{O}}(V)
- respectively
{\mathcal{O}}(g^{-1}(V))
-modules, this implies that
\varphi_{g,V}
is continuous (cf.
[
7
, 3.7.3 Prop. 2]
where this is shown for linear homomorphisms).
We apply this to all
V=U_{i_{0}}\cap\ldots\cap U_{i_{r}}
, for
i_{0},\ldots,i_{r}\in I
, to obtain a continuous homomorphism of the Čech complexes

(2.5)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod\limits_{i\in I}{\mathcal{E}}(U_{i})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod\limits_{i_{0},i_{1}\in I}{\mathcal{E}}(U_{i_{0}}\cap U_{i_{1}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ldots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod\limits_{i\in I}{\mathcal{E}}\big(g^{-1}(U_{i})\big)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod\limits_{i_{0},i_{1}\in I}{\mathcal{E}}\big(g^{-1}(U_{i_{0}}\cap U_{i_{1}})\big)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ldots.}

In fact (
2.5
) induces the isomorphisms (
2.4
) which therefore are continuous.

Note that more generally, for an admissible open subset
V\subset g^{-1}(U)
satisfying the previous assumptions,
g
induces continuous homomorphisms

H^{k}(U,{\mathcal{E}})\overset{\varphi_{g}}{\longrightarrow}H^{k}(g^{-1}(U),{\mathcal{E}})\longrightarrow H^{k}(V,{\mathcal{E}}).

Turning to the local cohomology groups, we now consider
Z\vcentcolon=X\setminus U
, and a subset
W\subset X
such that
g^{-1}(Z)\subset W
and
X\setminus W
is an admissible open subset satisfying the above assumptions.
The isomorphism
\Phi_{g}\colon g^{\ast}{\mathcal{E}}\xrightarrow{\cong}{\mathcal{E}}
then induces homomorphisms

(2.6)

\varphi_{g}\colon H_{Z}^{k}(X,{\mathcal{E}})\longrightarrow H_{W}^{k}(X,{\mathcal{E}})

of
K
-vector spaces.
These fit into an isomorphism of the long exact sequences of local cohomology

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ldots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{k}_{Z}(X,{\mathcal{E}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{k}(X,{\mathcal{E}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{k}(U,{\mathcal{E}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{k+1}_{Z}(X,{\mathcal{E}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ldots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ldots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{k}_{W}(X,{\mathcal{E}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{k}(X,{\mathcal{E}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{k}(X\setminus W,{\mathcal{E}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{k+1}_{W}(X,{\mathcal{E}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ldots.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{g}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{g}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial^{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{g}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{g}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial^{k}}

Under the suitable assumptions on coverings of
X
, we conclude by
[
67
, Lemma 5.1 (i)]
that the homomorphism
\varphi_{g}\colon H_{Z}^{k}(X,{\mathcal{E}})\rightarrow H_{W}^{k}(X,{\mathcal{E}})
is continuous, too.
Note that this
\varphi_{g}
is a topological isomorphism if
W=g^{-1}(Z)
.

2.3.
Coherent Cohomology of the Drinfeld Upper Half Space

Let
K
be non-archimedean local field with ring of integers
{\mathcal{O}}_{K}
and residue characteristic
p>0
.
We denote the completion of the algebraic closure of
K
by
C
, its ring of integers by
{\mathcal{O}}_{C}
, and write
{\lvert{{\,\_\,}}\rvert}
for the absolute value on
C
.
Moreover, we fix a uniformizer
{\pi}
of
K
so that
{\mathfrak{m}}_{K}=({\pi})
.

For fixed
d\in{\mathbb{N}}
, we now consider the action of the linear algebraic group
{\mathrm{\bf G}}\vcentcolon={\mathrm{GL}}_{d+1,K}
on the projective space
{\mathbb{P}}_{K}^{d}
.
We write
G={\mathrm{GL}}_{d+1}(K)
for the
K
-rational points of
{\mathrm{\bf G}}
, and set
G_{0}\vcentcolon={\mathrm{GL}}_{d+1}({\mathcal{O}}_{K})
which is an open, maximal compact subgroup of
G
.

We will use the convention that

{\mathbb{P}}^{d}_{K}={\mathrm{Proj}}\,\mathrm{Sym}(K^{d+1})^{\ast}
is the projective space of lines in
K^{d+1}
where
\mathrm{Sym}(K^{d+1})^{\ast}=K[X_{0},\ldots,X_{d}]
,
with
X_{0},\ldots,X_{d}
being the dual basis of the standard basis of
K^{d+1}
.

Then the natural left action
\sigma\colon{\mathrm{GL}}_{d+1,K}\times_{K}{\mathbb{P}}_{K}^{d}\rightarrow{\mathbb{P}}_{K}^{d}
is given by

\displaystyle{\mathrm{GL}}_{d+1}(C)\times{\mathbb{P}}^{d}_{K}(C)\longrightarrow{\mathbb{P}}^{d}_{K}(C)\,,\quad(g,z)\longmapsto gz\vcentcolon=[z_{0}{\,:\,}\ldots{\,:\,}z_{d}]\cdot g^{-1},

on
C
-valued points.
Note that the compatible left
G
-action on
K[X_{0},\ldots,X_{d}]
is given by
g\cdot X_{j}=\sum_{i=0}^{d}g_{ij}X_{i}
, for
g=(g_{ij})\in G
, so that
{\mathfrak{m}}_{gz}=g({\mathfrak{m}}_{z})
, where
{\mathfrak{m}}_{z}\subset K[X_{0},\ldots,X_{d}]
denotes the maximal ideal corresponding to
z\in{\mathbb{P}}_{K}^{d}(C)
.
We will continue to let
{\mathrm{GL}}_{d+1,K}
,
{\mathbb{P}}_{K}^{d}
, and
\sigma
denote the respective rigid analytifications and the rigid analytic group action when this causes no confusion.

We now recall the definition of the Drinfeld upper half space and its rigid-analytic structure following Schneider and Stuhler
[
69
, §1]
.
Unless stated otherwise, for
z\in{\mathbb{P}}^{d}_{K}(C)
, we always assume a representative
[z_{0}{\,:\,}\ldots{\,:\,}z_{d}]
of
z
to be
unimodular
, i.e. to satisfy
{\lvert{z_{i}}\rvert}\leq 1
, for all
i=0,\ldots,d
, and
{\lvert{z_{i}}\rvert}=1
, for some
i
.
Then, for any hyperplane
H\subset{\mathbb{P}}^{d}_{K}(C)
, we let
\ell_{H}\in\big({\mathcal{O}}_{C}^{d+1}\big)^{\ast}
be some unimodular linear form (i.e.
\ell_{H}(z)=\sum_{i=0}^{d}\lambda_{i}z_{i}
, for some unimodular
\lambda=(\lambda_{0},\ldots,\lambda_{d})\in{\mathcal{O}}_{C}^{d+1}
) such that

H=\left\{z\in{\mathbb{P}}^{d}_{K}(C)\,\middle|\,\ell_{H}(z)=0\right\}.

This linear form
\ell_{H}
is determined up to a unit in
{\mathcal{O}}_{C}
.

Let
{\mathcal{H}}
denote the set of all
K
-rational hyperplanes (i.e. there exists some
\lambda\in{\mathcal{O}}_{K}^{d+1}
defining
H
) in
{\mathbb{P}}^{d}_{K}(C)
.
As a set, the
Drinfeld upper half-space
of dimension
d
over
K
is defined as

{\mathcal{X}}={\mathbb{P}}^{d}_{K}(C)\Big\backslash\bigcup_{H\in{\mathcal{H}}}H.

To describe its structure as a rigid analytic variety, let

{\mathcal{X}}_{n}\vcentcolon=\left\{z\in{\mathbb{P}}_{K}^{d}(C)\,\middle|\,\forall H\in{\mathcal{H}}:{\lvert{\ell_{H}(z)}\rvert}\geq{\lvert{{\pi}}\rvert}^{n}\right\},

for
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
.
The
{\mathcal{X}}_{n}\subset{\mathbb{P}}_{K}^{d}
are open affinoid subvarieties.
Moreover, via the admissible covering
{\mathcal{X}}=\bigcup_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}{\mathcal{X}}_{n}
the Drinfeld upper half space is an admissible open
K
-analytic subvariety of
{\mathbb{P}}_{K}^{d}
, and in fact a Stein space
[
69
, §1, Prop. 4]
.
Recall that this implies that, for any coherent
{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathcal{X}}
-module
{\mathcal{E}}
, the higher cohomology groups vanish
[
45
, Satz 2.4]
:
H^{j}({\mathcal{X}},{\mathcal{E}})=0
, for
j>0
.

The open affinoid subsets
{\mathcal{X}}_{n}\subset{\mathbb{P}}_{K}^{d}
are stabilized under the action of
G_{0}
.
Indeed, let
g\in G_{0}
and
z=[z_{0}{\,:\,}\ldots{\,:\,}z_{d}]\in{\mathcal{X}}_{n}
.
Note that the representative
[z_{0}{\,:\,}\ldots{\,:\,}z_{d}]\cdot g^{-1}
of
gz
already is unimodular.
For any
H\in{\mathcal{H}}
, with
\ell_{H}
corresponding to unimodular
\lambda\in{\mathcal{O}}_{C}^{d+1}
, we have to check that
{\lvert{\ell_{H}(gz)}\rvert}\geq{\lvert{{\pi}}\rvert}^{n}
.
But we have

{\lvert{\ell_{H}(gz)}\rvert}={\lvert{[z_{0}{\,:\,}\ldots{\,:\,}z_{d}]\cdot g^{-1}\cdot\lambda^{t}}\rvert}={\lvert{\ell_{g^{-1}(H)}(z)}\rvert}\geq{\lvert{{\pi}}\rvert}^{n},

and
g^{-1}(H)
is the hyperplane corresponding to
g^{-1}\cdot\lambda^{t}
.

Now consider the admissible open rigid analytic subgroups

(2.7)

H_{n+1}\vcentcolon=\left\{g\in{\mathrm{GL}}_{d+1}({\mathcal{O}}_{C})\,\middle|\,\exists h\in G_{0},h^{\prime}\in\mathrm{M}_{d+1}({\mathcal{O}}_{C}):g=h+{\pi}^{n+1}h^{\prime}\right\}\subset{\mathrm{GL}}_{d+1}(C),

for
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
.
Then the action
\sigma
of
{\mathrm{GL}}_{d+1,K}
on
{\mathbb{P}}_{K}^{d}
restricts to an action of
H_{n+1}
on
{\mathcal{X}}_{n}
:
Let
z\in{\mathcal{X}}_{n}
and
g\in H_{n+1}
with
g=h+{\pi}^{n+1}h^{\prime}
, for
h\in G_{0}
,
h^{\prime}\in\mathrm{M}_{d+1}({\mathcal{O}}_{C})
.
We compute, for
H\in{\mathcal{H}}
,

\displaystyle{\lvert{\ell_{H}(g^{-1}z)}\rvert}={\lvert{\ell_{h(H)}(z)+{\pi}^{n+1}\ell_{H}([z_{0}{\,:\,}\ldots{\,:\,}z_{d}]\cdot h^{\prime})}\rvert}={\lvert{\ell_{h(H)}(z)}\rvert}\geq{\lvert{{\pi}}\rvert}^{n}

since
{\lvert{\ell_{h(H)}(z)}\rvert}>{\lvert{{\pi}^{n+1}\ell_{H}([z_{0}{\,:\,}\ldots{\,:\,}z_{d}]\cdot h^{\prime})}\rvert}
.

Consider now a
{\mathrm{\bf G}}
-equivariant vector bundle
{\mathcal{E}}
on
{\mathbb{P}}_{K}^{d}
.
We want to show how the strong dual
H^{0}({\mathcal{X}},{\mathcal{E}})^{\prime}_{b}
of the global sections of
{\mathcal{E}}
on the Drinfeld upper half space
{\mathcal{X}}
is a locally analytic
G
-representation.
The methods are analogous to the ones used by Schneider and Teitelbaum
[
70
]
for the canonical line bundle
{\mathcal{E}}=\Omega^{d}_{{\mathbb{P}}_{K}^{d}}
.
But for the convenience of the reader we include the proofs.

Proposition 2.3.1

(cf.
[
70
, Cor. 3.9]
)
.

The representation

(2.8)

\displaystyle G\times H^{0}({\mathcal{X}},{\mathcal{E}})^{\prime}_{b}\longrightarrow H^{0}({\mathcal{X}},{\mathcal{E}})^{\prime}_{b}\,,\quad(g,\ell)\longmapsto\ell(g^{-1}.{\,\_\,}),

is locally analytic.
Moreover, the canonical map

(2.9)

\displaystyle\varinjlim_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}H^{0}({\mathcal{X}}_{n},{\mathcal{E}})^{\prime}\longrightarrow\bigg(\varprojlim_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}H^{0}({\mathcal{X}}_{n},{\mathcal{E}})\bigg)_{b}^{\prime}=H^{0}({\mathcal{X}},{\mathcal{E}})_{b}^{\prime}

is a topological isomorphism and
H^{0}({\mathcal{X}},{\mathcal{E}})^{\prime}_{b}
is of compact type this way, i.e. it is the inductive limit of

(2.10)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{0}({\mathcal{X}}_{1},{\mathcal{E}})^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ldots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{0}({\mathcal{X}}_{n},{\mathcal{E}})^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{0}({\mathcal{X}}_{n+1},{\mathcal{E}})^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ldots}

where the transition maps induced from
{\mathcal{X}}_{n}\subset{\mathcal{X}}_{n+1}
are compact and injective.

For this proposition we proceed in several steps.

Lemma 2.3.2
.

The natural actions of
G
on
H^{0}({\mathcal{X}},{\mathcal{E}})
and of
H_{n+1}(K)
on
H^{0}({\mathcal{X}}_{n},{\mathcal{E}})
, for all
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, are given by continuous endomorphisms.

Proof.

This follows from the considerations in the previous section
Section

2.2
.
∎

Lemma 2.3.3
.

The space of global sections
H^{0}({\mathcal{X}},{\mathcal{E}})
is the projective limit of the
K
-Banach spaces

H^{0}({\mathcal{X}},{\mathcal{E}})\cong\varprojlim_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}H^{0}({\mathcal{X}}_{n},{\mathcal{E}})

with respect to the restriction maps
{\mathcal{E}}({\mathcal{X}}_{n+1})\rightarrow{\mathcal{E}}({\mathcal{X}}_{n})
.
These homomorphisms are compact and have dense image.
Moreover, the above isomorphism is
G_{0}
-equivariant.

Proof.

We apply the discussion of
Section

2.1
to the admissible covering
{\mathcal{X}}=\bigcup_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}{\mathcal{X}}_{n}
.
Noting that
{\mathcal{X}}_{n}\subset{\mathcal{X}}_{n+1}
, the topological isomorphism

\displaystyle H^{0}({\mathcal{X}},{\mathcal{E}})={\mathrm{Ker}}\bigg(\prod_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}{\mathcal{E}}({\mathcal{X}}_{n})\rightarrow\prod_{l,m\in{\mathbb{N}}}{\mathcal{E}}({\mathcal{X}}_{l}\cap{\mathcal{X}}_{m})\bigg)\cong\varprojlim_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}H^{0}({\mathcal{X}}_{n},{\mathcal{E}})

follows from (
2.2
).
This isomorphism is
G_{0}
-equivariant by construction.

We now argue analogously to
[
70
, §1 Prop. 4]
.
For each
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
{\mathcal{X}}_{n}
is a Weierstraß domain inside
{\mathcal{X}}_{n+1}

[
69
, §1, Proof of Prop. 4]
.
This implies that the image of
{\mathcal{O}}({\mathcal{X}}_{n+1})
is dense inside
{\mathcal{O}}({\mathcal{X}}_{n})

[
7
, 7.3.4 Prop. 2]
.

Furthermore, by
[
69
, §1, Proof of Prop. 4]
the homomorphism
\psi\colon{\mathcal{O}}({\mathcal{X}}_{n+1})\rightarrow{\mathcal{O}}({\mathcal{X}}_{n})
is inner in the sense of
[
5
, Def. 2.5.1]
, i.e. there exists a strict epimorphism

\tau\colon K\langle T_{1},\ldots,T_{m}\rangle\relbar\joinrel\twoheadrightarrow{\mathcal{O}}({\mathcal{X}}_{n+1})

of affinoid
K
-algebras, for some
m\in{\mathbb{N}}
, such that

\sup_{y\in{\mathcal{X}}_{n}}{\lvert{\psi(\tau(T_{i}))(y)}\rvert}<1,

for all
i=1,\ldots,m
.
By
[
70
, §1 Lemma 5]
it follows that
\psi
is compact as a homomorphism of locally convex
K
-vector spaces.

For a general vector bundle
{\mathcal{E}}
, we know by Kiehl’s theorem
[
7
, 9.4.3 Thm. 3]
that, for some
k\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
, there is a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{O}}({\mathcal{X}}_{n+1})^{\oplus k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{O}}({\mathcal{X}}_{n})^{\oplus k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{E}}({\mathcal{X}}_{n+1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{E}}({\mathcal{X}}_{n})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi^{\oplus k}}

where the vertical maps are strict epimorphisms.
Then
Lemma

A.3
(ii) and (iii) imply that
{\mathcal{E}}({\mathcal{X}}_{n+1})\rightarrow{\mathcal{E}}({\mathcal{X}}_{n})
is compact.
The above commutative diagram also shows that the image of this restriction homomorphism is dense.
∎

Lemma 2.3.4
.

For
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, the representation

G_{0}\times H^{0}({\mathcal{X}}_{n},{\mathcal{E}})\longrightarrow H^{0}({\mathcal{X}}_{n},{\mathcal{E}})\,,\quad(g,v)\longmapsto g.v,

on the
K
-Banach space
H^{0}({\mathcal{X}}_{n},{\mathcal{E}})
is locally analytic.

Proof.

We have already seen in
Lemma

2.3.2
that each
g\in G_{0}\subset H_{n+1}(K)
acts by a continuous automorphism on
H^{0}({\mathcal{X}}_{n},{\mathcal{E}})
.
Hence, it suffices to show that, for every
v\in H^{0}({\mathcal{X}}_{n},{\mathcal{E}})
, the orbit maps
G_{0}\rightarrow H^{0}({\mathcal{X}}_{n},{\mathcal{E}})
,
g\,\to\,g.v
, are locally analytic.

To this end we fix
v\in{\mathcal{E}}({\mathcal{X}}_{n})
and
g\in G_{0}
, and proceed just as in
[
70
, Prop. 2.1’]
.
We use the admissible open rigid analytic subgroup (
2.7
) and the rigid analytic chart

In terms of
K
-affinoid algebras the morphism
h\times{\mathrm{id}}\colon{\mathcal{X}}_{n}\rightarrow D_{n+1}\times_{K}{\mathcal{X}}_{n}
is given by the evaluation of power series

\displaystyle\mathrm{ev}_{h}\colon{\mathcal{O}}(D_{n+1}){\,\widehat{\otimes}_{K}\,}{\mathcal{O}}({\mathcal{X}}_{n})\cong{\mathcal{O}}({\mathcal{X}}_{n})\langle T_{1},\ldots,T_{(d+1)^{2}}\rangle

\displaystyle\longrightarrow{\mathcal{O}}({\mathcal{X}}_{n}),

\displaystyle F

\displaystyle\longmapsto F(h).

Hence we arrive at the commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{E}}({\mathcal{X}}_{n})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\iota_{g}\times{\mathrm{id}})^{\ast}\sigma^{\ast}{\mathcal{E}}(D_{n+1}\times_{K}{\mathcal{X}}_{n})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{E}}({\mathcal{X}}_{n})\langle T_{1},\ldots,T_{(d+1)^{2}}\rangle}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(gh)^{\ast}{\mathcal{E}}({\mathcal{X}}_{n})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{E}}({\mathcal{X}}_{n})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(h\times{\mathrm{id}})^{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{ev}_{h}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi_{gh}({\mathcal{X}}_{n})}

which shows that
gh.v=F_{v}(h)
.
We conclude that the orbit map
G_{0}\rightarrow{\mathcal{E}}({\mathcal{X}}_{n})
is analytic on the open neighbourhood
\iota_{g}(D_{n+1})(K)
of
g
.
∎

Corollary 2.3.5
.

The contragredient representation

G_{0}\times H^{0}({\mathcal{X}}_{n},{\mathcal{E}})^{\prime}\longrightarrow H^{0}({\mathcal{X}}_{n},{\mathcal{E}})^{\prime}\,,\quad(g,\ell)\longmapsto\ell(g^{-1}.{\,\_\,}),

on the
K
-Banach space
H^{0}({\mathcal{X}}_{n},{\mathcal{E}})^{\prime}
is locally analytic.

Proof.

We argue analogous to the proof of
[
70
, Prop 3.8]
.
By
Proposition

1.3.10
the representation of
G_{0}
on
H^{0}({\mathcal{X}}_{n},{\mathcal{E}})
from
Lemma

2.3.4
is given by a locally analytic homomorphism of locally
K
-analytic Lie groups
\rho\colon G_{0}\rightarrow{\mathrm{GL}}(H^{0}({\mathcal{X}}_{n},{\mathcal{E}}))
.
Then the contragredient representation

\rho^{\ast}\colon G_{0}\longrightarrow{\mathrm{GL}}\big(H^{0}({\mathcal{X}}_{n},{\mathcal{E}})^{\prime}\big)\,,\quad g\longmapsto\rho(g^{-1})^{t},

is given by a locally analytic homomorphism of Lie groups as well
[
10
, III. §3.11 Cor. 2]
, and is locally analytic therefore.
∎

Proof of
Proposition

2.3.1
.

For the statements about
H^{0}({\mathcal{X}},{\mathcal{E}})^{\prime}_{b}
, we argue analogously to the proof of
[
70
, Prop. 1.4]
.
By
Lemma

2.3.3
, the homomorphisms
H^{0}({\mathcal{X}}_{n+1},{\mathcal{E}})\rightarrow H^{0}({\mathcal{X}}_{n},{\mathcal{E}})
have dense image.
Therefore, the image of the projections
H^{0}({\mathcal{X}},{\mathcal{E}})\rightarrow H^{0}({\mathcal{X}}_{n},{\mathcal{E}})
is dense, too
[
8
, Ch. II. §3.5 Thm. 1]
.
We apply
[
67
, Prop. 16.5]
to conclude that (
2.9
) is a topological isomorphism.

As
H^{0}({\mathcal{X}}_{n+1},{\mathcal{E}})\rightarrow H^{0}({\mathcal{X}}_{n},{\mathcal{E}})
has dense image, the transpose
H^{0}({\mathcal{X}}_{n},{\mathcal{E}})^{\prime}\rightarrow H^{0}({\mathcal{X}}_{n+1},{\mathcal{E}})^{\prime}
is injective.
Moreover,
[
67
, Lemma 16.4]
implies that they are compact.

Concerning the
G
-action (
2.8
), we have seen in
Lemma

2.3.2
that
G
acts by continuous endomorphisms on
H^{0}({\mathcal{X}},{\mathcal{E}})
.
Therefore the contragredient
G
-action on
H^{0}({\mathcal{X}},{\mathcal{E}})^{\prime}_{b}
is by continuous endomorphisms as well.

In view of
Proposition

1.3.9
it suffices to show that the orbit maps of (
2.8
) restricted to
G_{0}
are locally analytic.
For any
\ell\in H^{0}({\mathcal{X}},{\mathcal{E}})^{\prime}_{b}
, there exists some
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
such that
\ell\in H^{0}({\mathcal{X}}_{n},{\mathcal{E}})^{\prime}
, and the inclusion
H^{0}({\mathcal{X}}_{n},{\mathcal{E}})^{\prime}\hookrightarrow H^{0}({\mathcal{X}},{\mathcal{E}})^{\prime}_{b}
is
G_{0}
-equivariant by
Lemma

2.3.3
.

But we have seen in
Corollary

2.3.5
that the orbit map
G_{0}\rightarrow H^{0}({\mathcal{X}}_{n},{\mathcal{E}})^{\prime}
of
\ell
is locally analytic.
As
H^{0}({\mathcal{X}},{\mathcal{E}})^{\prime}_{b}
is of compact type with respect to (
2.10
),
H^{0}({\mathcal{X}}_{n},{\mathcal{E}})^{\prime}\hookrightarrow H^{0}({\mathcal{X}},{\mathcal{E}})_{b}^{\prime}
constitutes a BH-subspace.
Then by definition the orbit map
G_{0}\rightarrow H^{0}({\mathcal{X}},{\mathcal{E}})^{\prime}_{b}
of
\ell
is locally analytic as well.
∎

2.4.
Strictness of certain Čech Complexes for the Complement of Schubert Varieties

Our next aim is to see how the strong duals of the local cohomology groups for
{\mathcal{E}}
with respect to Schubert varieties
{\mathbb{P}}_{K}^{j}\subset({\mathbb{P}}_{K}^{d})^{\mathrm{rig}}
become locally analytic representations.
Moreover, we will show that these strong dual spaces are of compact type similarly to (
2.10
) by giving an exhaustion by the local cohomology groups with respect to “tubes” around the Schubert varieties.

However, the first step in this section is to prove that certain Čech complexes which compute the cohomology of the complement of these Schubert varieties consist of strict continuous homomorphisms.
The strictness property enables us to pass to the local cohomology with respect to the “tubes” around the Schubert varieties in a well-behaved way.

First we define certain rigid analytic subvarieties of
{\mathbb{P}}_{K}^{d}
which are neighbourhoods of the Schubert varieties

{\mathbb{P}}^{r}_{K}=\left\{[z_{0}{\,:\,}\ldots{\,:\,}z_{r}{\,:\,}0{\,:\,}\ldots{\,:\,}0]\in{\mathbb{P}}_{K}^{d}\right\}\vcentcolon=V_{+}(X_{{r}+1},\ldots,X_{d})\subset{\mathbb{P}}_{K}^{d},

for fixed
{r}\in\{0,\ldots,d-1\}
.
For
0<\varepsilon<1
,
\varepsilon\in{\lvert{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{K}}\rvert}
, the “open”
\varepsilon
-neighbourhood around
{\mathbb{P}}_{K}^{r}
is defined as

(2.11)

{\mathbb{P}}^{r}_{K}(\varepsilon)\vcentcolon=\left\{[z_{0}{\,:\,}\ldots{\,:\,}z_{d}]\in{\mathbb{P}}^{d}_{K}\,\middle|\,\forall i={r}+1,\ldots,d:{\lvert{z_{i}}\rvert}\leq\varepsilon\right\},

and the “closed” one as

{\mathbb{P}}^{r}_{K}(\varepsilon)^{-}\vcentcolon=\left\{[z_{0}{\,:\,}\ldots{\,:\,}z_{d}]\in{\mathbb{P}}^{d}_{K}\,\middle|\,\forall i={r}+1,\ldots,d:{\lvert{z_{i}}\rvert}<\varepsilon\right\}.

We will describe some admissible coverings of the complements of these
\varepsilon
-neighbourhoods.
Let
\lambda\in K^{\times}
and
m\in{\mathbb{N}}
such that
\varepsilon=\sqrt[m]{{\lvert{\lambda}\rvert}}
.
We have the admissible covering

(2.12)

{\mathbb{P}}^{d}_{K}\setminus{\mathbb{P}}^{r}_{K}(\varepsilon)^{-}=\bigcup_{i={r}+1}^{d}U_{i,\varepsilon}

by the Weierstraß domains

\displaystyle U_{i,\varepsilon}=D_{+}(X_{i})_{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\left\{[z_{0}{\,:\,}\ldots{\,:\,}z_{d}]\in{\mathbb{P}}^{d}_{K}\,\middle|\,\forall j=0,\ldots,d:\varepsilon\,{\lvert{z_{j}}\rvert}\leq{\lvert{z_{i}}\rvert}\right\}

\displaystyle\phantom{\vcentcolon}=\left\{[z_{0}{\,:\,}\ldots{\,:\,}z_{d}]\in{\mathbb{P}}^{d}_{K}\,\middle|\,\forall j=0,\ldots,d:\left\lvert\lambda\frac{z_{j}^{m}}{z_{i}^{m}}\right\rvert\leq 1\right\},

cf.
[
7
, 6.1.5 Thm. 4]
.
We will denote this covering (
2.12
) by
{\mathcal{U}}_{\varepsilon}
in the following.
Note that
U_{i,\varepsilon}
is contained in the standard open affine subset
D_{+}(X_{i})
of the projective space.

Moreover, for
0\leq\varepsilon<1
,
\varepsilon\in{\lvert{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{K}}\rvert}
, let

U_{i,\varepsilon}^{-}=D_{+}(X_{i})_{\varepsilon}^{-}\vcentcolon=\left\{[z_{0}{\,:\,}\ldots{\,:\,}z_{d}]\in{\mathbb{P}}^{d}_{K}\,\middle|\,\forall j=0,\ldots,d:\varepsilon\,{\lvert{z_{j}}\rvert}<{\lvert{z_{i}}\rvert}\right\}.

Then
U_{i,\varepsilon}^{-}
becomes an open admissible subdomain of
{\mathbb{P}}^{d}_{K}
which is quasi-Stein via the admissible covering

U_{i,\varepsilon}^{-}=\bigcup_{{\lvert{{\mathcal{O}}_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{K}}}\rvert}\ni\varepsilon_{m}\searrow\varepsilon}U_{i,\varepsilon_{m}},

for any strictly decreasing sequence of
(\varepsilon_{m})_{m\in{\mathbb{N}}}\subset{\lvert{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{K}^{\times}}\rvert}
, with
\varepsilon_{m}>\varepsilon
,
\varepsilon_{m}\rightarrow\varepsilon
.
The condition on the associated homomorphisms of affinoid algebras to have dense image is fulfilled for every inclusion of Weierstraß domains
[
7
, 7.3.4 Prop. 2]
.
In the extreme case of
\varepsilon=0
, we have
U_{i,0}^{-}=U_{i}^{\mathrm{rig}}
, cf.
[
7
, 9.3.4 Example 2]
.
Here
U_{i}^{\mathrm{rig}}
denotes the rigid analytification of the open affine subscheme
U_{i}\vcentcolon=D_{+}(X_{i})\subset{\mathbb{P}}_{K}^{d}
.

We let
{\mathcal{U}}_{\varepsilon}^{-}
denote the admissible covering

(2.13)

{\mathbb{P}}^{d}_{K}\setminus{\mathbb{P}}^{r}_{K}(\varepsilon)=\bigcup_{i={r}+1}^{d}U_{i,\varepsilon}^{-}.

In the extreme case
\varepsilon=0
, we also write
({\mathbb{P}}^{r}_{K})^{\mathrm{rig}}\subset{\mathbb{P}}_{K}^{d}
when we want to signify that the complement of
{\mathbb{P}}_{K}^{r}\subset({\mathbb{P}}_{K}^{d})^{\mathrm{rig}}
is an admissible open subset

{\mathbb{P}}^{d}_{K}\setminus\big({\mathbb{P}}^{r}_{K}\big)^{\mathrm{rig}}=\bigcup_{i={r}+1}^{d}U_{i}^{\mathrm{rig}}=\bigcup_{i={r}+1}^{d}\bigcup_{{\lvert{{\mathcal{O}}_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{K}}}\rvert}\ni\varepsilon_{m}\searrow 0}U_{i,\varepsilon_{m}}.

Moreover, we let
{\mathcal{U}}
denote the standard covering by open affine subschemes

(2.14)

{\mathbb{P}}_{K}^{d}\setminus{\mathbb{P}}_{K}^{r}=\bigcup_{i={r}+1}^{d}U_{i}.

Finally, we write, for any subset
I\subset\{0,\ldots,d\}
,

(2.15)

U_{I,\varepsilon}\vcentcolon=\bigcap_{i\in I}U_{i,\varepsilon},

and similarly
U_{I,\varepsilon}^{-}
,
U_{I}
, and
U_{I}^{\mathrm{rig}}
.
By convention we set
U_{\emptyset}=U_{\emptyset,\varepsilon}=U_{\emptyset,\varepsilon}^{-}\vcentcolon={\mathbb{P}}_{K}^{d}
.

Lemma 2.4.1
.

Let
0<\varepsilon<1
with
\varepsilon\in{\lvert{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{K}}\rvert}
.
Let
I\subset\{0,\ldots,d\}
and denote by
{\mathcal{E}}^{\mathrm{alg}}(U_{I})
the sections of
{\mathcal{E}}
on the algebraic variety
U_{I}
.
Then the homomorphism
{\mathcal{E}}^{\mathrm{alg}}(U_{I})\rightarrow{\mathcal{E}}(U_{I,\varepsilon})
which is induced from
U_{I,\varepsilon}
being an admissible open subdomain of
U_{I}^{\mathrm{rig}}
is injective and its image is dense.
In particular,
{\mathcal{E}}(U_{I,\varepsilon})
is the completion of
{\mathcal{E}}^{\mathrm{alg}}(U_{I})
when the latter is considered with the topology coming from the Banach topology of
{\mathcal{E}}(U_{I,\varepsilon})
.

Proof.

We may assume that
I=\{i_{0},\ldots,i_{m}\}\neq\emptyset
.
Then the isomorphism

\displaystyle U_{i_{0}}=D_{+}(X_{i_{0}})

\displaystyle\overset{\cong}{\longrightarrow}{\mathrm{Spec}}\,K\big[T_{(j,i_{0})}\mid j=0,\ldots,d,j\neq i_{0}\big],

\displaystyle\frac{X_{j}}{X_{i_{0}}}

\displaystyle\longmapsfrom T_{(j,i_{0})},

of schemes induces an isomorphism
U_{I}\cong{\mathrm{Spec}}(A/{\mathfrak{a}})
where

\displaystyle A

\displaystyle=K\big[T_{(j,i_{k})}\,\big|\,j=0,\ldots,d,k=0,\ldots,m\text{, with }j\neq i_{k}\big],

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{a}}

\displaystyle=\big(T_{(i_{j},i_{k})}T_{(i_{k},i_{j})}-1\,\big|\,j,k=0,\ldots,m\text{, with }j\neq k\big).

Recall that
U_{I}^{\mathrm{rig}}
is defined via the admissible covering
[
7
, 9.3.4 Example 2]

U_{I}^{\mathrm{rig}}=\bigcup_{n\geq 0}U_{I,{\lvert{{\pi}}\rvert}^{n}},

and we have isomorphisms
U_{I,{\lvert{{\pi}}\rvert}^{n}}\cong{\mathrm{Sp}}(A_{n}/{\mathfrak{a}}A_{n})
, for

\displaystyle A_{n}=K\big\langle{\pi}^{n}T_{(j,i_{k})}\,\big|\,j=0,\ldots,d,k=0,\ldots,m\text{, with }j\neq i_{k}\big\rangle.

We see that
A/{\mathfrak{a}}\hookrightarrow A_{n}/{\mathfrak{a}}A_{n}
has dense image as
A
is dense in
A_{n}
.
Furthermore, the Weierstraß subdomain
U_{I,\varepsilon}\subset U_{I}^{\mathrm{rig}}
is contained in some
U_{I,{\lvert{{\pi}}\rvert}^{n}}
so that
A/{\mathfrak{a}}\hookrightarrow{\mathcal{O}}(U_{I,\varepsilon})
is dense as well
[
7
, 7.3.4 Prop. 2]
.
This settles the case of the structure sheaf
{\mathcal{E}}={\mathcal{O}}
.

For a general
{\mathrm{\bf G}}
-equivariant vector bundle
{\mathcal{E}}
on
{\mathbb{P}}_{K}^{d}
by
Lemma

2.4.4
, we find a trivialization
{\mathcal{E}}^{\mathrm{alg}}{\!\,\mid_{U_{i_{0}}}}\cong({\mathcal{O}}^{\mathrm{alg}})^{\oplus n}{\!\,\mid_{U_{i_{0}}}}
, for
n\vcentcolon={\rm rk}({\mathcal{E}})
.
Therefore the claim follows from the compatible isomorphisms
{\mathcal{E}}^{\mathrm{alg}}(U_{I})\cong{\mathcal{O}}^{\mathrm{alg}}(U_{I})^{\oplus n}
and
{\mathcal{E}}(U_{I,\varepsilon})\cong{\mathcal{O}}(U_{I,\varepsilon})^{\oplus n}
. ∎

Remark 2.4.2
.

One can give a concrete description of the Gauss norm
{\lvert{{\,\_\,}}\rvert}_{\varepsilon}
on the affinoid algebra
{\mathcal{O}}(U_{I,\varepsilon})
(and in turn on
{\mathcal{O}}^{\mathrm{alg}}(U_{I})
), cf.
[
56
, Proof of Lemma 1.3.1]
:

(2.16)

\bigg\lvert\sum_{{\underline{k}}\in\Lambda_{I}}a_{{\underline{k}}}\,X_{0}^{k_{0}}\cdots X_{d}^{k_{d}}\bigg\rvert_{\varepsilon}=\sup_{{\underline{k}}\in\Lambda_{I}}{\lvert{a_{{\underline{k}}}}\rvert}\bigg(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\bigg)^{{\lvert{\max(0,{\underline{k}})}\rvert}}.

Here we use the notation

\displaystyle\Lambda_{I}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\bigg\{{\underline{k}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{d+1}\bigg|\sum_{i=0}^{d}k_{i}=0\text{ , and }\forall i\in\{0,\ldots,d\}\setminus I:k_{i}\geq 0\bigg\},

and

\displaystyle\max(0,{\underline{k}})
