{\mathfrak{g}}={\rm Lie}({\mathrm{\bf G}})
,
{\mathfrak{p}}={\rm Lie}({\mathrm{\bf P}})
, etc. denote the corresponding Lie algebras.
We consider the following subcategories of modules for the universal enveloping algebra
U({\mathfrak{g}})
.

Definition 3.4.1

(
[
58
, §2.5]
)
.

Let
{\mathcal{O}}^{\mathfrak{p}}
be the full subcategory of
U({\mathfrak{g}})
-modules
M
satisfying

M
is finitely generated as a
U({\mathfrak{g}})
-module,

viewed as an
{\mathfrak{l}}_{\mathrm{\bf P}}
-module,
M
is the direct sum of finite-dimensional simple modules,

the action of
{\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{\bf P}}
on
M
is locally finite, i.e. for every
m\in M
, the
K
-vector subspace
U({\mathfrak{u}}_{\mathrm{\bf P}})m\subset M
is finite-dimensional.

Let
{\rm Irr}({\mathfrak{l}}_{\mathrm{\bf P}})^{\rm fd}
be the set of isomorphism classes of finite-dimensional irreducible
{\mathfrak{l}}_{\mathrm{\bf P}}
-representations.
We define
{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathrm{alg}}^{\mathfrak{p}}
to be the full subcategory of
{\mathcal{O}}^{\mathfrak{p}}
of
U({\mathfrak{g}})
-modules
M
such that for a decomposition

M=\bigoplus_{{\mathfrak{a}}\in{\rm Irr}({\mathfrak{l}}_{\mathrm{\bf P}})^{\rm fd}}M_{\mathfrak{a}}

into the
{\mathfrak{a}}
-isotypic components as in (2), we have: If
M_{\mathfrak{a}}\neq(0)
then
{\mathfrak{a}}
is the Lie algebra representation induced by a finite-dimensional algebraic
{\mathrm{\bf L}}_{\mathrm{\bf P}}
-representation.

Note that for
{\mathfrak{p}}={\mathfrak{b}}
,
{\mathcal{O}}\vcentcolon={\mathcal{O}}^{\mathfrak{b}}
is the adaptation of the classical category
{\mathcal{O}}
introduced by Bernstein, Gelfand and Gelfand for semi-simple Lie algebras over the complex numbers.

The functor
{\mathcal{F}}_{P}^{G}
from
[
58
, §3,4]
is now defined as follows:
Let
M\in{\mathcal{O}}^{\mathfrak{p}}_{\mathrm{alg}}
and let
V
be a smooth admissible representation of the Levi subgroup
L_{\mathrm{\bf P}}\subset P
on a
K
-vector space.
We regard
V
as a smooth
P
-representation via inflation and endow it with the finest locally convex topology so that it becomes a locally analytic
P
-representation of compact type, see
[
75
, §2]
.
By the conditions on
M\in{\mathcal{O}}^{\mathfrak{p}}
, there exists a finite-dimensional
U({\mathfrak{p}})
-submodule
W\subset M
which generates
M
as a
U({\mathfrak{g}})
-module, i.e. there is a short exact sequence of
U({\mathfrak{g}})
-modules

0\longrightarrow{\mathfrak{d}}\longrightarrow U({\mathfrak{g}}){\,\otimes_{U({\mathfrak{p}})}\,}W\longrightarrow M\longrightarrow 0.

Then the
{\mathfrak{p}}
-representation
W
uniquely lifts to the structure of an algebraic
{\mathrm{\bf P}}
-representation on
W

[
58
, Lemma 3.2]
.
There is a pairing (cf.
[
58
, (3.2.2)]
)

\displaystyle\langle{\,\_\,},\!{\,\_\,}\rangle_{C^{\mathrm{la}}(G,V)}\colon D(G){\,\otimes_{D(P)}\,}W\times{\mathrm{Ind}}^{G}_{P}(W^{\prime}{\,\otimes_{K}\,}V)

\displaystyle\longrightarrow C^{\mathrm{la}}(G,V),

\displaystyle(\delta\otimes w,f)

\displaystyle\longmapsto\big[g\mapsto\big(\delta\ast_{r}({\mathrm{ev}}_{w}\circ f)\big)(g)\big].

Here we use the identification
W^{\prime}{\,\otimes_{K}\,}V\cong{\mathcal{L}}_{b}(W,V)
from
[
67
, Cor. 18.8]
and denote the evaluation homomorphism by
{\mathrm{ev}}_{w}\colon{\mathcal{L}}_{b}(W,V)\rightarrow V
,
h\mapsto h(w)
.
Moreover, “
\ast_{r}
” signifies the
D(G)
-module action on
C^{\mathrm{la}}(G,V)
induced from the right regular action of
G
(see
Example

1.3.7
(ii)).
Via the injective map

U({\mathfrak{g}}){\,\otimes_{U({\mathfrak{p}})}\,}W\lhook\joinrel\longrightarrow D(G){\,\otimes_{D(P)}\,}W

one may consider the subspace of
{\mathrm{Ind}}^{G}_{P}(W^{\prime}{\,\otimes_{K}\,}V)
annihilated by
{\mathfrak{d}}
and define
[
58
, (4.4.1)]

{\mathcal{F}}^{G}_{P}(M,V)\vcentcolon={\mathrm{Ind}}^{G}_{P}(W^{\prime}{\,\otimes_{K}\,}V)^{\mathfrak{d}}=\big\{f\in{\mathrm{Ind}}^{G}_{P}(W^{\prime}{\,\otimes_{K}\,}V)\,\big|\,\forall{\mathfrak{z}}\in{\mathfrak{d}}:\langle{\mathfrak{z}},f\rangle_{C^{\mathrm{la}}(G,V)}=0\big\}.

The resulting
{\mathcal{F}}^{G}_{P}(M,V)
is an admissible
12
12

12

In the sense of
[
71
, §6]
.
locally analytic
G
-representation which even is strongly admissible
13
13

13

Meaning that as a representation of any (equivalently, of one) compact open subgroup
H\subset G
, it is strongly admissible in the sense of
[
74
, §3]
, i.e. its strong dual is finitely generated as a
D(H)
-module.
if
V
is of finite length
[
58
, Prop. 4.8]
.
This construction yields an exact bi-functor

{\mathcal{F}}^{G}_{P}\colon{\mathcal{O}}^{\mathfrak{p}}_{\mathrm{alg}}\times{\rm Rep}_{K}^{{\mathrm{sm}},{\rm adm}}(L_{\mathrm{\bf P}})\longrightarrow{\rm Rep}_{K}^{{\mathrm{la}},{\rm adm}}(G)\,,\quad(M,V)\longmapsto{\mathcal{F}}_{P}^{G}(M,V),

which is contravariant in
M
and covariant in
V
, see
[
58
, Prop. 4.7]
.

In the case that
V=K
is the trivial representation, there is another description of
{\mathcal{F}}^{G}_{P}(M)={\mathcal{F}}^{G}_{P}(M,K)
, for
M\in{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathrm{alg}}^{\mathfrak{p}}
.
Since
M
is the union of finite-dimensional
U({\mathfrak{p}})
-submodules, via lifting each of those to an algebraic
{\mathrm{\bf P}}
-representation one obtains a
D(P)
-module structure on
M
, cf.
[
58
, §3.4]
.
This yields a unique
D({\mathfrak{g}},P)
-module structure on
M
such that the two actions of
U({\mathfrak{p}})
agree and the Dirac distributions
\delta_{p}\in D(P)
act like the group elements
p\in P
on
M
(
[
58
, Cor. 3.6]
).
Then there are isomorphisms of
D(G)
- resp.
D(G_{0})
-modules
[
58
, Prop. 3.7]

(3.25)

{\mathcal{F}}^{G}_{P}(M)^{\prime}\cong D(G){\,\otimes_{D({\mathfrak{g}},P)}\,}M\cong D(G_{0}){\,\otimes_{D({\mathfrak{g}},P_{0})}\,}M.

By
[
71
, Thm. 5.1]
the locally analytic distribution algebra
D(G_{0})
is a Fréchet–Stein algebra since
G_{0}
is compact.
It holds that
D(G){\,\otimes_{D({\mathfrak{g}},P)}\,}M
is a coadmissible
D(G)
-module
[
58
, Prop. 3.7]
.
Recall that any coadmissible module over a Fréchet–Stein algebra can be endowed with a canonical Fréchet topology
[
71
, §3]
.
We note that with this topology (
3.25
) is a topological isomorphism.

Remark 3.4.2
.

It is expected that a description similar to (
3.25
) holds for the case of non-trivial
V
as well.
In fact, in
[
1
]
Agrawal and Strauch consider functors defined by

\check{{\mathcal{F}}}_{P}^{G}(M,V)\vcentcolon=D(G){\,\otimes_{D({\mathfrak{g}},P)}\,}\big({\rm Lift}(M,\log){\,\otimes_{K}\,}V^{\prime}\big)

to generalize
{\mathcal{F}}_{P}^{G}
to the case of
M
being an element of the extension closure
{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathrm{alg}}^{{\mathfrak{p}},\infty}
of
{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathrm{alg}}^{{\mathfrak{p}}}
.
They show that the resulting
D(G)
-modules
\check{{\mathcal{F}}}_{P}^{G}(M,V)
are coadmissible, see
[
1
, Thm. 4.2.3]
.

We now come back to the concrete situation of
{\mathrm{\bf G}}={\mathrm{GL}}_{d+1,K}
.
As mentioned towards the end of
Section

3.1
, Orlik shows in
[
56
, Lemma 1.2.1]
that the
U({\mathfrak{g}})
-module
\widetilde{H}^{q}_{{\mathbb{P}}_{K}^{d-q}}({\mathbb{P}}_{K}^{d},{\mathcal{E}})
is contained in
{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathrm{alg}}^{{\mathfrak{p}}_{d-q}}
.
Moreover, let
{\mathrm{\bf B}}_{q}={\mathrm{\bf B}}\cap{\mathrm{GL}}_{q,K}
denote the induced Borel subgroup of
{\mathrm{GL}}_{q,K}\hookrightarrow{\mathrm{\bf L}}_{{\mathrm{\bf P}}_{d-q}}
.
Then the Steinberg representation
v^{{\mathrm{GL}}_{q}(K)}_{B_{q}}
is an irreducible smooth representation of
{\mathrm{GL}}_{q}(K)
(
[
17
, Thm. 2 (a)]
), and hence of
P_{d-q}
.
Orlik then obtains a description of the locally analytic
G
-representation (
3.13
) as being isomorphic to

(3.26)

{\mathcal{F}}^{G}_{P_{d-q}}\Big(\widetilde{H}^{q}_{{\mathbb{P}}_{K}^{d-q}}({\mathbb{P}}_{K}^{d},{\mathcal{E}}),v^{{\mathrm{GL}}_{q}(K)}_{B_{q}}\Big).

The other term
H^{q}({\mathbb{P}}_{K}^{d},{\mathcal{E}})^{\prime}{\,\otimes_{K}\,}v^{G}_{Q_{d-q}}
of the extension (
3.12
) is a strongly admissible locally analytic
G
-representation as well, cf. the proof of
[
58
, Lemma 2.4]
.
It follows that the extension
(V^{q}/V^{q-1})^{\prime}_{b}
of the two terms is strongly admissible.
Since the homomorphisms between these (strongly) admissible representations in Orlik’s description are necessarily strict (see
[
71
, Prop. 6.4 (ii)]
), we can conclude that there is a topological isomorphism between (
3.26
) and the strong dual of

(3.27)

\displaystyle D(G){\,\widehat{\otimes}_{D({\mathfrak{g}},P_{d-q}),\iota}\,}\bigg(\widetilde{H}^{q}_{({\mathbb{P}}_{K}^{d-q})^{\mathrm{rig}}}({\mathbb{P}}_{K}^{d},{\mathcal{E}}){\,\widehat{\otimes}_{K}\,}\Big(v^{{\mathrm{GL}}_{q}(K)}_{B_{q}}\Big)^{\prime}_{b}\bigg)

from our description in
Theorem

3.3.2
by the uniqueness of the quotient.

We recall from
Corollary

2.5.5
that
\widetilde{H}^{q}_{({\mathbb{P}}_{K}^{d-q})^{\mathrm{rig}}}({\mathbb{P}}_{K}^{d},{\mathcal{E}})
is the completion of its subspace
\widetilde{H}^{q}_{{\mathbb{P}}_{K}^{d-q}}({\mathbb{P}}_{K}^{d},{\mathcal{E}})
.
Hence (
3.27
) is nothing but the Hausdorff completion of

(3.28)

D(G){\,\otimes_{D({\mathfrak{g}},P_{d-q}),\iota}\,}\bigg(\widetilde{H}^{q}_{{\mathbb{P}}_{K}^{d-q}}({\mathbb{P}}_{K}^{d},{\mathcal{E}}){\,\otimes_{K,\pi}\,}\Big(v^{{\mathrm{GL}}_{q}(K)}_{B_{q}}\Big)^{\prime}_{b}\bigg).

Therefore it is natural to ask how this Hausdorff completion relates to the coadmissible abstract
D(G)
-module underlying (
3.28
) when one considers the latter with its canonical Fréchet topology.
We answer this question in
Corollary

3.4.9
by showing that they agree, i.e. that (
3.28
) already is complete and its locally convex topology is the same as the canonical Fréchet topology.

For the first ingredient used to this end, we return to the general setup of connected split reductive
{\mathrm{\bf G}}
considered at the beginning of this section.
We fix on
U({\mathfrak{g}})\subset D(G)
the subspace topology, and likewise on all subalgebras of
U({\mathfrak{g}})
.
As
U({\mathfrak{g}})
already is contained in
D(G_{0})
and the latter is a
K
-Fréchet algebra,
U({\mathfrak{g}})
and its subalgebras become (jointly continuous) locally convex
K
-algebras this way.

We want to give a concrete description of this topology of
U({\mathfrak{g}})
.
Let
{\mathfrak{x}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathfrak{x}}_{s}
be a
K
-basis of
{\mathfrak{g}}
, and
\varepsilon>0
.
Via the associated PBW basis for
U({\mathfrak{g}})
consisting of
{\mathfrak{x}}_{1}^{k_{1}}\cdots{\mathfrak{x}}_{s}^{k_{s}}
, for
{\underline{k}}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}^{s}
, we define the norm

(3.29)

\bigg\lvert\sum_{{\underline{k}}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}^{s}}a_{{\underline{k}}}\,{\mathfrak{x}}_{1}^{k_{1}}\cdots{\mathfrak{x}}_{s}^{k_{s}}\bigg\rvert_{\varepsilon}\vcentcolon=\sup_{{\underline{k}}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}^{s}}{\lvert{a_{{\underline{k}}}}\rvert}\bigg(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\bigg)^{{\lvert{{\underline{k}}}\rvert}}

on
U({\mathfrak{g}})
where
{\lvert{{\underline{k}}}\rvert}\vcentcolon=k_{1}+\ldots+k_{s}
.

Lemma 3.4.3
.

The topology on
U({\mathfrak{g}})
prescribed by the family of norms
{\lvert{{\,\_\,}}\rvert}_{\varepsilon}
, for
0<\varepsilon<1
, equals the topology defined by regarding
U({\mathfrak{g}})
as a subspace of
D(G)
.

Proof.

By
[
74
, Lemma 2.4]
, the subspace topology of
U({\mathfrak{g}})\subset D_{e}(G)\subset D(G)
is defined via the family of norms

\bigg\lVert\sum_{{\underline{k}}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}^{s}}a_{{\underline{k}}}\,{\mathfrak{x}}_{1}^{k_{1}}\cdots{\mathfrak{x}}_{s}^{k_{s}}\bigg\rVert_{\varepsilon}\vcentcolon=\sup_{{\underline{k}}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}^{s}}{\lvert{a_{{\underline{k}}}\,{\underline{k}}!}\rvert}\bigg(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\bigg)^{{\lvert{{\underline{k}}}\rvert}},

for
\varepsilon>0
, where
{\underline{k}}!\vcentcolon=(k_{1}!)\cdots(k_{s}!)
.
Here
\lVert{\,\_\,}\rVert_{\varepsilon}
yields a topology finer than the one of
\lVert{\,\_\,}\rVert_{\varepsilon^{\prime}}
, for
\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon^{\prime}
.
We immediately find that
\lVert{\,\_\,}\rVert_{\varepsilon}\leq{\lvert{{\,\_\,}}\rvert}_{\varepsilon}
since
{\lvert{{\underline{k}}!}\rvert}\leq 1
.

On the other hand, we obtain by Legendre’s formula for the
p
-adic valuation of
n!
that
v_{p}(n!)\leq\frac{n}{p-1}
, for
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
.
Hence

{\lvert{{\underline{k}}!}\rvert}=p^{-v_{p}({\underline{k}}!)}\geq\Big(p^{\frac{1}{p-1}}\Big)^{-{\lvert{{\underline{k}}}\rvert}}.

It follows that
\lVert{\,\_\,}\rVert_{\varepsilon}\geq\lvert{\,\_\,}\rvert_{p^{\frac{1}{p-1}}\varepsilon}
, and we conclude that the topology defined by the family
(\lvert{\,\_\,}\rvert_{\varepsilon})_{0<\varepsilon<1}
is equal to the topology defined by the family
(\lVert{\,\_\,}\rVert_{\varepsilon})_{0<\varepsilon}
.
∎

Remark 3.4.4
.

In
[
63
, Thm. 2.1]
Schmidt shows that there is a natural isomorphism of topological
K
-algebras between the completion of
U({\mathfrak{g}})
with respect to the family of norms
{\lvert{{\,\_\,}}\rvert}_{\varepsilon}
, for
0<\varepsilon<1
, and the Arens–Michael envelope
\widehat{U}({\mathfrak{g}})
of
U({\mathfrak{g}})
.
The latter is defined as the Hausdorff completion of
U({\mathfrak{g}})
with respect to all submultiplicative seminorms on
U({\mathfrak{g}})
.
Using that the completion of
U({\mathfrak{g}})\subset D(G)
with respect to the subspace topology is its closure
D_{e}(G)
in
D(G)
(see
Corollary

1.6.13
), it follows that there is a natural isomorphism of topological
K
-algebras
\widehat{U}({\mathfrak{g}})\cong D_{e}(G)
as well.

Recalling that we fix the subspace topology on
U({\mathfrak{g}})\subset D(G)
, we now consider a finitely generated
U({\mathfrak{g}})
-module
M
(e.g.
M\in{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathrm{alg}}^{\mathfrak{p}}
).
By assumption we then find some
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
and an epimorphism of
U({\mathfrak{g}})
-modules

(3.30)

U({\mathfrak{g}})^{\oplus n}\relbar\joinrel\twoheadrightarrow M.

Lemma 3.4.5
.

When
M
is endowed with the quotient topology via (
3.30
), it becomes a locally convex
U({\mathfrak{g}})
-module.
Its Hausdorff completion is a nuclear
K
-Fréchet space.

Any homomorphism
f\colon M\rightarrow M^{\prime}
between finitely generated
U({\mathfrak{g}})
-modules is continuous and strict when
M
and
M^{\prime}
carry the quotient topology induced by some epimorphism as above.
In particular, this topology on
M
does not depend on the choice of the epimorphism (
3.30
).

Proof.

For (i), since the quotient topology on
M
is locally convex, we only have to show that the multiplication
U({\mathfrak{g}})\times M\rightarrow M
is continuous.
To do so, we consider the commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U({\mathfrak{g}})\times U({\mathfrak{g}})^{\oplus n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U({\mathfrak{g}})^{\oplus n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U({\mathfrak{g}})\times M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}

where the vertical maps are open by our choice of topology on
M
.
But the multiplication map for the finite free
U({\mathfrak{g}})
-module
U({\mathfrak{g}})^{\oplus n}
is continuous which implies that the multiplication map for
M
is as well.

The Hausdorff completion of
U({\mathfrak{g}})
is its closure
D_{e}(G)
in
D(G)
which is a nuclear
K
-Fréchet space.
In particular,
D_{e}(G)
is hereditarily complete, see the discussion after
[
27
, Def. 1.1.39]
.
Hence the strict epimorphism (
3.30
) induces a strict epimorphism

D_{e}(G)^{\oplus n}\relbar\joinrel\twoheadrightarrow\widehat{M}

onto the Hausdorff completion of
M
by
[
13
, Cor. 2.2]
.
As a quotient of a nuclear
K
-Fréchet space
\widehat{M}
then is one itself, see
[
67
, Prop. 8.3]
and
[
67
, Prop. 19.4 (ii)]
.

For (ii), we argue similarly to
[
7
, 3.7.3 Prop. 2]
.
Consider an epimorphism
\varphi\colon U({\mathfrak{g}})^{\oplus n}\twoheadrightarrow M
of
U({\mathfrak{g}})
-modules which endows
M
with its topology.
Then the homomorphism
\varphi^{\prime}\vcentcolon=f\circ\varphi
is continuous because the addition and multiplication maps of the locally convex
U({\mathfrak{g}})
-module
M^{\prime}
are.
As
\varphi
is open by definition, it follows that
f
is continuous.
Furthermore,
M/{\mathrm{Ker}}(f)
and
{\mathrm{Im}}(f)
are isomorphic as abstract
U({\mathfrak{g}})
-modules.
The homomorphisms between these finitely generated
U({\mathfrak{g}})
-modules that arrange this isomorphism are continuous.
Therefore
M/{\mathrm{Ker}}(f)
and
{\mathrm{Im}}(f)
are topologically isomorphic, i.e.
f
is strict. ∎

Lemma 3.4.6
.

The multiplication map
U({\mathfrak{g}})\times D(P_{0})\rightarrow D(G_{0})
,
(\mu,\delta)\mapsto\mu\ast\delta
induces a topological isomorphism

U({\mathfrak{g}}){\,\otimes_{U({\mathfrak{p}}),\pi}\,}D(P_{0})\overset{\cong}{\longrightarrow}D({\mathfrak{g}},P_{0})

of
U({\mathfrak{g}})
-
D(P_{0})
-bi-modules.

Proof.

Since the convolution product is jointly continuous here, it induces a continuous homomorphism
U({\mathfrak{g}}){\,\otimes_{U({\mathfrak{p}}),\pi}\,}D(P_{0})\rightarrow D(G_{0})
of
U({\mathfrak{g}})
-
D(P_{0})
-bi-modules.
By
[
65
, Lemma 4.1]
this homomorphism is injective with image being precisely
D({\mathfrak{g}},P_{0})
.

On the other hand, let
{\mathfrak{u}}^{-}
denote the Lie algebra of the opposite unipotent radical
{\mathrm{\bf U}}^{-}
of
{\mathrm{\bf P}}
.
Then the direct sum decomposition
{\mathfrak{g}}={\mathfrak{u}}^{-}\oplus{\mathfrak{p}}
yields an isomorphism
U({\mathfrak{g}})\cong U({\mathfrak{u}}^{-}){\,\otimes_{K}\,}U({\mathfrak{p}})
.
As mentioned in
Remark

3.4.4
the completion of
U({\mathfrak{g}})
with respect to the subspace topology
U({\mathfrak{g}})\subset D(G)
is topologically isomorphic to the Arens–Michael envelope
\widehat{U}({\mathfrak{g}})
of
U({\mathfrak{g}})
considered in
[
64
, §3.2]
.
Hence we obtain a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{U}({\mathfrak{u}}^{-}){\,\widehat{\otimes}_{K,\pi}\,}\widehat{U}({\mathfrak{p}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{U}({\mathfrak{g}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U({\mathfrak{u}}^{-}){\,\otimes_{K,\pi}\,}U({\mathfrak{p}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U({\mathfrak{g}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

where the vertical maps are the canonical embeddings, and the upper map is a topological isomorphism by
[
64
, Lemma 3.2.4]
.
It follows from
[
49
, Lemma 2]
that the bijective continuous homomorphism
U({\mathfrak{u}}^{-}){\,\otimes_{K,\pi}\,}U({\mathfrak{p}})\rightarrow U({\mathfrak{g}})
is strict and therefore a topological isomorphism.

Via this topological isomorphism,
Lemma

1.5.11
(ii), and the exactness of the projective tensor product (see
[
13
, Lemma 2.1 (ii)]
) we obtain the topological embedding

U({\mathfrak{g}}){\,\otimes_{U({\mathfrak{p}}),\pi}\,}D(P_{0})\cong U({\mathfrak{u}}^{-}){\,\otimes_{K,\pi}\,}D(P_{0})\lhook\joinrel\longrightarrow D(U^{-}_{0}){\,\otimes_{K,\pi}\,}D(P_{0})\lhook\joinrel\longrightarrow D(U^{-}_{0}){\,\widehat{\otimes}_{K,\pi}\,}D(P_{0}).

The group multiplication induces an isomorphism
U^{-}_{0}\times P_{0}\cong G_{0}
which yields a topological isomorphism

D(U^{-}_{0}){\,\widehat{\otimes}_{K,\pi}\,}D(P_{0})\cong D(U^{-}_{0}\times P_{0})\cong D(G_{0}).

This isomorphism is given by the multiplication in
D(G_{0})
like in the definition of the convolution product in the proof of
Proposition

1.4.3
.
Hence we obtain the commutative square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D(U^{-}_{0}){\,\widehat{\otimes}_{K,\pi}\,}D(P_{0})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D(G_{0})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U({\mathfrak{g}}){\,\otimes_{U({\mathfrak{p}}),\pi}\,}D(P_{0})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D({\mathfrak{g}},P_{0})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

where the vertical maps are strict monomorphisms.
Again,
[
49
, Lemma 2]
implies that
U({\mathfrak{g}}){\,\otimes_{U({\mathfrak{p}}),\pi}\,}D(P_{0})\rightarrow D({\mathfrak{g}},P_{0})
is strict as well, and thus a topological isomorphism as claimed. ∎

Proposition 3.4.7
.

Let
M\in{\mathcal{O}}^{\mathfrak{p}}_{\mathrm{alg}}
be endowed with the topology coming from an epimorphism (
3.30
).
Moreover, let
V
be a strongly admissible smooth
P
-representation endowed with the finest locally convex topology and considered as a locally analytic representation of
P
.
Then there is a topological isomorphism of separately continuous
D(G)
-modules

(3.31)

D(G){\,\widehat{\otimes}_{D({\mathfrak{g}},P),\iota}\,}\big(M{\,\widehat{\otimes}_{K,\pi}\,}V^{\prime}_{b}\big)\cong D(G){\,\otimes_{D({\mathfrak{g}},P)}\,}\big(M{\,\otimes_{K}\,}V^{\prime}\big)

in the sense that
D(G){\,\otimes_{D({\mathfrak{g}},P),\iota}\,}\big(M{\,\otimes_{K,\pi}\,}V^{\prime}_{b}\big)
defined according to
Definition

1.5.8
is complete, and its topology agrees with the canonical Fréchet topology induced from its underlying (abstract)
D(G)
-module being coadmissible.

Proof.

By the assumptions on
M\in{\mathcal{O}}^{\mathfrak{p}}_{\mathrm{alg}}
, we may find a finite-dimensional
U({\mathfrak{p}})
-module
W
and an epimorphism
\varphi\colon U({\mathfrak{g}}){\,\otimes_{U({\mathfrak{p}})}\,}W\twoheadrightarrow M
of
U({\mathfrak{g}})
-modules.
Since
U({\mathfrak{g}}){\,\otimes_{U({\mathfrak{p}}),\pi}\,}W\cong U({\mathfrak{g}})^{\oplus{\mathrm{dim}}_{K}(W)}
is a quotient of
U({\mathfrak{g}}){\,\otimes_{K,\pi}\,}W
, the composition

U({\mathfrak{g}}){\,\otimes_{K,\pi}\,}W\relbar\joinrel\twoheadrightarrow U({\mathfrak{g}}){\,\otimes_{U({\mathfrak{p}}),\pi}\,}W\overset{\varphi}{\relbar\joinrel\twoheadrightarrow}M

is a strict epimorphism by
Lemma

3.4.5
(i).
Hence it follows from
[
49
, Lemma 2 (3)]
that
\varphi
is a strict epimorphism, too.

Using
Lemma

3.4.6
and
Lemma

1.5.11
there is a topological isomorphism

\displaystyle D({\mathfrak{g}},P_{0}){\,\otimes_{D(P_{0}),\pi}\,}W

\displaystyle\cong\big(U({\mathfrak{g}}){\,\otimes_{U({\mathfrak{p}}),\pi}\,}D(P_{0})\big){\,\otimes_{D(P_{0}),\pi}\,}W

\displaystyle\cong U({\mathfrak{g}}){\,\otimes_{U({\mathfrak{p}}),\pi}\,}\big(D(P_{0}){\,\otimes_{D(P_{0}),\pi}\,}W\big)

\displaystyle\cong U({\mathfrak{g}}){\,\otimes_{U({\mathfrak{p}}),\pi}\,}W

which maps
\mu\ast\delta\otimes w
to
\mu\otimes\delta\ast w
, for
\mu\in U({\mathfrak{g}})
,
\delta\in D(P_{0})
,
w\in W
.
Therefore
\varphi
induces a strict epimorphism

D({\mathfrak{g}},P_{0}){\,\otimes_{D(P_{0}),\pi}\,}W\relbar\joinrel\twoheadrightarrow M

which one checks to be
D({\mathfrak{g}},P_{0})
-linear via the method of the proof of Proposition
1.6.16
.

As taking the projective tensor product is exact (see
[
13
, Lemma 2.1 (ii)]
), we obtain a
D({\mathfrak{g}},P_{0})
-linear strict epimorphism

D({\mathfrak{g}},P_{0}){\,\otimes_{D(P_{0}),\pi}\,}\big(W{\,\otimes_{K,\pi}\,}V^{\prime}_{b}\big)\cong\big(D({\mathfrak{g}},P_{0}){\,\otimes_{D(P_{0}),\pi}\,}W\big){\,\otimes_{K,\pi}\,}V^{\prime}_{b}\relbar\joinrel\twoheadrightarrow M{\,\otimes_{K,\pi}\,}V^{\prime}_{b}

using
Lemma

1.5.11
(ii) once again.
Here we extend the trivial
U({\mathfrak{p}})
-action on
V^{\prime}_{b}
(recall that
V
is a smooth representation) to the trivial
U({\mathfrak{g}})
-action.
We note that this extended action together with the given
P
-action on
V
satisfies the condition (2) of
Definition

1.6.18
.

Moreover, the locally convex
D(P_{0})
-module
W{\,\otimes_{K,\pi}\,}V^{\prime}_{b}
is finitely generated as an abstract
D(P_{0})
-module, cf. the proof of
[
1
, Prop. 4.1.5]
and
[
1
, Prop. 6.4.1]
.
Such an epimorphism
D(P_{0})^{\oplus n}\twoheadrightarrow W{\,\otimes_{K,\pi}\,}V^{\prime}_{b}
, for some
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, is necessarily strict by the open mapping theorem
[
67
, Prop. 8.6]
since
W{\,\otimes_{K,\pi}\,}V^{\prime}_{b}
is a
K
-Fréchet space.
Therefore we obtain a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D({\mathfrak{g}},P_{0}){\,\otimes_{K,\pi}\,}D(P_{0})^{\oplus n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D({\mathfrak{g}},P_{0}){\,\otimes_{K,\pi}\,}\big(W{\,\otimes_{K,\pi}\,}V^{\prime}_{b}\big)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D({\mathfrak{g}},P_{0})^{\oplus n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D({\mathfrak{g}},P_{0}){\,\otimes_{D(P_{0}),\pi}\,}\big(W{\,\otimes_{K,\pi}\,}V^{\prime}_{b}\big)}

where the top homomorphism is a strict epimorphism by
[
13
, Lemma 2.1 (ii)]
.
It follows from
[
49
, Lemma 2]
that the bottom epimorphism is strict as well.

In total we thus arrive at a strict epimorphism (cf.
[
1
, Prop. 4.1.5]
for the statement that the abstract module is finitely presented)

\psi\colon D({\mathfrak{g}},P_{0})^{\oplus n}\relbar\joinrel\twoheadrightarrow M{\,\otimes_{K,\pi}\,}V^{\prime}_{b}.

Using the exactness of the projective tensor product again, we obtain the commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D(G_{0}){\,\otimes_{K,\pi}\,}D({\mathfrak{g}},P_{0})^{\oplus n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D(G_{0}){\,\otimes_{K,\pi}\,}\big(M{\,\otimes_{K,\pi}\,}V^{\prime}_{b}\big)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D(G_{0})^{\oplus n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D(G_{0}){\,\otimes_{D({\mathfrak{g}},P_{0}),\pi}\,}\big(M{\,\otimes_{K,\pi}\,}V^{\prime}_{b}\big)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}

where all maps except a priori the bottom one are strict epimorphisms.
Similarly to before one argues that the epimorphism
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}
is strict.
On the other hand, the (abstract)
D(G_{0})
-module
D(G_{0}){\,\otimes_{D({\mathfrak{g}},P_{0})}\,}\big(M{\,\otimes_{K}\,}V^{\prime}_{b}\big)
is coadmissible by
[
1
, Thm. 4.2.3]
.
Therefore
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}
also is strict when
D(G_{0}){\,\otimes_{D({\mathfrak{g}},P_{0})}\,}\big(M{\,\otimes_{K}\,}V^{\prime}_{b}\big)
carries its canonical Fréchet topology (see
[
71
, §3]
).
By the uniqueness of the quotient we conclude that this Fréchet topology agrees with the topology on
D(G_{0}){\,\otimes_{D({\mathfrak{g}},P_{0}),\pi}\,}(M{\,\otimes_{K,\pi}\,}V^{\prime}_{b})
from
Definition

1.5.8
.
In particular, the latter already is complete so that taking the Hausdorff completion is redundant:

D(G_{0}){\,\widehat{\otimes}_{D({\mathfrak{g}},P_{0})}\,}\big(M{\,\widehat{\otimes}_{K,\pi}\,}V^{\prime}_{b}\big)=D(G_{0}){\,\otimes_{D({\mathfrak{g}},P_{0}),\pi}\,}\big(M{\,\otimes_{K,\pi}\,}V^{\prime}_{b}\big).

Note here that since
\widehat{M}
is a
K
-Fréchet space by
Lemma

3.4.5
(i),
M{\,\widehat{\otimes}_{K,\pi}\,}V^{\prime}_{b}\cong\widehat{M}{\,\widehat{\otimes}_{K,\pi}\,}V^{\prime}_{b}
is so as well by
[
67
, Lemma 19.10 (i)]
and the discussion after
[
67
, Prop. 17.6]
.
Therefore the projective and inductive tensor product of
D(G_{0})
and
M{\,\widehat{\otimes}_{K,\pi}\,}V^{\prime}_{b}
over
D({\mathfrak{g}},P_{0})
agree.
Finally, the Iwasawa decomposition yields the isomorphism

D(G){\,\otimes_{D({\mathfrak{g}},P)}\,}\big(M{\,\otimes_{K}\,}V^{\prime}\big)\cong D(G_{0}){\,\otimes_{D({\mathfrak{g}},P_{0})}\,}\big(M{\,\otimes_{K}\,}V^{\prime}\big)

of
D(G_{0})
-modules (cf.
[
1
, §4.2.2]
), and
Lemma

3.3.1
(ii) yields the topological isomorphism

D(G){\,\widehat{\otimes}_{D({\mathfrak{g}},P),\iota}\,}\big(M{\,\widehat{\otimes}_{K,\pi}\,}V^{\prime}_{b}\big)\cong D(G_{0}){\,\widehat{\otimes}_{D({\mathfrak{g}},P_{0})}\,}\big(M{\,\widehat{\otimes}_{K,\pi}\,}V^{\prime}_{b}\big)

of separately continuous
D(G_{0})
-modules.
Together they give the topological isomorphism (
3.31
) of separately continuous
D(G)
-modules.
∎

We return to the concrete setting of
{\mathrm{\bf G}}={\mathrm{GL}}_{d+1,K}
and the parabolic subgroup
{\mathrm{\bf P}}={\mathrm{\bf P}}_{r}
, for
{r}\in\{0,\ldots,d-1\}
, from
Theorem

3.3.2
.
We want to apply
Proposition

3.4.7
to show that (
3.28
) is complete and its locally convex topology agrees with the canonical Fréchet topology.

Let
I\subset\{0,\ldots,d\}
be a non-empty subset, and let
U_{I}\subset{\mathbb{P}}_{K}^{d}
denote the intersection of the corresponding principal open subsets as considered in
Section

2.4
.
Via the countable admissible covering

U_{I}^{\mathrm{rig}}=\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}0<\varepsilon<1\\
\varepsilon\in{\lvert{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{K}}\rvert}\end{subarray}}U_{I,\varepsilon},

and
Lemma

2.4.1
we regard
{\mathcal{E}}(U_{I})
as a subspace of
{\mathcal{E}}(U_{I}^{\mathrm{rig}})=\varprojlim_{\varepsilon\searrow 0}{\mathcal{E}}(U_{I,\varepsilon})
.
Then the topology of
{\mathcal{E}}(U_{I})
is induced by the family of norms
{\lvert{{\,\_\,}}\rvert}_{\varepsilon}
, for
0<\varepsilon<1
, defined in (
2.19
).

Proposition 3.4.8
.

For every non-empty subset
I\subset\{0,\ldots,d\}
, there exist
m\in{\mathbb{N}}
and a surjective homomorphism of
U({\mathfrak{g}})
-modules

\varphi\colon U({\mathfrak{g}})^{\oplus m}\relbar\joinrel\twoheadrightarrow{\mathcal{E}}(U_{I})

such that
\varphi
is continuous and strict when
U({\mathfrak{g}})\subset D(G)
and
{\mathcal{E}}(U_{I})\subset{\mathcal{E}}(U_{I}^{\mathrm{rig}})
carry their respective subspace topologies.

Corollary 3.4.9
.

The
U({\mathfrak{g}})
-module
\widetilde{H}^{d-{r}}_{{\mathbb{P}}_{K}^{r}}({\mathbb{P}}_{K}^{d},{\mathcal{E}})
is finitely generated, and its locally convex topology induced by an epimorphism from a finite free
U({\mathfrak{g}})
-module via (
3.30
) agrees with the subspace topology
\widetilde{H}^{d-{r}}_{{\mathbb{P}}_{K}^{r}}({\mathbb{P}}_{K}^{d},{\mathcal{E}})\subset\widetilde{H}^{d-{r}}_{({\mathbb{P}}_{K}^{{r}})^{\mathrm{rig}}}({\mathbb{P}}_{K}^{d},{\mathcal{E}})
from
Corollary

2.5.5
.
Consequently, the separately continuous
D(G)
-module (
3.28
) already is complete and its topology agrees with the canonical Fréchet topology.

Proof of
Corollary

3.4.9
.

Recall that we defined the Čech complex
C^{\bullet}({\mathcal{U}},{\mathcal{E}})
for the covering
{\mathcal{U}}
given by

{\mathbb{P}}_{K}^{d}\setminus{\mathbb{P}}_{K}^{r}=\bigcup_{i={r}+1}^{d}U_{i}.

We fix on
C^{q}({\mathcal{U}},{\mathcal{E}})
, for
q\geq 0
, the locally convex topology given via the subspace topologies of
{\mathcal{E}}(U_{I})\subset{\mathcal{E}}(U_{I}^{\mathrm{rig}})
, for all non-empty
I\subset\{{r}+1,\ldots,d\}
.
Then we have seen in
Corollary

2.5.5
(or rather in the proof of
Proposition

2.5.2
) that the thereby induced topology on
\widetilde{H}^{d-{r}}_{{\mathbb{P}}_{K}^{r}}({\mathbb{P}}_{K}^{d},{\mathcal{E}})
agrees with the topology of the latter as a subspace of
\widetilde{H}^{d-{r}}_{({\mathbb{P}}_{K}^{{r}})^{\mathrm{rig}}}({\mathbb{P}}_{K}^{d},{\mathcal{E}})
.

On the other hand, the epimorphisms of
U({\mathfrak{g}})
-modules onto
{\mathcal{E}}(U_{I})
from
Proposition

3.4.8
yield
m_{q}\in{\mathbb{N}}
and epimorphisms

(3.32)

\varphi_{q}\colon U({\mathfrak{g}})^{\oplus m_{q}}\relbar\joinrel\twoheadrightarrow C^{q}({\mathcal{U}},{\mathcal{E}})\quad\text{, for all $q\geq 0$,}

which are strict with regard to the subspace topologies of
U({\mathfrak{g}})\subset D(G)
and
{\mathcal{E}}(U_{I})\subset{\mathcal{E}}(U_{I}^{\mathrm{rig}})
.
Therefore the topology on
C^{q}({\mathcal{U}},{\mathcal{E}})
we fixed above agrees with the one induced via (
3.32
) by the uniqueness of the quotient.

Since
U({\mathfrak{g}})
is noetherian and the differentials of
C^{\bullet}({\mathcal{U}},{\mathcal{E}})
are
U({\mathfrak{g}})
-linear, the subspace
Z^{q}({\mathcal{U}},{\mathcal{E}})\subset C^{q}({\mathcal{U}},{\mathcal{E}})
of Čech cocycles is a finitely generated
U({\mathfrak{g}})
-module, too.

Lemma

3.4.5
(ii) implies that its topology induced by some epimorphism from a finite free
U({\mathfrak{g}})
-module agrees with its topology as a subspace of
C^{q}({\mathcal{U}},{\mathcal{E}})
.
We conclude that
H^{q}({\mathbb{P}}_{K}^{d}\setminus{\mathbb{P}}_{K}^{r},{\mathcal{E}})
is finitely generated as a
U({\mathfrak{g}})
-module, and its topology via an epimorphism from a finite free
U({\mathfrak{g}})
-module agrees with its topology as a subquotient of
C^{q}({\mathcal{U}},{\mathcal{E}})
.
Arguing similarly for
\widetilde{H}^{d-{r}}_{{\mathbb{P}}_{K}^{r}}({\mathbb{P}}_{K}^{d},{\mathcal{E}})
, we find that its topology induced via some epimorphism from a finite free
U({\mathfrak{g}})
-module agrees with the topology as a subspace of
\widetilde{H}^{d-{r}}_{({\mathbb{P}}_{K}^{{r}})^{\mathrm{rig}}}({\mathbb{P}}_{K}^{d},{\mathcal{E}})
.

Proposition

3.4.7
applied to
M=\widetilde{H}^{d-{r}}_{{\mathbb{P}}_{K}^{r}}({\mathbb{P}}_{K}^{d},{\mathcal{E}})
and
V=v^{{\mathrm{GL}}_{d-{r}}(K)}_{B_{d-{r}}}
then shows the last statement.
∎

Proof of
Proposition

3.4.8
.

We begin by fixing a suitable
K
-basis of
{\mathfrak{g}}
.
For
\alpha_{u,v}\in\Phi
with
(u,v)\in\{0,\ldots,d\}^{2}
,
u\neq v
, let
L_{(u,v)}
be the standard generator of the root space
{\mathfrak{g}}_{\alpha_{u,v}}
.
Moreover, let
L_{0},\ldots,L_{d}
be the standard basis of
{\mathfrak{t}}
.
Then the
L_{(u,v)}
together with the
L_{i}
constitute a
K
-basis of
{\mathfrak{g}}
.
The action of
U({\mathfrak{g}})
on
{\mathcal{O}}(U_{I})
is given by
[
56
, (1.5)]

\displaystyle L_{(u,v)}^{k}.X^{\underline{\mu}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\mu_{v}!}{(\mu_{v}-k)!}\,X^{{\underline{\mu}}+k\alpha_{u,v}},

\displaystyle L^{k}_{j}.X^{\underline{\mu}}

\displaystyle=\mu_{j}^{k}\,X^{\underline{\mu}},

for
k\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
, where we use the convention that
\frac{\mu_{v}!}{(\mu_{v}-k)!}=\mu_{v}\,(\mu_{v}-1)\cdots(\mu_{v}-k+1)
.

Let the fixed subset
I\subset\{0,\ldots,d\}
be
I=\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{r}\}
with pairwise distinct
i_{j}
.
Let
E
be a finitely generated graded
K[X_{0},\ldots,X_{d}]
-module such that
{\mathcal{E}}
is associated with
E
, e.g.

E=\bigoplus_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}}\Gamma\big({\mathbb{P}}_{K}^{d},{\mathcal{E}}(k)\big),

cf.
[
77
,
Tag 0BXD
]
.
As
{\mathcal{E}}(U_{I})
is the homogeneous localization
(E_{X_{i_{1}}\cdots X_{i_{r}}})^{0}
which is a finitely generated
{\mathcal{O}}(U_{I})
-module, we find homogeneous elements
v_{1},\ldots,v_{n}\in E
and
l_{1},\ldots,l_{n}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
with
\deg(v_{k})=rl_{k}
such that
{\mathcal{E}}(U_{I})
is generated by

\frac{v_{k}}{(X_{i_{1}}\cdots X_{i_{r}})^{l_{k}}}\quad\text{, for $k=1,\ldots,n$,}

as an
{\mathcal{O}}(U_{I})
-module.
For
N\vcentcolon=\max_{k=1}^{n}l_{k}
, we set
E^{\prime}\vcentcolon=\bigoplus_{l=0}^{N}E_{rl}
and may assume that
v_{1},\ldots,v_{n}
is a
K
-basis of
E^{\prime}
.
Since
{\mathrm{\bf G}}
acts on
E
by homogeneous endomorphisms, see
[
50
, §2.3.1]
, so does
{\mathfrak{g}}
and
E^{\prime}
is a
U({\mathfrak{g}})
-submodule of
E
.
Moreover, for
\varepsilon>0
small enough, we may suppose that the estimate

(3.33)

\big\lVert L_{(u,v)}.v_{k}\big\rVert_{E^{\prime}}\leq\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\quad\text{, for all $(u,v)\in\{0,\ldots,d\}^{2}$ with $u\neq v$, and $k=1,\ldots,n$,}

for the maximum norm on
E^{\prime}
with respect to the basis
v_{1},\ldots,v_{n}
holds.

We recall the notation

\displaystyle\Lambda_{I}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\bigg\{{\underline{\mu}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{d+1}\bigg|\sum_{j=0}^{d}\mu_{j}=0,\forall j\in\{0,\ldots,d\}\setminus I:\mu_{j}\geq 0\bigg\},

\displaystyle\Delta_{d}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\left\{{\underline{\mu}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{d+1}\,\middle|\,\forall j=0,\ldots,d:{\lvert{\mu_{j}}\rvert}\leq d\right\}.

As a candidate for the sought
U({\mathfrak{g}})
-linear epimorphism
\varphi
we consider

\displaystyle\varphi\colon U({\mathfrak{g}})^{(I)}\vcentcolon=\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\underline{\nu}}\in\Lambda_{I}\cap\Delta_{d}\\
k=1,\ldots,n\end{subarray}}U({\mathfrak{g}})\,e_{{\underline{\nu}},k}\longrightarrow{\mathcal{E}}(U_{I})\,,\quad{\mathfrak{X}}_{{\underline{\nu}},k}\,e_{{\underline{\nu}},k}\longmapsto{\mathfrak{X}}_{{\underline{\nu}},k}.\bigg(X^{\underline{\nu}}\,\frac{v_{k}}{(X_{i_{1}}\cdots X_{i_{r}})^{l_{k}}}\bigg).

Let
{\lvert{{\,\_\,}}\rvert}_{\varepsilon}
denote the norm on
U({\mathfrak{g}})
defined in (
3.29
) in terms of the
K
-basis of
{\mathfrak{g}}
chosen in the beginning of the proof.
Then it follows from
Lemma

3.4.3
that the topology on the locally convex direct sum
U({\mathfrak{g}})^{(I)}
is induced by the family of norms

\bigg\lvert\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\underline{\nu}}\in\Lambda_{I}\cap\Delta_{d}\\
k=1,\ldots,n\end{subarray}}{\mathfrak{X}}_{{\underline{\nu}},k}\,e_{{\underline{\nu}},k}\bigg\rvert_{\varepsilon}\vcentcolon=\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\underline{\nu}}\in\Lambda_{I}\cap\Delta_{d}\\
k=1,\ldots,n\end{subarray}}\lvert{\mathfrak{X}}_{{\underline{\nu}},k}\rvert_{\varepsilon}\quad\text{, for $0<\varepsilon<1$.}

Lemma 3.4.10
.

The map
\varphi
is a continuous homomorphism of locally convex
K
-vector spaces.

Proof.

It suffices to show that, for all
v\in{\mathcal{E}}(U_{I})
, the map
U({\mathfrak{g}})\rightarrow{\mathcal{E}}(U_{I})
,
{\mathfrak{X}}\mapsto{\mathfrak{X}}.v
, is continuous.
This in turn follows once we see that, for all
v\in{\mathcal{E}}(U_{I})
and
0<\varepsilon<1
, there exists
0<\delta<1
such that

(3.34)

\lvert{\mathfrak{X}}.v\rvert_{\varepsilon}\leq\lvert{\mathfrak{X}}\rvert_{\delta}\lvert v\rvert_{\varepsilon}\quad\text{, for all ${\mathfrak{X}}\in U({\mathfrak{g}})$.}

We denote the
K
-basis elements of
{\mathfrak{g}}
fixed earlier by

\big\{{\mathfrak{x}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathfrak{x}}_{s}\big\}\vcentcolon=\big\{L_{(u,v)}\,\big|\,\alpha_{u,v}\in\Phi\big\}\cup\big\{L_{0},\ldots,L_{d}\big\}.

Moreover, let
w_{1},\ldots,w_{m}
be an
{\mathcal{O}}(U_{I})
-basis of
{\mathcal{E}}(U_{I})
which defines the family of norms
{\lvert{{\,\_\,}}\rvert}_{\varepsilon}
as in (
2.19
).
For a given
0<\varepsilon<1
, we choose
\delta>0
small enough such that
\delta\leq\varepsilon
and

\lvert{\mathfrak{x}}_{j}w_{l}\rvert_{\varepsilon}\leq\frac{1}{\delta}\quad\text{, for all $j=1,\ldots,s$ and $l=1,\ldots,m$.}

We first want to show that

(3.35)

\lvert{\mathfrak{x}}.f\rvert_{\varepsilon}\leq\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\,\lvert f\rvert_{\varepsilon}\quad\text{, for all ${\mathfrak{x}}\in\{{\mathfrak{x}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathfrak{x}}_{s}\}$ and $f\in{\mathcal{O}}(U_{I})$.}

To this end it suffices to consider monomials
f=X^{\underline{\mu}}
, for
{\underline{\mu}}\in\Lambda_{I}
.
For
{\mathfrak{x}}=L_{j}
, we have

\lvert L_{j}.X^{\underline{\mu}}\rvert_{\varepsilon}=\lvert\mu_{j}\,X^{\underline{\mu}}\rvert_{\varepsilon}={\lvert{\mu_{j}}\rvert}\lvert X^{\underline{\mu}}\rvert_{\varepsilon}\leq\lvert X^{\underline{\mu}}\rvert_{\varepsilon}\leq\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\,\lvert X^{\underline{\mu}}\rvert_{\varepsilon}.

Moreover, for
{\mathfrak{x}}=L_{(u,v)}
, we compute

\displaystyle\lvert L_{(u,v)}.X^{\underline{\mu}}\rvert_{\varepsilon}=\lvert\mu_{v}\,X^{{\underline{\mu}}+\alpha_{u,v}}\rvert_{\varepsilon}={\lvert{\mu_{v}}\rvert}\bigg(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\bigg)^{{\lvert{\max(0,{\underline{\mu}}+\alpha_{u,v})}\rvert}}\leq\bigg(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\bigg)^{{\lvert{\max(0,{\underline{\mu}}+\alpha_{u,v})}\rvert}}.

The sought inequality
\lvert L_{(u,v)}.X^{\underline{\mu}}\rvert_{\varepsilon}\leq\big(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\big)^{1+{\lvert{\max(0,{\underline{\mu}})}\rvert}}=\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\lvert X^{\underline{\mu}}\rvert_{\varepsilon}
then follows from

\max(0,\mu_{u}+1)+\max(0,\mu_{v}-1)\leq 1+\max(0,\mu_{u})+\max(0,\mu_{v}).

Now, we turn towards
{\mathcal{E}}(U_{I})\cong{\mathcal{O}}(U_{I}){\,\otimes_{K}\,}{\mathcal{E}}(x_{i})
.
To prove (
3.34
) it suffices to show the following inequalities on the PBW basis of
U({\mathfrak{g}})
:

\bigg\lvert({\mathfrak{x}}_{1}^{k_{1}}\cdots{\mathfrak{x}}_{s}^{k_{s}}).\bigg(\sum_{l=1}^{m}f_{l}\,w_{l}\bigg)\bigg\rvert_{\varepsilon}\leq\bigg(\frac{1}{\delta}\bigg)^{{\lvert{{\underline{k}}}\rvert}}\max_{l=1}^{m}\,\lvert f_{l}\rvert_{\varepsilon}=\lvert{\mathfrak{x}}_{1}^{k_{1}}\cdots{\mathfrak{x}}_{s}^{k_{s}}\rvert_{\delta}\,\bigg\lvert\sum_{l=1}^{m}f_{l}\,w_{l}\bigg\rvert_{\varepsilon},

for all
{\underline{k}}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}^{s}
and
\sum_{l=1}^{m}f_{l}\,w_{l}\in{\mathcal{E}}(U_{I})
.
We do so via induction on
{\lvert{{\underline{k}}}\rvert}
.
With the base case being clear, let
{\underline{k}}\in{\mathbb{N}}^{s}_{0}
with
{\lvert{{\underline{k}}}\rvert}>0
, and
\sum_{l=1}^{m}f_{l}\,w_{l}\in{\mathcal{E}}(U_{I})
.
Let
j\in\{1,\ldots,s\}
be maximal such that
k_{j}>0
and
k_{j^{\prime}}=0
, for all
j^{\prime}>j
.
By the Leibniz product rule we have

\displaystyle\bigg\lvert{\mathfrak{x}}_{j}.\bigg(\sum_{l=1}^{m}f_{l}\,w_{l}\bigg)\bigg\rvert_{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle=\bigg\lvert\sum_{l=1}^{m}({\mathfrak{x}}_{j}.f_{l})\,w_{l}+f_{l}\,({\mathfrak{x}}_{j}.w_{l})\bigg\rvert_{\varepsilon}\leq\max_{l=1}^{m}\,\max\big(\,\lvert{\mathfrak{x}}_{j}.f_{l}\rvert_{\varepsilon}\,,\,\lvert f_{l}\rvert_{\varepsilon}\,\lvert{\mathfrak{x}}_{j}.w_{l}\rvert_{\varepsilon}\big)

\displaystyle\leq\max_{l=1}^{m}\,\max\bigg(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\,\lvert f_{l}\rvert_{\varepsilon}\,,\,\lvert f_{l}\rvert_{\varepsilon}\,\frac{1}{\delta}\bigg)\leq\frac{1}{\delta}\max_{l=1}^{m}\,\lvert f_{l}\rvert_{\varepsilon}.

Here we have additionally used (
3.35
),
\delta\leq\varepsilon
, and that
\lvert{\mathfrak{x}}_{j}.w_{l}\rvert_{\varepsilon}\leq\frac{1}{\delta}
, for all
l=1,\ldots,m
.
Together with the induction hypothesis we then conclude that

\displaystyle\bigg\lvert({\mathfrak{x}}_{1}^{k_{1}}\cdots{\mathfrak{x}}_{s}^{k_{s}}).\bigg(\sum_{l=1}^{m}f_{l}\,w_{l}\bigg)\bigg\rvert_{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle=\Bigg\lvert({\mathfrak{x}}_{1}^{k_{1}}\cdots{\mathfrak{x}}_{j}^{k_{j}-1}).\Bigg({\mathfrak{x}}_{j}.\bigg(\sum_{l=1}^{m}f_{l}\,w_{l}\bigg)\Bigg)\Bigg\rvert_{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle\leq\lvert{\mathfrak{x}}_{1}^{k_{1}}\cdots{\mathfrak{x}}_{j}^{k_{j}-1}\rvert_{\delta}\,\bigg\lvert{\mathfrak{x}}_{j}.\bigg(\sum_{l=1}^{m}f_{l}\,w_{l}\bigg)\bigg\rvert_{\varepsilon}\leq\bigg(\frac{1}{\delta}\bigg)^{{\lvert{{\underline{k}}}\rvert}}\max_{l=1}^{m}\,\lvert f_{l}\rvert_{\varepsilon}.

∎

We also introduce, for
{\underline{\mu}}\in\Lambda_{I}
, the auxiliary constants

A({\underline{\mu}})\vcentcolon=\prod_{k=0}^{N}\Bigg(\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}j=0\\
j\in I\end{subarray}}^{d}\frac{1}{{\lvert{\min(0,\mu_{j}+1-k)!}\rvert}}\cdot\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}j=0\\
j\notin I\end{subarray}}^{d}\frac{1}{{\lvert{\min(0,\mu_{j}+1)!}\rvert}}\Bigg).

Here and later we use the conventions

\displaystyle(-n)!

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=(-n)(-n+1)\cdots(-1)=(-1)^{n}n!\quad\text{, for $n\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}$, and}

\displaystyle\max(0,{\underline{\mu}})

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\big(\max(0,\mu_{0}),\ldots,\max(0,\mu_{d})\big).

Lemma 3.4.11
.

For all
l=0,\ldots,N
,
{\underline{\mu}}\in\Lambda_{I}
, and
u,v\in\{0,\ldots,d\}
with
u\neq v
,
\mu_{u}>1
,
\mu_{v}<-1
, it holds that

\frac{1}{{\lvert{\mu_{v}+1-l}\rvert}}\,A({\underline{\mu}}-\alpha_{u,v})\leq A({\underline{\mu}}).

There are constants
C>0
,
a\geq 1
such that
A({\underline{\mu}})\leq Ca^{\lvert{\max(0,{\underline{\mu}})}\rvert}
, for all
{\underline{\mu}}\in\Lambda_{I}
.

Proof.

Writing
{\underline{\mu}}^{\prime}\vcentcolon={\underline{\mu}}-\alpha_{u,v}
in the setting of (i), it suffices to show that the rational number

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\mu_{v}+1-l}\,\prod_{k=0}^{N}\Bigg(\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}j=0\\
j\in I\end{subarray}}^{d}\frac{\min(0,\mu_{j}+1-k)!}{\min(0,\mu^{\prime}_{j}+1-k)!}\cdot\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}j=0\\
j\notin I\end{subarray}}^{d}\frac{\min(0,\mu_{j}+1)!}{\min(0,\mu^{\prime}_{j}+1)!}\Bigg)

in fact is an integer.
We may focus on the terms in the product for
j=u
and
j=v
.
But using
\mu_{u}>1
and
\mu_{v}<-1
, we indeed find that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\mu_{v}+1-l}\,\prod_{k=0}^{N}\frac{\min(0,\mu_{v}+1-k)!}{\min(0,\mu^{\prime}_{v}+1-k)!}\cdot\frac{\min(0,\mu_{u}+1)!}{\min(0,\mu^{\prime}_{u}+1)!}

\displaystyle\qquad=\frac{1}{\mu_{v}+1-l}\,\prod_{k=0}^{N}\frac{(\mu_{v}+1-k)!}{(\mu_{v}+2-k)!}\cdot\frac{0!}{0!}=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=0\\
k\neq l\end{subarray}}^{N}(\mu_{v}+1-k)\in{\mathbb{Z}}.

For (ii), we again use the estimate
v_{p}(n!)\leq\frac{n}{p-1}
of the
p
-adic valuation with
n\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
.
This yields, for
j\in I
,

\displaystyle v_{p}\big(\min(0,\mu_{j}+1-k)!\big)\leq\frac{-\min(0,\mu_{j}+1-k)}{p-1}\leq\frac{-\min(0,\mu_{j})+k}{p-1}.

It follows from similar estimates for
j\notin I
and from
\sum_{j=0}^{d}\mu_{j}=0
that

\displaystyle v_{p}\Bigg(\prod_{k=0}^{N}\Bigg(\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}j=0\\
j\in I\end{subarray}}^{d}\min(0,\mu_{j}+1-k)!\cdot\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}j=0\\
j\notin I\end{subarray}}^{d}\min(0,\mu_{j}+1)!\Bigg)\Bigg)

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\frac{1}{p-1}\sum_{k=0}^{N}\bigg(rk-\sum_{j=0}^{d}\min(0,\mu_{j})\bigg)=\frac{1}{p-1}\bigg(r\,\frac{N(N+1)}{2}+(N+1){\lvert{\max(0,{\underline{\mu}})}\rvert}\bigg).

Hence we conclude that

\displaystyle A({\underline{\mu}})\leq p^{\frac{1}{p-1}\big(r\,\frac{N(N+1)}{2}+(N+1){\lvert{\max(0,{\underline{\mu}})}\rvert}\big)}

which shows the claim with the constants
C=p^{\frac{rN(N+1)}{2(p-1)}}
and
a=p^{\frac{N+1}{p-1}}
.
∎

Lemma 3.4.12
.

Let
0<\varepsilon<1
be small enough such that it satisfies the condition of (
3.33
).
Then, for all
{\underline{\mu}}\in\Lambda_{I}
and
k\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
, there exists
{\mathfrak{Y}}_{{\underline{\mu}},k}\in U({\mathfrak{g}})^{(I)}
such that

\displaystyle\varphi\big({\mathfrak{Y}}_{{\underline{\mu}},k}\big)=X^{\underline{\mu}}\,\frac{v_{k}}{(X_{i_{1}}\cdots X_{i_{r}})^{l_{k}}}
,

\displaystyle\big\lvert{\mathfrak{Y}}_{{\underline{\mu}},k}\big\rvert_{\varepsilon}\leq A({\underline{\mu}})\bigg(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\bigg)^{\lvert{\max(0,{\underline{\mu}})}\rvert}
.

Proof.

We use induction on
\lVert{\underline{\mu}}\rVert\vcentcolon=\sum_{j=0}^{d}{\lvert{\mu_{j}}\rvert}
.
For
{\underline{\mu}}\in\Delta_{d}
, we can take
{\mathfrak{Y}}_{{\underline{\mu}},k}\vcentcolon=1\,e_{{\underline{\mu}},k}
and the claim is trivial.
Therefore we may assume that there is
u\in\{0,\ldots,d\}
such that
{\lvert{\mu_{u}}\rvert}>d
.
If
\mu_{u}>0
, it follows from

0=\sum_{j=0}^{d}\mu_{j}=\mu_{u}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j=0\\
j\neq u\end{subarray}}^{d}\mu_{j}

that
\sum_{j=0,j\neq u}^{d}\mu_{j}<-d
, and hence that there exists
v\in I
,
v\neq u
such that
\mu_{v}<-1
.
Arguing similarly for the case
\mu_{u}<0
, we thus may assume that there are
u\in\{0,\ldots,d\}
,
v\in I
,
u\neq v
, such that
\mu_{u}>1
,
\mu_{v}<-1
.

We now set
{\underline{\mu}}^{\prime}\vcentcolon={\underline{\mu}}-\alpha_{u,v}={\underline{\mu}}-\epsilon_{u}+\epsilon_{v}
so that
\lVert{\underline{\mu}}^{\prime}\rVert=\lVert{\underline{\mu}}\rVert-2
.
For
k\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
, we express
L_{(u,v)}.v_{k}
in the
K
-basis
v_{1},\ldots,v_{n}
of
E^{\prime}
:

L_{(u,v)}.v_{k}=\sum_{k^{\prime}=1}^{n}a_{k^{\prime}}v_{k^{\prime}}\quad\text{, for $a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}\in K$,}

so that
\max_{k^{\prime}=1}^{n}{\lvert{a_{k^{\prime}}}\rvert}=\lVert L_{(u,v)}.v_{k}\rVert_{E^{\prime}}\leq\frac{1}{\varepsilon}
using (
3.33
).
Note that for the degrees of these homogeneous elements we have
l_{k^{\prime}}=l_{k}
, for all
k^{\prime}\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
with
a_{k^{\prime}}\neq 0
, since
{\mathfrak{g}}
acts on
E^{\prime}
by homogeneous endomorphisms.
Moreover, by the induction hypothesis for
{\underline{\mu}}^{\prime}
, for all
k^{\prime}=1,\ldots,n
, there exist
{\mathfrak{Y}}_{{\underline{\mu}}^{\prime},k^{\prime}}\in U({\mathfrak{g}})^{(I)}
as specified in the statement of the lemma.
We set

{\mathfrak{Y}}_{{\underline{\mu}},k}\vcentcolon=\frac{1}{\mu_{v}+1-l_{k}}\bigg(L_{(u,v)}{\mathfrak{Y}}_{{\underline{\mu}}^{\prime},k}-\sum_{k^{\prime}=1}^{n}a_{k^{\prime}}{\mathfrak{Y}}_{{\underline{\mu}}^{\prime},k^{\prime}}\bigg).

Applying the Leibniz product rule we then find that

\displaystyle L_{(u,v)}.\bigg(X^{{\underline{\mu}}^{\prime}}\frac{v_{k}}{(X_{i_{1}}\cdots X_{i_{r}})^{l_{k}}}\bigg)

\displaystyle=\bigg(L_{(u,v)}.\frac{X^{{\underline{\mu}}^{\prime}}}{(X_{i_{1}}\cdots X_{i_{r}})^{l_{k}}}\bigg)v_{k}+\frac{X^{{\underline{\mu}}^{\prime}}}{(X_{i_{1}}\cdots X_{i_{r}})^{l_{k}}}\big(L_{(u,v)}.v_{k}\big)

\displaystyle=(\mu_{v}+1-l_{k})\frac{X^{{\underline{\mu}}}}{(X_{i_{1}}\cdots X_{i_{r}})^{l_{k}}}v_{k}+\sum_{k^{\prime}=1}^{n}a_{k^{\prime}}X^{{\underline{\mu}}^{\prime}}\frac{v_{k^{\prime}}}{(X_{i_{1}}\cdots X_{i_{r}})^{l_{k^{\prime}}}}.

From this it follows that

\varphi\big({\mathfrak{Y}}_{{\underline{\mu}},k}\big)=\frac{X^{{\underline{\mu}}}}{(X_{i_{1}}\cdots X_{i_{r}})^{l_{k}}}v_{k}.

Moreover, the estimates from the induction hypothesis for the
{\mathfrak{Y}}_{{\underline{\mu}}^{\prime},k^{\prime}}
yield

\displaystyle{\lvert{{\mathfrak{Y}}_{{\underline{\mu}},k}}\rvert}_{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{{\lvert{\mu_{v}+1-l_{k}}\rvert}}\max\Big({\lvert{L_{(u,v)}{\mathfrak{Y}}_{{\underline{\mu}}^{\prime},k}}\rvert}_{\varepsilon}\,,\,\max_{k^{\prime}=1}^{n}{\lvert{a_{k^{\prime}}}\rvert}{\lvert{{\mathfrak{Y}}_{{\underline{\mu}}^{\prime},k^{\prime}}}\rvert}_{\varepsilon}\Big)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{{\lvert{\mu_{v}+1-l_{k}}\rvert}}\max\Bigg(A({\underline{\mu}}^{\prime})\bigg(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\bigg)^{{\lvert{\max(0,{\underline{\mu}}^{\prime})}\rvert}+1},\Big(\max_{k^{\prime}=1}^{n}{\lvert{a_{k^{\prime}}}\rvert}\Big)A({\underline{\mu}}^{\prime})\bigg(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\bigg)^{{\lvert{\max(0,{\underline{\mu}}^{\prime})}\rvert}}\Bigg)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{{\lvert{\mu_{v}+1-l_{k}}\rvert}}A({\underline{\mu}}^{\prime})\bigg(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\bigg)^{{\lvert{\max(0,{\underline{\mu}}^{\prime})}\rvert}+1}

\displaystyle\leq A({\underline{\mu}})\bigg(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\bigg)^{{\lvert{\max(0,{\underline{\mu}})}\rvert}}.

In the last step we also have used
Lemma

3.4.11
(i) and that
{\lvert{\max(0,{\underline{\mu}}^{\prime})}\rvert}={\lvert{\max(0,{\underline{\mu}})}\rvert}-1
. ∎

After these preparations we now show that the epimorphism
\varphi
is strict.
It suffices to prove that
\varphi
is an open map.
Hence, let
U\subset U({\mathfrak{g}})^{(I)}
be an open neighbourhood of
0
and we want to show that
\varphi(U)
is an open neighbourhood of
0
.
By
[
59
, Thm. 3.3.6]
there exist
0<\varepsilon_{1},\ldots,\varepsilon_{t}<1
, for some
t\in{\mathbb{N}}
, and
R>0
such that

\big\{{\mathfrak{X}}\in U({\mathfrak{g}})^{(I)}\big|\max_{i=1}^{t}\,\lvert{\mathfrak{X}}\rvert_{\varepsilon_{i}}<R\big\}\subset U.

We may suppose that
\varepsilon\vcentcolon=\varepsilon_{1}\leq\ldots\leq\varepsilon_{t}
so that the former set is equal to

B^{({\lvert{{\,\_\,}}\rvert}_{\varepsilon}),-}_{R}(0)\vcentcolon=\big\{{\mathfrak{X}}\in U({\mathfrak{g}})^{(I)}\,\big|\,\lvert{\mathfrak{X}}\rvert_{\varepsilon}<R\big\}

as
\lvert{\,\_\,}\rvert_{\varepsilon^{\prime}}\leq\lvert{\,\_\,}\rvert_{\varepsilon}
, for
\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon^{\prime}
.
It follows that
\varphi\big(B^{({\lvert{{\,\_\,}}\rvert}_{\varepsilon}),-}_{R}(0)\big)\subset\varphi(U)
.
We moreover may assume that
\varepsilon>0
is sufficiently small as in (
3.33
).

Then let
\delta\vcentcolon=a^{-1}\varepsilon
where
a\geq 1
is the constant from
Lemma

3.4.11
(ii).
Let
\lVert{\,\_\,}\rVert_{\delta}
denote the quotient norm on
{\mathcal{E}}(U_{I})
induced via

\bigoplus_{k=1}^{n}{\mathcal{O}}(U_{I})\,e_{k}\relbar\joinrel\twoheadrightarrow{\mathcal{E}}(U_{I})\,,\quad X^{\underline{\mu}}\,e_{k}\longmapsto X^{\underline{\mu}}\frac{v_{k}}{(X_{i_{1}}\cdots X_{i_{r}})^{l_{k}}},

with
{\mathcal{O}}(U_{I})\subset{\mathcal{O}}(U_{I,\delta})
carrying the Gauss norm
{\lvert{{\,\_\,}}\rvert}_{\delta}
, see
Remark

2.4.2
.
Note that the norm
\lvert{\,\_\,}\rvert_{\delta}
on
{\mathcal{E}}(U_{I})
(i.e. the one given by a fixed
{\mathcal{O}}(U_{I})
-basis as in (
2.19
)) is equivalent to
\lVert{\,\_\,}\rVert_{\delta}
by
[
7
, 3.7.3 Prop. 3]
. To prove the proposition it therefore suffices to show that

(3.36)

B_{C^{-1}R}^{(\lVert{\,\_\,}\rVert_{\delta}),-}(0)\subset\varphi\big(B_{R}^{({\lvert{{\,\_\,}}\rvert}_{\varepsilon}),-}(0)\big)

where
C>0
is the constant from
Lemma

3.4.11
(ii).
Indeed, then
B_{C^{-1}R}^{(\lVert{\,\_\,}\rVert_{\delta}),-}(0)\subset\varphi(U)
is an open neighbourhood of
0
with respect to
\lvert{\,\_\,}\rvert_{\delta}
on
{\mathcal{E}}(U_{I})
, and hence with respect to the subspace topology of
{\mathcal{E}}(U_{I})\subset{\mathcal{E}}(U_{I}^{\mathrm{rig}})
as well.

To show (
3.36
), let
v\in{\mathcal{E}}(U_{I})
with
\lVert v\rVert_{\delta}<\frac{R}{C}
.
By the definition of the quotient norm
\lVert{\,\_\,}\rVert_{\delta}
, there exist
a_{{\underline{\mu}},k}\in K
such that

\displaystyle v=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\underline{\mu}}\in\Lambda_{I}\\
k=1,\ldots,n\end{subarray}}a_{{\underline{\mu}},k}\,X^{\underline{\mu}}\frac{v_{k}}{(X_{i_{1}}\cdots X_{i_{r}})^{l_{k}}}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\underline{\mu}}\in\Lambda_{I}\\
k=1,\ldots,n\end{subarray}}{\lvert{a_{{\underline{\mu}},k}}\rvert}\bigg(\frac{1}{\delta}\bigg)^{{\lvert{\max(0,{\underline{\mu}})}\rvert}}<\frac{R}{C}.

For all
{\underline{\mu}}\in\Lambda_{I}
and
k=1,\ldots,n
, we find
{\mathfrak{Y}}_{{\underline{\mu}},k}\in U({\mathfrak{g}})^{(I)}
as specified in
Lemma

3.4.12
.
Then

{\mathfrak{Y}}\vcentcolon=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\underline{\mu}}\in\Lambda_{I}\\
k=1,\ldots,n\end{subarray}}a_{{\underline{\mu}},k}\,{\mathfrak{Y}}_{{\underline{\mu}},k}\in U({\mathfrak{g}})^{(I)}

satisfies
\varphi({\mathfrak{Y}})=v
.
Moreover, it follows from
Lemma

3.4.11
(ii) that

\displaystyle\lvert{\mathfrak{Y}}\rvert_{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\underline{\mu}}\in\Lambda_{I}\\
k=1,\ldots,n\end{subarray}}{\lvert{a_{{\underline{\mu}},k}}\rvert}{\lvert{{\mathfrak{Y}}_{{\underline{\mu}},k}}\rvert}_{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\underline{\mu}}\in\Lambda_{I}\\
k=1,\ldots,n\end{subarray}}{\lvert{a_{{\underline{\mu}},k}}\rvert}A({\underline{\mu}})\bigg(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\bigg)^{{\lvert{\max(0,{\underline{\mu}})}\rvert}}\leq C\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\underline{\mu}}\in\Lambda_{I}\\
k=1,\ldots,n\end{subarray}}{\lvert{a_{{\underline{\mu}},k}}\rvert}\bigg(\frac{1}{\delta}\bigg)^{{\lvert{\max(0,{\underline{\mu}})}\rvert}}<R

showing that
v\in\varphi\big(B_{R}^{({\lvert{{\,\_\,}}\rvert}_{\varepsilon}),-}(0)\big)
.
∎

Finally, we allow
K
to be a finite extension of a non-archimedean local field
L
of arbitrary characteristic, and return to the setting of a connected split reductive group
{\mathrm{\bf G}}
over
L
as considered in the beginning of this section.
Let
{\mathrm{\bf P}}
a standard parabolic subgroup of
{\mathrm{\bf G}}
.
The preceding considerations as well as the generalization of the functors
{\mathcal{F}}^{G}_{P}
in the
p
-adic situation due to Agrawal and Strauch
[
1
]
motivate the following definition.

Definition 3.4.13

(cf.
[
1
, Def. 4.2.1]
)
.

For a separately continuous
D({\mathfrak{g}},P,K)
-module
M
and an admissible smooth
L_{\mathrm{\bf P}}
-representations
V
on a
K
-vector space considered with the finest locally convex topology, we define the functor

\widetilde{{\mathcal{F}}}^{G}_{P}(M,V)\vcentcolon=D(G,K){\,\widehat{\otimes}_{D({\mathfrak{g}},P,K),\iota}\,}\big(M{\,\widehat{\otimes}_{K,\pi}\,}V^{\prime}_{b}\big)

which takes values in the category of separately continuous
D(G,K)
-modules.

Like before
V^{\prime}_{b}
is a nuclear
K
-Fréchet space, for such an admissible smooth
L_{\mathrm{\bf P}}
-representation
V
.
If the Hausdorff completion
\widehat{M}
of
M
is a nuclear
K
-Fréchet space as well, it follows from the discussion after
[
67
, Prop. 17.6]
and
[
67
, Prop. 19.11, Prop. 20.4]
that
M{\,\widehat{\otimes}_{K,\pi}\,}V^{\prime}_{b}=\widehat{M}{\,\widehat{\otimes}_{K,\pi}\,}V^{\prime}_{b}
is a nuclear
K
-Fréchet space, too.
Since
Lemma

3.3.1
(ii) yields a topological isomorphism

D(G){\,\widehat{\otimes}_{D({\mathfrak{g}},P),\iota}\,}\big(M{\,\widehat{\otimes}_{K,\pi}\,}V^{\prime}_{b}\big)\cong D(G_{0}){\,\widehat{\otimes}_{D({\mathfrak{g}},P_{0})}\,}\big(M{\,\widehat{\otimes}_{K,\pi}\,}V^{\prime}_{b}\big),

it follows from
[
67
, Prop. 19.4 (ii)]
that
\widetilde{{\mathcal{F}}}^{G}_{P}(M,V)
is a nuclear
K
-Fréchet space.
Hence the strong dual
\widetilde{{\mathcal{F}}}^{G}_{P}(M,V)^{\prime}_{b}
is a locally analytic
G
-representation of compact type in this case, by
Proposition

1.4.10
.

Remark 3.4.14
.

Let
M
be an abstract module for the (algebraic) distribution algebra
{\rm Dist}({\mathrm{\bf G}}){\,\otimes_{L}\,}K={\mathrm{hy}}(G)
.
An obvious question is under which algebraic conditions on the module
M
(such as
M\in{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathrm{alg}}^{\mathfrak{p}}
in the
p
-adic case) this lifts to a separately continuous
D({\mathfrak{g}},P)
-module structure on
M
.
Here analogues of the BGG category
{\mathcal{O}}
in the setting of
{\rm char}(K)>0
should play a role.
For recent developments in regard to these analogues, see
[
2
,
3
,
31
,
55
]
.

Appendix A
Non-Archimedean Functional Analysis

Here we collect some results of non-archimedean functional analysis.
They are all (slight generalizations of) statements that can be found in the literature or adaptions from the archimedean setting.
Let
K
be a spherically complete non-archimedean field with ring of integers
{\mathcal{O}}_{K}=\{x\in K\mid{\lvert{x}\rvert}\leq 1\}
.

We recall the definition of a compact continuous homomorphism between locally convex
K
-vector spaces
[
59
, Ch. 8.8 p. 334]
:

Definition A.1
.

A continuous homomorphism
f\colon V\rightarrow W
between Hausdorff locally convex
K
-vector spaces is
compact
if there exists a complete compactoid subset
X\subset W
(cf.
[
59
, Def. 3.8.1]
) such that
f^{-1}(X)\subset V
is a neighbourhood of
0
in
V
.

A continuous homomorphism
f\colon V\rightarrow W
between Hausdorff locally convex
K
-vector spaces is
semicompact
if there exists a compactoid Banach disk
B\subset W
(cf.
[
59
, p. 414]
) such that
f^{-1}(B)\subset V
is a neighbourhood of
0
in
V
.

Remarks A.2
.

If
f
is compact, then
f
is semicompact.
If
W
is quasi-complete, then the converse holds as well
[
59
, Ch. 8.8 p. 334]
.

This definition is equivalent to the one in
[
67
, §16]
.
There
f
is defined to be compact if there exists an open lattice
L\subset V
such that the closure
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{f}\subset W
is bounded and c-compact.

Proof of (ii).

For an open lattice
L\subset V
, by
[
67
, Prop. 12.7]

\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{f}
is bounded and c-compact if and only if it is compactoid and complete.
Hence
X\vcentcolon=\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{f}
yields a complete compactoid subset such that
f^{-1}(X)\supset L
is a neighbourhood of
0
in
V
.

On the other hand, given a complete compactoid
X\subset W
such that
f^{-1}(X)
is a neighbourhood of
0
in
V
, i.e.
f^{-1}(X)
contains an open lattice
L\subset V
, it follows that
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{f}\subset\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{X}
.
But this implies that
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{f}
itself is compactoid by
[
59
, Thm. 3.8.4]
, and complete by
[
67
, Rmk. 7.1 (iv), (v)]
.
∎

Lemma A.3
.

Consider the following commutative square of Hausdorff locally convex
K
-vector spaces:

(A.1)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W^{\prime}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}

If
g^{\prime}
is compact and
f^{\prime}
a strict monomorphism, then
g
is compact.

In the commutative square (
A.1
), if
g
is compact and
f
a strict epimorphism, then
g^{\prime}
is compact.

Finite products of compact homomorphisms are compact.

Proof.

For (i), it follows from
[
67
, Rmk. 16.7 (i)]
that
f^{\prime}\circ g=g^{\prime}\circ f
is compact if
g^{\prime}
is.
Since
{\mathrm{Im}}(f^{\prime})\cong V^{\prime}
,
[
67
, Rmk. 16.7 (ii)]
implies that
g
is compact.

In the situation of (ii), we again know from
[
67
, Rem. 16.7 (i)]
that
g^{\prime}\circ f=f^{\prime}\circ g
is compact.
Then by definition there exists an open lattice
L\subset V
such that
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}
is bounded and c-compact.
But as
f
is a strict epimorphism,
f(L)
is an open lattice in
W
which shows that
g^{\prime}
is compact.

For (iii), we consider compact homomorphisms
f\colon V\rightarrow W
and
f^{\prime}\colon V^{\prime}\rightarrow W^{\prime}
with open lattices
L\subset V
,
L^{\prime}\subset V^{\prime}
such that
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{f}
is bounded and c-compact in
W
and the same holds for
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{f}
in
W^{\prime}
.
Then
L\times L^{\prime}
is an open lattice in
V\times V^{\prime}
and
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}=\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{f}\times\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{f}
is bounded in
W\times W^{\prime}
.
Moreover,
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{f}\times\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{f}
is c-compact by
[
67
, Prop. 12.2]
.
∎

Proposition A.4

(
[
74
, Prop. 1.2 (i)]
)
.

Let
V
be a locally convex
K
-vector space of compact type.
If
U\subset V
is a closed subspace then
U
and
V/U
are of compact type again.

Proof.

Let
(V_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
be an inductive sequence of
K
-Banach spaces with injective and compact transition maps such that
V\cong\varinjlim_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}V_{n}
.
By
[
23
, Thm. 3.1.16]
,
U
with its subspace topology is topologically isomorphic to the inductive limit of the sequence
(U_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
where
U_{n}\vcentcolon=U\cap V_{n}
.
Then the transition maps of
(U_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
are compact again
[
67
, Rmk. 16.7]
.
Therefore
U
is of compact type.

Furthermore, by
Lemma

A.3
(ii), the induced maps
V_{n}/U_{n}\rightarrow V_{n+1}/U_{n+1}
are injective and compact.
Hence
\varinjlim_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}V_{n}/U_{n}
is of compact type.
Moreover, taking the inductive limit over the short strictly exact sequences
0\rightarrow U_{n}\rightarrow V_{n}\rightarrow V_{n}/U_{n}\rightarrow 0
we arrive at the sequence of continuous homomorphisms

0\longrightarrow U\longrightarrow V\longrightarrow\varinjlim_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}V_{n}/U_{n}\longrightarrow 0

which is exact as a sequence of
K
-vector spaces.
It follows from the open mapping theorem
[
11
, II. §4.6 Cor.]
that the continuous surjection
V\rightarrow\varinjlim_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}V_{n}/U_{n}
is strict.
Therefore
\varinjlim_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}V_{n}/U_{n}\rightarrow V/U
is a topological isomorphism.
∎

Proposition A.5

(
[
74
, Thm. 1.3]
)
.

The strong dual of a locally convex
K
-vector space of compact type is a nuclear Fréchet space, and the strong dual of a nuclear
K
-Fréchet space is of compact type.

Proof.

If
V
is a locally convex
K
-vector space of compact type, it follows from
[
23
, Thm. 3.1.7 (vii),(viii)]
that
V^{\prime}_{b}
is a nuclear
K
-Fréchet space.

Conversely, let
V
be a nuclear
K
-Fréchet space.
For a decreasing neighbourhood base of
0
consisting of lattices
(L_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
, one obtains an inductive sequence
(V^{\prime}_{L_{n}^{\circ}})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
of certain
K
-Banach spaces
V^{\prime}_{L_{n}^{\circ}}
with
V^{\prime}=\bigcup_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}V^{\prime}_{L_{n}^{\circ}}
, see
[
23
, Def. 2.5.2]
.
Then
[
23
, Prop. 3.1.13]
says that this sequence is semicompact; even compact by Remark
A.2
(ii).
Note that if
K
is spherically complete every locally convex
K
-vector space is polar
[
59
, Thm. 4.4.3 (i)]
.

Moreover, by
[
59
, Cor. 8.5.3]
,
V
in particular is reflexive, and therefore
V^{\prime}_{b}
is barrelled by
[
59
, Thm. 7.4.11 (i)]
.
(If
K
is spherically complete, barrelled and polarly barrelled are equivalent
[
59
, Thm. 7.1.9 (ii)]
.)
Now
[
23
, Cor. 2.5.9]
implies that the inductive limit topology on
\bigcup_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}V^{\prime}_{L_{n}^{\circ}}
agrees with the strong topology of
V^{\prime}
.
Hence
V^{\prime}_{b}
is of compact type.
∎

Lemma A.6
.

Let
(V_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
be a projective sequence of locally convex
K
-vector spaces and
W
a normed
K
-vector space.
Then the canonical continuous homomorphism

(A.2)

\varinjlim_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}{\mathcal{L}}_{b}(V_{n},W)\longrightarrow{\mathcal{L}}_{b}\bigg(\varprojlim_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}V_{n},W\bigg)

is surjective.
If all projections
{\mathrm{pr}}_{n}\colon\varprojlim_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}V_{n}\rightarrow V_{n}
have dense image, or if all
V_{n}
are Hausdorff and the transition homomorphisms
V_{n+1}\rightarrow V_{n}
are compact, then (
A.2
) even is bijective.

Proof.

First consider
f\in{\mathcal{L}}\big(\varprojlim_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}V_{n},W\big)
, and let
B_{W}\vcentcolon=\left\{w\in W\,\middle|\,\lVert w\rVert_{W}\leq 1\right\}
denote the unit ball of
W
so that
f^{-1}(B_{W})\subset\varprojlim_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}V_{n}
is open.
By the definition of the initial topology of
\varprojlim_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}V_{n}
, there exist integers
n_{1},\ldots,n_{r}\in{\mathbb{N}}
and open lattices
L_{n_{i}}\subset V_{n_{i}}
,
i=1,\ldots,r
, such that

{\mathrm{pr}}_{n_{1}}^{-1}(L_{n_{1}})\cap\ldots\cap{\mathrm{pr}}_{n_{r}}^{-1}(L_{n_{r}})\subset f^{-1}(B_{W}).

Let
m\geq n_{1},\ldots,n_{r}
, and note that
{\mathrm{Ker}}({\mathrm{pr}}_{m})\subset{\mathrm{Ker}}({\mathrm{pr}}_{n_{i}})\subset{\mathrm{pr}}_{n_{i}}^{-1}(L_{n_{i}})
, for
i=1,\ldots,r
.
Hence we find that
{\mathrm{Ker}}({\mathrm{pr}}_{m})\subset f^{-1}(B_{W})
.
As
{\mathrm{Ker}}({\mathrm{pr}}_{m})
is a
K
-subvector space, it follows that
{\mathrm{Ker}}({\mathrm{pr}}_{m})\subset{\mathrm{Ker}}(f)
.
Therefore
f
factors over
V_{m}
via
{\mathrm{pr}}_{m}
.
This shows that (
A.2
) is surjective.

If all projections
{\mathrm{pr}}_{n}
have dense image, then the homomorphisms

{\mathcal{L}}(V_{n},W)\longrightarrow{\mathcal{L}}\bigg(\varprojlim_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}V_{n},W\bigg)\,,\quad f\longmapsto f\circ{\mathrm{pr}}_{n},

are injective because
W
is Hausdorff.
As (
A.2
) is the direct limit of these homomorphisms, its injectivity follows.

If all
V_{n}
are Hausdorff and the transition maps are compact, then there exists a projective system
(U_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
such that
\varprojlim_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}V_{n}
and
\varprojlim_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}U_{n}
are topologically isomorphic, and the canonical projections of the latter have dense image
[
67
, p. 93]
.
Moreover, we have
\varinjlim_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}{\mathcal{L}}_{b}(V_{n},W)\cong\varinjlim_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}{\mathcal{L}}_{b}(U_{n},W)
by functoriality, and can conclude using the previous case.
∎

Proposition A.7

(
[
74
, Prop. 1.5]
)
.

Let
V
be a locally convex
K
-vector space of compact type, expressed as
V=\varinjlim_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}V_{n}
, for a sequence of
K
-Banach spaces
V_{n}
with compact and injective transition maps.
Moreover, let
W
be a
K
-Banach space.
Then the canonical continuous homomorphism

\varinjlim_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}\big(V_{n}{\,\widehat{\otimes}_{K}\,}W\big)\longrightarrow V{\,\widehat{\otimes}_{K}\,}W

is bijective.

Proof.

To ease the notation, we will simply denote the strong dual of a locally convex
K
-vector space
U
by
U^{\prime}
.
Using
[
67
, Cor. 18.8]
and the fact that
V
is reflexive, we have a topological isomorphism

V{\,\widehat{\otimes}_{K}\,}W\overset{\cong}{\longrightarrow}V^{\prime\prime}{\,\widehat{\otimes}_{K}\,}W\overset{\cong}{\longrightarrow}{\mathcal{L}}_{b}(V^{\prime},W)\,,\quad v\otimes w\longmapsto\big[\ell\mapsto\ell(v)\,w\big].

For each
V_{n}
, the duality map and
[
67
, Lemma 18.1]
at least give a continuous homomorphism

V_{n}{\,\widehat{\otimes}_{K}\,}W\longrightarrow V_{n}^{\prime\prime}{\,\widehat{\otimes}_{K}\,}W\longrightarrow{\mathcal{L}}_{b}(V_{n}^{\prime},W)\,,\quad v\otimes w\longmapsto\big[\ell\mapsto\ell(v)\,w\big],

so that we arrive at a commutative diagram of continuous homomorphism

(A.3)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varinjlim_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}(V_{n}{\,\widehat{\otimes}_{K}\,}W)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V{\,\widehat{\otimes}_{K}\,}W}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varinjlim_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}{\mathcal{L}}_{b}(V_{n}^{\prime},W)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{L}}_{b}(V^{\prime},W).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

By
[
67
, Lemma 16.4 (ii)]
the projective system
(V_{n}^{\prime})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
is compact, and therefore the lower map in (
A.3
) is a bijection by
Lemma

A.6
.

Hence the claim follows if we show that the left homomorphism is an isomorphism.
But by
[
67
, Lemma 16.4 (iii)]
, the transition maps
V_{n}^{\prime\prime}\rightarrow V_{n+1}^{\prime\prime}
factor over
V_{n+1}\subset V_{n+1}^{\prime\prime}
which gives

\varinjlim_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}(V_{n}{\,\widehat{\otimes}_{K}\,}W)\overset{\cong}{\longrightarrow}\varinjlim_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}\big(V_{n}^{\prime\prime}{\,\widehat{\otimes}_{K}\,}W\big).

Moreover, the image of
V_{n}^{\prime\prime}{\,\widehat{\otimes}_{K}\,}W
in
{\mathcal{L}}_{b}(V_{n}^{\prime},W)
precisely is the subspace of compact homomorphisms
{\mathcal{C}}(V_{n}^{\prime},W)
by
[
67
, Prop. 18.11]
.
It follows from
[
67
, Rmk. 16.7 (i)]
that the transition maps
{\mathcal{L}}_{b}(V_{n}^{\prime},W)\rightarrow{\mathcal{L}}_{b}(V_{n+1}^{\prime},W)
factor over
{\mathcal{C}}(V_{n+1}^{\prime},W)\subset{\mathcal{L}}_{b}(V_{n+1}^{\prime},W)
because they are given by precomposition with the compact maps
V_{n+1}^{\prime}\rightarrow V_{n}^{\prime}
.
Hence

\varinjlim_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}\big(V_{n}^{\prime\prime}{\,\widehat{\otimes}_{K}\,}W\big)\cong\varinjlim_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}{\mathcal{C}}(V_{n}^{\prime},W)\overset{\cong}{\longrightarrow}\varinjlim_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}{\mathcal{L}}_{b}(V^{\prime}_{n},W)

is a topological isomorphism, too.
∎

Lemma A.8

(cf.
[
70
, Lemma 1.3]
)
.

Let
X
be a locally compact topological space.
Let
V
and
W
be locally convex
K
-vector spaces, and assume that
V
is barrelled.
If
\beta\colon X\times V\rightarrow W
is a separately continuous map and
\beta(x,{\,\_\,})\colon V\rightarrow W
is
K
-linear, for every
x\in X
, then
\beta
is jointly continuous.

Proof.

By the linearity of the
\beta(x,{\,\_\,})
it suffices to show that
\beta
is continuous at
(x,0)
, for every
x\in X
.
Fix
x_{0}\in X
, and let
U\subset X
be a compact neighbourhood of
x_{0}
.
We claim that
H\vcentcolon=\left\{\beta(x,{\,\_\,})\,\middle|\,x\in U\right\}\subset{\mathcal{L}}_{s}(V,W)
is bounded.
Assuming this claim for the moment, it follows from the Banach-Steinhaus theorem
[
67
, Prop. 6.15]
that
H
is equicontinuous.
Hence, for any open lattice
M\subset W
, there exists an open lattice
L\subset V
such that
\beta(U,L)\subset M
.
This shows that
\beta
is continuous at
(x_{0},0)
.

To show the claim, recall that the seminorms of
{\mathcal{L}}_{s}(V,W)
are
q_{v,p}
, for
v\in V
and
p
a continuous seminorm of
W
, and defined by
q_{v,p}(f)\vcentcolon=p(f(v))
, for
f\in{\mathcal{L}}_{s}(V,W)

[
67
, Example 1 after 6.6]
.
For such a seminorm, we have

\sup_{f\in H}q_{v,p}(f)=\sup_{x\in U}p(\beta(x,v))<\infty

because the image of the compact subset
U
under the continuous map
p\circ\beta({\,\_\,},v)
is bounded.
It follows that
H\subset{\mathcal{L}}_{s}(V,W)
is bounded.
∎

Lemma A.9
.

Let
(V_{i})_{i\in I}
and
W
be locally convex
K
-vector spaces.
Then there exists a natural topological isomorphism

{\mathcal{L}}_{b}\bigg(\bigoplus_{i\in I}V_{i},W\bigg)\overset{\cong}{\longrightarrow}\prod_{i\in I}{\mathcal{L}}_{b}(V_{i},W)\,,\quad f\longmapsto(f{\!\,\mid_{V_{i}}})_{i\in I}.

(For the case of
W=K
, see
[
67
, Prop. 9.10]
)

Proof.

Let
\iota_{i}\colon V_{i}\rightarrow V\vcentcolon=\bigoplus_{i\in I}V_{i}
denote the canonical embeddings, and
{\mathrm{pr}}_{i}\colon V\rightarrow V_{i}
the canonical projections.
By
[
11
, III. §3 Ex. 5 on p. III.41]
there is a natural topological isomorphism

{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathcal{B}}(V,W)\overset{\cong}{\longrightarrow}\prod_{i\in I}{\mathcal{L}}_{b}(V_{i},W)\,,\quad f\longmapsto(f{\!\,\mid_{V_{i}}})_{i\in I},

where
{\mathcal{B}}
is the family
\left\{\iota_{i}(B_{i})\,\middle|\,i\in I,B_{i}\subset V_{i}\text{ bounded}\right\}
of bounded sets of
V
.
We want to show that the
{\mathcal{B}}
-topology coincides with the strong topology on
{\mathcal{L}}(V,W)
.
In view of
[
67
, Lemma 6.5]
, it suffices to show that for a given bounded subset
B\subset V
, there exist
\iota_{i_{1}}(B_{1}),\ldots,\iota_{i_{m}}(B_{m})\in{\mathcal{B}}
such that
B
is contained in the closure of the
{\mathcal{O}}_{K}
-module generated by
\iota_{i_{1}}(B_{1})\cup\ldots\cup\iota_{i_{m}}(B_{m})
.

To do so, we proceed similarly to the proof of
[
67
, Prop. 9.10]
, and fix a bounded subset
B\subset V
.
By
[
59
, Thm. 3.6.4 (ii)]
, all
{\mathrm{pr}}_{i}(B)\subset V_{i}
are bounded and there exists a finite subset
J\subset I
such that
{\mathrm{pr}}_{i}(B)\subset\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}
, for all
i\in I\setminus J
.
We define the bounded subsets
B_{j}\vcentcolon={\mathrm{pr}}_{j}(B)
, for
j\in J
.
For given
v\in B
, we write
v=\sum_{i\in I}\iota_{i}(v_{i})
where
v_{i}\in V_{i}
.
We then have
\sum_{j\in J}\iota_{j}(v_{j})\in\sum_{j\in J}\iota_{j}(B_{j})
.

To deal with
\sum_{i\in I\setminus J}\iota_{i}(v_{i})
, let
q
be a continuous seminorm of
V
.
By
[
67
, Lemma 5.1 (ii)]
,
q\circ\iota_{i}
is a continuous seminorm of
V_{i}
.
Hence

q(\iota_{i}(v_{i}))=(q\circ\iota_{i})({\mathrm{pr}}_{i}(v))=0,

for all
i\in I\setminus J
, as
{\mathrm{pr}}_{i}(B)\subset\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}
for those
i
.
It follows that, for
i\in I\setminus J
,

\iota_{i}(v_{i})\in\bigcap_{q}{\mathrm{Ker}}(q)=\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}

where we take the intersection over all continuous seminorms
q
of
V
.
This shows that
v
is contained in the closure of
\sum_{j\in J}\iota_{j}(B_{j})
in
V
.
∎

Proposition A.10

(cf.
[
14
, Prop. 1]
)
.

Let
K
be a complete field with absolute value
{\lvert{{\,\_\,}}\rvert}
.
Let
U\subset K^{n}
be open and let
f\colon U\longrightarrow E
be a function into a
K
-Banach space
E
.
Let
V
and
W
be
K
-Banach spaces and let

(A.4)

E\times V\longrightarrow W\,,\quad(u,v)\longmapsto\langle u,v\rangle,

be a continuous
K
-bilinear map which induces an isometric embedding of
E
into the
K
-Banach space
{\mathcal{L}}_{b}(V,W)
, i.e. such that, for all
u\in E
,

{\lvert{u}\rvert}=\left\|\langle u,\_\,\rangle\right\|_{{\mathcal{L}}_{b}(V,W)}\vcentcolon=\sup_{v\in V\setminus\{0\}}\frac{{\lvert{\langle u,v\rangle}\rvert}}{{\lvert{v}\rvert}}\,.

Then, for
z_{0}\in U
, the following are equivalent:
The function
f
is analytic in some open neighbourhood of
z_{0}
.

For all
v\in V
, there exists an open neighbourhood of
z_{0}
such that the function

f_{v}\colon U\longrightarrow W\,,\quad z\longmapsto\langle f(z),v\rangle,

is analytic there.

Proof.

First assume that there exists
r\in{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}
such that
f
is given by the power series

f(z)=\sum_{{\underline{i}}\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}_{0}}a_{{\underline{i}}}\,(z-z_{0})^{\underline{i}}\quad\text{, for $x\in B_{r}^{n}(z_{0})$,}

for certain
a_{{\underline{i}}}\in E
.
For
v\in V
, it follows from the continuity of (
A.4
) that

f_{v}(z)=\langle f(z),v\rangle=\sum_{{\underline{i}}\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}_{0}}\langle a_{{\underline{i}}},v\rangle\,(z-z_{0})^{\underline{i}}\quad\text{, for all $z\in B_{r}^{n}(z_{0})$.}

This shows that (i) implies (ii).

Conversely, for all
v\in V
, let
f_{v}
be analytic in a neighbourhood of
z_{0}
.
This means that there exists a radius
r^{(v)}\in{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}
such that
f_{v}
is given by the convergent power series

f_{v}(z)=\sum_{{\underline{i}}\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}_{0}}a_{\underline{i}}^{(v)}(z-z_{0})^{\underline{i}}\quad\text{, for all $z\in B^{n}_{r^{(v)}}(z_{0})$,}

for certain
a_{\underline{i}}^{(v)}\in W
.
Hence there is a constant
C^{(v)}>0
such that

(A.5)

\displaystyle{\lvert{a_{\underline{i}}^{(v)}}\rvert}\leq C^{(v)}\left(\frac{1}{r^{(v)}}\right)^{{\lvert{{\underline{i}}}\rvert}}\,,

for all
{\underline{i}}\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}_{0}
.
As an intermediate step, we want to show:

Claim:
There are
r>0
and
C>0
such that, for all
{\underline{j}}\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}_{0}
, the maps

b_{\underline{j}}\colon V\longrightarrow W\,,\quad v\longmapsto a_{\underline{j}}^{(v)},

are
K
-linear and continuous with

(A.6)

\displaystyle\lVert{b_{\underline{j}}}\rVert_{{\mathcal{L}}_{b}(V,W)}\leq C\left(\frac{1}{r}\right)^{{\lvert{{\underline{j}}}\rvert}}\,.

Proof of the Claim.

The bilinearity of
\langle{\,\_\,},{\,\_\,}\rangle
implies that

V\longrightarrow\mathrm{Map}(U,W)\,,\quad v\longmapsto f_{v},

is
K
-linear.
The linearity of
b_{\underline{j}}
then follows from the identity theorem for the coefficients of convergent power series
[
12
, 3.2.1 resp. 4.2.1]
.

To proof the continuity and the bound (
A.6
), we endow
{\mathbb{N}}^{n}_{0}
with the lexicographical order, i.e.
{\underline{i}}<{\underline{j}}
if and only if
i_{k}<j_{k}
for the smallest
k\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
where
i_{k}\neq j_{k}
.
We proceed by induction on
{\underline{j}}\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}_{0}
with respect to this order.

Fix
{\underline{j}}\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}_{0}
and assume that the claim holds for all
{\underline{i}}<{\underline{j}}
(a vacuous assumption if
{\underline{j}}=(0,\ldots,0)
).
Hence there are
r_{\underline{j}}>0
and
C_{\underline{j}}>0
such that

{\lvert{a_{\underline{i}}^{(v)}}\rvert}\leq C_{\underline{i}}\left(\frac{1}{r_{\underline{j}}}\right)^{{\lvert{{\underline{i}}}\rvert}}{\lvert{v}\rvert}\quad\text{, for all $v\in V$ and all ${\underline{i}}<{\underline{j}}$.}

Therefore the power series
\sum_{{\underline{i}}<{\underline{j}}}a_{\underline{i}}^{(v)}h_{1}^{i_{1}}\cdots h_{n}^{i_{n}}
converges, for all
(h_{1},\ldots,h_{n})\in B^{n}_{r_{\underline{j}}}(0)
and all
v\in V
.
Moreover, for any
z\in U
, the linear map
V\rightarrow W
,
v\mapsto f_{v}(z)=\langle f(z),v\rangle
, is continuous. Hence, for any
h\in K^{n}
with
z_{0}+h\in B^{n}_{r_{\underline{j}}}(z_{0})\cap U
, the operator

T_{h}\colon V\longrightarrow W\,,\quad v\longmapsto\frac{1}{h_{1}^{j_{1}}\cdots h_{n}^{j_{n}}}\bigg(f_{v}(z_{0}+h)-\sum_{{\underline{i}}<{\underline{j}}}a_{\underline{i}}^{(v)}h^{\underline{i}}\bigg),

is
K
-linear and continuous, too.
For fixed
v\in V
, we may assume
h\in B_{r^{(v)}}^{n}(0)
, and we compute:

\displaystyle\lim_{h_{n}\rightarrow 0}\ldots\lim_{h_{1}\rightarrow 0}T_{h}(v)=

\displaystyle\lim_{h_{n}\rightarrow 0}\ldots\lim_{h_{1}\rightarrow 0}\,\,\,\frac{1}{h_{1}^{j_{1}}\cdots h_{n}^{j_{n}}}\bigg(\sum_{{\underline{i}}\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}_{0}}a_{\underline{i}}^{(v)}h^{\underline{i}}\,-\sum_{{\underline{i}}<{\underline{j}}}a_{\underline{i}}^{(v)}h^{\underline{i}}\bigg)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lim_{h_{n}\rightarrow 0}\ldots\lim_{h_{1}\rightarrow 0}\,\,\,\sum_{{\underline{i}}\geq{\underline{j}}}a_{\underline{i}}^{(v)}h_{1}^{i_{1}-j_{1}}\cdots h_{n}^{i_{n}-j_{n}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lim_{h_{n}\rightarrow 0}\ldots\lim_{h_{2}\rightarrow 0}\,\,\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\underline{i}}\geq{\underline{j}}\\
i_{1}=j_{1}\end{subarray}}a_{\underline{i}}^{(v)}h_{2}^{i_{2}-j_{2}}\cdots h_{n}^{i_{n}-j_{n}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,\,\,a_{\underline{j}}^{(v)}=b_{{\underline{j}}}(v).

By the Banach–Steinhaus theorem
[
11
, III. §4.2 Cor. 2]
we conclude that
b_{\underline{j}}
is continuous as the pointwise limit of the operators
T_{h}
.

It remains to show the bound (
A.6
) for all
{\underline{i}}\leq{\underline{j}}
.
Define, for
k,l\in{\mathbb{N}}
,

B^{k,l}_{\underline{j}}=\big\{v\in V\,\big|\,\forall{\underline{i}}\leq{\underline{j}}\colon{\lvert{a_{\underline{i}}^{(v)}}\rvert}\leq k\,l^{{\lvert{{\underline{i}}}\rvert}}\big\}.

Then each
B_{\underline{j}}^{k,l}
is closed in
V
by the continuity of
b_{\underline{i}}
, for
{\underline{i}}\leq{\underline{j}}
.
By the pointwise bounds (
A.5
), we have
V=\bigcup_{k,l\in{\mathbb{N}}}B_{\underline{j}}^{k,l}
.
Hence it follows from Baire’s theorem
[
9
, Ch. IX. §5.3 Thm. 1]
that some
B_{\underline{j}}^{k,l}
contains an open ball
B_{\varepsilon}(v_{0})
of radius
\varepsilon>0
centred at some
v_{0}\in B_{\underline{j}}^{k,l}
.
Hence, for all
v\in V
with
{\lvert{v}\rvert}<\varepsilon
and all
{\underline{i}}\leq{\underline{j}}
, we have

{\lvert{a_{\underline{i}}^{(v)}}\rvert}={\lvert{a_{\underline{i}}^{(v+v_{0})}-a_{\underline{i}}^{(v_{0})}}\rvert}\leq{\lvert{a_{\underline{i}}^{(v+v_{0})}}\rvert}+{\lvert{a_{\underline{i}}^{(v_{0})}}\rvert}\leq 2k\,l^{{\lvert{{\underline{i}}}\rvert}}\,.

We fix some
\varpi\in K
with
0<{\lvert{\varpi}\rvert}<1
.
For any fixed
v\in V\setminus\{0\}
, let
m\in{\mathbb{Z}}
such that
{\lvert{\varpi}\rvert}^{m}<\frac{\varepsilon}{{\lvert{v}\rvert}}\leq{\lvert{\varpi}\rvert}^{m-1}
.
Then
