\displaystyle{\lvert{a_{\underline{i}}^{(v)}}\rvert}=\frac{{\lvert{a_{\underline{i}}^{(\varpi^{m}v)}}\rvert}}{{\lvert{\varpi^{m}}\rvert}}\leq\frac{2k}{\varepsilon\,{\lvert{\varpi}\rvert}}l^{{\lvert{{\underline{i}}}\rvert}}\,{\lvert{v}\rvert}

which implies

\left\|b_{\underline{i}}\right\|_{{\mathcal{L}}_{b}(V,W)}=\sup_{v\in V\setminus\{0\}}\frac{{\lvert{a_{\underline{i}}^{(v)}}\rvert}}{{\lvert{v}\rvert}}\leq\frac{2k}{\varepsilon\,{\lvert{\varpi}\rvert}}l^{{\lvert{{\underline{i}}}\rvert}}\,,

for all
{\underline{i}}\leq{\underline{j}}
.
∎

It follows from the claim that the power series
\sum_{{\underline{j}}\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}_{0}}b_{\underline{j}}\,(z-z_{0})^{\underline{j}}
defines an analytic function with values in
{\mathcal{L}}_{b}(V,W)
, on some open neighbourhood
U^{\prime}\subset U
of
z_{0}
.
On
U^{\prime}
, this power series agrees with
f
viewed as a map

f\colon U^{\prime}\longrightarrow{\mathcal{L}}_{b}(V,W)\,,\quad z\longmapsto\langle f(z),{\,\_\,}\rangle.

Indeed, for
z\in U^{\prime}
and
v\in V
:

\displaystyle\bigg(\sum_{{\underline{j}}\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}_{0}}b_{\underline{j}}\,(z-z_{0})^{\underline{j}}\bigg)(v)=\sum_{{\underline{j}}\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}_{0}}b_{\underline{j}}(v)\,(z-z_{0})^{{\underline{j}}}=\sum_{{\underline{j}}\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}_{0}}a_{\underline{j}}^{(v)}(z-z_{0})^{\underline{j}}=f_{v}(z)=\langle f(z),v\rangle.

Hence
f(z)=\sum_{{\underline{j}}\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}_{0}}b_{\underline{j}}\,(z-z_{0})^{\underline{j}}
is analytic on
U^{\prime}
as a map into
{\mathcal{L}}_{b}(V,W)
.

Finally,
E
can be identified with a closed subspace of
{\mathcal{L}}_{b}(V,W)
by assumption.
By the same reasoning as in the proof of the claim, the coefficients
b_{\underline{j}}
of
f
can be computed as the limits of sequences in
E
.
Therefore
b_{\underline{j}}\in E
, for all
{\underline{j}}\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}_{0}
, and
f\colon U^{\prime}\rightarrow E
is given by a convergent power series.
∎

Lemma A.11
.

Let
f\colon V\rightarrow W
be a continuous homomorphism between locally convex
K
-vector spaces, and assume that
V
is Hausdorff.
Then in
V^{\prime}
we have the equality

{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}^{s}={\mathrm{Ker}}(f)^{\perp}\vcentcolon=\big\{\ell\in V^{\prime}\,\big|\,\forall v\in{\mathrm{Ker}}(f):\ell(v)=0\big\}

where
{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}^{s}
denotes the closure of the image of the transpose
f^{t}\colon W^{\prime}\rightarrow V^{\prime}
in
V^{\prime}_{s}
.

Moreover, if in addition
V
is semi-reflexive, then
{\mathrm{Ker}}(f)^{\perp}\subset\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}
in
V^{\prime}_{b}
.

Proof.

Taking the transpose twice

{\mathcal{L}}(V,W)\longrightarrow{\mathcal{L}}(W^{\prime}_{s},V^{\prime}_{s})\longrightarrow{\mathcal{L}}\big((V^{\prime}_{s})^{\prime}_{s},(W^{\prime}_{s})^{\prime}_{s}\big),

we see that
f
still defines a continuous homomorphism when
V
and
W
carry the respective weak topologies
V_{s}=(V^{\prime}_{s})^{\prime}_{s}
and
W_{s}=(W^{\prime}_{s})^{\prime}_{s}
.
Then the statement
{\mathrm{Ker}}(f)^{\perp}={\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}^{s}
is part of
[
11
, II. §6.4 Cor. 2]
.

It is a consequence of the Hahn–Banach theorem that the closed subspace
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}\subset V^{\prime}_{b}
is weakly closed as well
[
59
, Thm. 5.2.1]
.
If we assume that
V
is semi-reflexive, i.e. the duality homomorphism
V\rightarrow(V^{\prime}_{b})^{\prime}
is bijective, then the topology on the weak dual of
V
agrees with the weak topology of the strong dual:
V^{\prime}_{s}=(V^{\prime}_{b})_{s}
, see
[
23
, Thm. 7.4.9]
.
Since
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}
now is a closed subset of
V^{\prime}_{s}
which contains
{\mathrm{Im}}(f^{t})
, it follows that
{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}^{s}\subset\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}
.
∎

Lemma A.12
.

Let
V
be a locally convex
K
-vector space and
f\colon V\rightarrow W
a homomorphism of
K
-vector spaces.
If
W
is given the locally convex final topology with respect to
f
, then
f
is strict.

Proof.

Note that
f
as a homomorphism between locally convex
K
-vector spaces is continuous if and only if the induced algebraic isomorphism
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{f}\colon V/{\mathrm{Ker}}(f)\rightarrow{\mathrm{Im}}(f)
is continuous with respect to the quotient respectively subspace topology.

We assume by the way of contradiction that
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{f}
is not a homeomorphism if
W
carries the locally convex final topology with respect to
f
.
In this case we find an open lattice
L\subset V
such that
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{f}(L+{\mathrm{Ker}}(f))
is a lattice in
{\mathrm{Im}}(f)
which is not open.
By extending we obtain a lattice
M\subset W
such that
M\cap{\mathrm{Im}}(f)=\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{f}(L+{\mathrm{Ker}}(f))
.
But
f^{-1}(M)=L+{\mathrm{Ker}}(f)
is an open lattice of
V
.
Therefore
M
must be an open lattice by the definition of the topology on
W
.
This yields a contradiction.
∎

Lemma A.13

(Snake lemma for quasi-abelian categories
[
49
]
14
14

14

Note that the notion of “semiabelian” categories used in
[
49
]
agrees with the one of “quasi-abelian” categories from
[
76
]
, cf.
[
49
, p. 511]
.

)
.

Consider the commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V^{\prime\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W^{\prime\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma}

of continuous homomorphisms between locally convex
K
-vector spaces with strictly exact rows.
Then the induced
{\mathrm{Ker}}
-
{\mathrm{Coker}}
-sequence

(A.7)

0\longrightarrow{\mathrm{Ker}}(\alpha)\overset{\varepsilon}{\longrightarrow}{\mathrm{Ker}}(\beta)\overset{\zeta}{\longrightarrow}{\mathrm{Ker}}(\gamma)\overset{\delta}{\longrightarrow}{\mathrm{Coker}}(\alpha)\overset{\tau}{\longrightarrow}{\mathrm{Coker}}(\beta)\overset{\theta}{\longrightarrow}{\mathrm{Coker}}(\gamma)\longrightarrow 0

of continuous homomorphisms is exact in
{\mathrm{Ker}}(\alpha)
,
{\mathrm{Ker}}(\beta)
,
{\mathrm{Coker}}(\beta)
, and
{\mathrm{Coker}}(\gamma)
.
Furthermore,
\varepsilon
and
\theta
are strict.
We moreover have:
If
\beta
is strict, then (
A.7
) is exact in
{\mathrm{Ker}}(\gamma)
and
{\mathrm{Coker}}(\alpha)
, and
\delta
is strict.

If
\alpha
is strict, then (
A.7
) is exact in
{\mathrm{Ker}}(\beta)
and
{\mathrm{Ker}}(\gamma)
, and
\zeta
is strict.

If
\gamma
is strict, then (
A.7
) is exact in
{\mathrm{Coker}}(\alpha)
and
{\mathrm{Coker}}(\beta)
, and
\tau
is strict.

In particular, if all three
\alpha
,
\beta
, and
\gamma
are strict, then (
A.7
) is a strictly exact sequence.

Appendix B
Continuous and Locally Analytic Characters

In this appendix we consider continuous and locally analytic characters, i.e. one-dimensional representations, of the multiplicative group of a non-archimedean local field of positive characteristic.

However, let us first recall the situation for a
p
-adic field, i.e. a finite extension
K
of
{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
.
Let
{\mathbb{C}}_{p}
be the completion of an algebraic closure of
{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
.
Fixing a uniformizer
{\pi}
in the ring of integers
{\mathcal{O}}_{K}
, there is an isomorphism of topological groups
[
54
, II. Satz 5.3]

(B.1)

K^{\times}\cong{\pi}^{\mathbb{Z}}\times\mu_{q-1}\times U_{K}^{(1)}.

Here
\mu_{q-1}
denotes the group of
(q-1)
-st roots of unity of
K
, where
q
is the number of elements of the residue field
{\mathcal{O}}_{K}/({\pi})
, and

U_{K}^{(n)}\vcentcolon=\big\{x\in{\mathcal{O}}_{K}\,\big|\,x\equiv 1{\,\,\mathrm{mod}\,\,}({\pi}^{n})\big\}\subset{\mathcal{O}}_{K}^{\times}\quad\text{, for $n\in{\mathbb{N}}$.}

Because
\mu_{q-1}
and
{\pi}^{\mathbb{Z}}
both are discrete groups, it suffices to focus on
U_{K}^{(1)}
when considering continuous characters of
K^{\times}
with values in
{\mathbb{C}}_{p}^{\times}
.
For
n>\frac{e}{p-1}
with
e
being the index of ramification of
K/{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
, the logarithm and exponential functions afford an isomorphism of topological groups between
U_{K}^{(n)}
and the additive subgroup
({\pi}^{n})
of
{\mathcal{O}}_{K}

[
54
, II. Satz 5.5]
.
Moreover, every
U^{(n)}_{K}
is of finite index in
U^{(1)}_{K}
.

It follows from Mahler’s theorem
[
72
, Thm. 13.1]
that every continuous additive character
\psi\colon({\pi})\rightarrow{\mathbb{C}}_{p}^{\times}
is of the form
\psi(a)=z_{1}^{a_{1}}\cdots z_{d}^{a_{d}}
, for
z_{i}\in{\mathbb{C}}_{p}
with
{\lvert{z_{i}-1}\rvert}<1
.
Here we write
d\vcentcolon=[K:{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}]
and
a=a_{1}e_{1}+\ldots+a_{d}e_{d}
in some
{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}
-basis
e_{1},\ldots,e_{d}
of
{\mathcal{O}}_{K}

[
72
, Example 16.2]
.
By Amice’s theorem
[
72
, Thm. 13.2]
such a character
\psi
is locally
{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
-analytic, i.e. it is a locally analytic function when its source is considered as a locally
{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
-analytic Lie group.
Moreover, it is locally
K
-analytic if and only if its differential
d_{0}\psi\colon K\rightarrow{\mathbb{C}}_{p}
is not only
{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
-linear but even
K
-linear
[
72
, Prop. 16.3]
.

As the logarithm and exponential functions are locally
K
-analytic, it follows that every continuous character
\chi\colon K^{\times}\rightarrow{\mathbb{C}}_{p}^{\times}
is locally
{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
-analytic, and even locally
K
-analytic if the differential
d_{1}\chi
is
K
-linear.
Furthermore, in the latter case there exists
c\in{\mathbb{C}}_{p}
such that
\chi(z)=z^{c}\vcentcolon=\exp\big(c\log(z)\big)
on
U^{(n)}_{K}
, for
n>\frac{e}{p-1}
.

B.1.
Continuous Characters

We now turn towards the situation of a local non-archimedean field
K
of positive characteristic
p
, say
K={\mathbb{F}}_{q}\mkern-3.0mu\left(\mkern-3.0mu\left(t\right)\mkern-3.0mu\right)
, for
q=p^{r}
.
Of course, the only additive character of
K
or subgroups thereof with values in a field of equal characteristic
p
is the trivial one.
For continuous multiplicative characters of
K^{\times}
, the same decomposition (
B.1
) holds and again reduces the task to studying the continuous characters of
U^{(1)}_{K}
.

To this end, consider more generally abelian metric groups
G
and
H
with
G
locally compact and second countable.
Then the set
{\mathrm{Hom}}_{\mathrm{cts}}(G,H)
of continuous group homomorphisms can be endowed with the compact-open topology whose open sets are given by

{\mathcal{U}}(C,U)\vcentcolon=\big\{f\in{\mathrm{Hom}}_{\mathrm{cts}}(G,H)\,\big|\,f(C)\subset U\big\}\quad\text{, for $C\subset G$ compact and $U\subset H$ open.}

With the pointwise group operation
(f+f^{\prime})(g)\vcentcolon=f(g)+f^{\prime}(g)
,
{\mathrm{Hom}}_{\mathrm{cts}}(G,H)
becomes a metrizable topological group itself
[
53
, Prop. 6.5.2]
.
If moreover
G
is compact, this topology coincides with the one of uniform convergence and a metric is given by
[
53
, p. 238]

d(f,f^{\prime})\vcentcolon=\sup_{g\in G}d_{H}\big(f(g),f^{\prime}(g)\big)\quad\text{, for $f,f^{\prime}\in{\mathrm{Hom}}_{\mathrm{cts}}(G,H)$.}

In the following, we will always view
{\mathrm{Hom}}_{\mathrm{cts}}(G,H)
as a metric group this way.

Let
K\subset L
be a finite field extension with absolute value
{\lvert{{\,\_\,}}\rvert}
on
L
which extends the one of
K
.
Let
f
and
e
be the inertia degree respectively the ramification index of
L
over
K
, i.e.
L\cong{\mathbb{F}}_{q^{f}}\mkern-3.0mu\big(\mkern-3.0mu\big(t^{\frac{1}{e}}\big)\mkern-3.0mu\big)
.
Then the metric on
{\mathrm{Hom}}_{\mathrm{cts}}\big(U_{K}^{(1)},U_{L}^{(1)}\big)
is given by

d(\chi,\chi^{\prime})\vcentcolon=\sup_{z\in U_{K}^{(1)}}{\lvert{\chi(z)-\chi^{\prime}(z)}\rvert}\quad\text{, for $\chi,\chi^{\prime}\in{\mathrm{Hom}}_{\mathrm{cts}}\big(U_{K}^{(1)},U_{L}^{(1)}\big)$.}

We need the following description
[
54
, II. Satz 5.7 (ii)]
(which in turn reproduces
[
40
, Prop. 2.8]
):
Let
\omega_{1},\ldots,\omega_{r}
be an
{\mathbb{F}}_{p}
-basis of
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
and define

E_{K}\vcentcolon=\prod_{p\hskip 1.0pt\nmid\hskip 1.0ptm}\prod_{i=1}^{r}{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}

where the first product is taken over all positive integers
m
which are not divisible by
p
.
Then the map

(B.2)

E_{K}\overset{\cong}{\longrightarrow}U_{K}^{(1)}\,,\quad\big(a_{({m,i})}\big)_{({m,i})}\longmapsto\prod_{p\hskip 1.0pt\nmid\hskip 1.0ptm}\prod_{i=1}^{r}\left(1+\omega_{i}t^{m}\right)^{a_{({m,i})}},

is a well-defined isomorphism of topological groups.

Moreover, consider an
{\mathbb{F}}_{p}
-basis
\omega_{1,1},\ldots,\omega_{r,f}
of
{\mathbb{F}}_{q^{f}}
such that
\omega_{i,1}=\omega_{i}
, for all
i=1,\ldots,r
.
Analogously, we have the isomorphism

\displaystyle E_{L}\vcentcolon=\prod_{p\hskip 1.0pt\nmid\hskip 1.0ptn}\prod_{j,k=1}^{r,f}{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}\overset{\cong}{\longrightarrow}U_{L}^{(1)}\,,\quad\big(b_{({n,j,k})}\big)\longmapsto\prod_{p\hskip 1.0pt\nmid\hskip 1.0ptn}\prod_{j,k=1}^{r,f}\left(1+\omega_{j,k}t^{\frac{n}{e}}\right)^{b_{({n,j,k})}}

of topological groups.
Let
s,e^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{N}}
such that
e=p^{s}e^{\prime}
and
p\nmid e^{\prime}
.
Under the above isomorphisms, the canonical inclusion
U_{K}^{(1)}\hookrightarrow U_{L}^{(1)}
corresponds to the embedding

E_{K}\lhook\joinrel\longrightarrow E_{L}\,,\quad\big(a_{({m,i})}\big)\longmapsto\big(b_{({n,j,k})}\big)\quad\text{, where $b_{({n,j,k})}=\begin{cases}p^{s}a_{(m,j)}&,\text{if $n=e^{\prime}m$ and $k=1$,}\\
0&,\text{else.}\end{cases}$}

Proposition B.1.1
.

There is an isomorphism of topological groups

(B.3)

{\mathrm{Hom}}_{\mathrm{cts}}\big(U_{K}^{(1)},U_{L}^{(1)}\big)\overset{\cong}{\longrightarrow}\prod_{p\hskip 1.0pt\nmid\hskip 1.0ptn}\prod_{j,k=1}^{r,f}c_{0}({{\mathbb{N}}_{p^{\prime}}},{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}^{r})\,,\quad\chi\longmapsto\Big({\underline{a}}_{({n,j,k})}=\big(a_{({n,j,k})}^{({m,i})}\big)\Big),

given by

\chi(1+\omega_{i}t^{m})=\prod_{p\hskip 1.0pt\nmid\hskip 1.0ptn}\prod_{j,k=1}^{r,f}(1+\omega_{j,k}t^{\frac{n}{e}})^{a_{({n,j,k})}^{(m,i)}}.

Here

c_{0}({{\mathbb{N}}_{p^{\prime}}},{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}^{r})\vcentcolon=\left\{{\underline{a}}=\big(a^{({m,1})},\ldots,a^{({m,r})}\big)_{p\hskip 1.0pt\nmid\hskip 1.0ptm}\subset{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}^{r}\,\middle|\,\max_{i=1}^{r}\big\lvert a^{({m,i})}\big\rvert\rightarrow 0\text{ as $m\rightarrow\infty$}\right\}.

carries the structure of a metric group by addition of sequences and the supremum-norm.

Proof.

First we use the description of
U_{L}^{(1)}
as a countable product of copies of
{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}
to reduce to determining
{\mathrm{Hom}}_{\mathrm{cts}}\big(U_{K}^{(1)},{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}\big)
.
This is done by the following probably well-known lemma for which we did not find a reference in the literature.

Lemma B.1.2
.

Let
G
be a locally compact, second countable, abelian metric group, and let
H_{i}
,
i\in I
, be abelian metric groups, for a countable index set
I
.
Then the map

\displaystyle\alpha\colon{\mathrm{Hom}}_{\mathrm{cts}}\bigg(G,\prod_{i\in I}H_{i}\bigg)

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\prod_{i\in I}{\mathrm{Hom}}_{\mathrm{cts}}(G,H_{i})

\displaystyle\chi

\displaystyle\longmapsto({\mathrm{pr}}_{i}\circ\chi)_{i\in I}

is an isomorphism of topological groups.

Proof.

Consider the homomorphisms

\alpha_{j}\colon{\mathrm{Hom}}_{\mathrm{cts}}\bigg(G,\prod_{i\in I}H_{i}\bigg)\longrightarrow{\mathrm{Hom}}_{\mathrm{cts}}(G,H_{j})\,,\quad\chi\longmapsto{\mathrm{pr}}_{j}\circ\chi,

for
j\in I
.
For
C\subset G
compact and
U_{j}\subset H_{j}
open, we have

\alpha_{j}^{-1}\big({\mathcal{U}}(C,U_{j})\big)={\mathcal{U}}\bigg(C,U_{j}\times\prod_{i\in I\setminus\{j\}}H_{i}\bigg).

Therefore the
\alpha_{j}
are continuous.
As
\alpha
is induced from the
\alpha_{j}
by the universal property of the product,
\alpha
is a continuous group homomorphism.

An inverse
\beta
to
\alpha
on the level of group homomorphisms is given by mapping a collection
(\chi_{i}\colon G\rightarrow H_{i})_{i\in I}
of homomorphisms to the homomorphism
\prod_{i\in I}\chi_{i}\colon G\rightarrow\prod_{i\in I}H_{i}
induced by the universal property of the product.
Moreover, this inverse is continuous:
Let
C\subset G
be compact and let
U_{i}\subset H_{i}
be open with
U_{i}=H_{i}
, for almost all
i\in I
.
Then we have

\displaystyle\beta^{-1}\Bigg({\mathcal{U}}\bigg(C,\prod_{i\in I}U_{i}\bigg)\Bigg)=\prod_{i\in I}{\mathcal{U}}(C,U_{i}).

∎

For
m\in{\mathbb{N}}
with
{p\hskip 1.0pt\nmid\hskip 1.0ptm}
, and
i\in\{1,\ldots,r\}
, let
\mathbbm{1}_{(m,i)}\in E_{K}
denote the element with

{\mathrm{pr}}_{(n,j)}\big(\mathbbm{1}_{({m,i})}\big)=\begin{cases}1&,\text{if $m=n$ and $i=j$,}\\
0&,\text{else.}\end{cases}

Via the topological isomorphism (
B.2
), the following description of
{\mathrm{Hom}}_{\mathrm{cts}}(E_{K},{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})
finishes the proof of
Proposition

B.1.1
.

Lemma B.1.3
.

There is an isomorphism of topological groups

(B.4)

\displaystyle{\mathrm{Hom}}_{\mathrm{cts}}(E_{K},{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})

\displaystyle\overset{\cong}{\longrightarrow}c_{0}({{\mathbb{N}}_{p^{\prime}}},{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}^{r})

\displaystyle\chi

\displaystyle\longmapsto\Big(\chi\big(\mathbbm{1}_{(m,1)}\big),\ldots,\chi\big(\mathbbm{1}_{(m,r)}\big)\Big)_{p\hskip 1.0pt\nmid\hskip 1.0ptm}

\displaystyle\bigg[a=\big(a_{(m,i)}\big)\mapsto\sum_{p\hskip 1.0pt\nmid\hskip 1.0ptm}\sum_{i=1}^{r}a_{({m,i})}\lambda^{({m,i})}\bigg]

\displaystyle\longmapsfrom\Big(\lambda^{({m,1})},\ldots,\lambda^{({m,r})}\Big)_{p\hskip 1.0pt\nmid\hskip 1.0ptm},

(cf.
[
22
, Prop. 3.5 and 3.6]
where the above map is shown to be a bijection).

Proof.

For
N\geq 0
, let

E_{K,N}\vcentcolon=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}{p\hskip 1.0pt\nmid\hskip 1.0ptm}\\
m<N\end{subarray}}p^{N}{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}^{r}\times\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}{p\hskip 1.0pt\nmid\hskip 1.0ptm}\\
m\geq N\end{subarray}}{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}^{r}.

The subsets
E_{K,N}
form a system of fundamental open neighbourhoods of
0\in E_{K}
, and satisfy
\mathbbm{1}_{(m)}\vcentcolon=(\mathbbm{1}_{(m,1)},\ldots,\mathbbm{1}_{(m,r)})\in E_{K,N}
, for all
m\geq N
.
Therefore, for
\chi\in{\mathrm{Hom}}_{\mathrm{cts}}(E_{K},{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})
, the continuity of
\chi
implies that the sequence
\big(\chi(\mathbbm{1}_{(m)})\big)_{p\hskip 1.0pt\nmid\hskip 1.0ptm}=\big(\chi(\mathbbm{1}_{({m,1})}),\ldots,\chi(\mathbbm{1}_{(m,r)})\big)_{p\hskip 1.0pt\nmid\hskip 1.0ptm}
tends to
0
in
{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}^{r}
when
m\rightarrow\infty
.

On the other hand, for a zero sequence
\big(\lambda^{(m)}\big)_{p\hskip 1.0pt\nmid\hskip 1.0ptm}\vcentcolon=\big(\lambda^{({m,1})},\ldots,\lambda^{({m,r})}\big)_{p\hskip 1.0pt\nmid\hskip 1.0ptm}
of the right hand side, clearly
a_{({m,i})}\lambda^{({m,i})}
, for
a_{({m,i})}\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}
, is summable.
It follows that (
B.4
) is an isomorphism of (abstract) groups.

It remains to show that (
B.4
) is a homeomorphism.
To do so, we consider a sequence
(\chi_{k})_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}
in
{\mathrm{Hom}}_{\mathrm{cts}}(E_{K},{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})
.
Then convergence of
(\chi_{k})_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}
to
0
is equivalent to

\sup_{a\in E_{K}}{\lvert{\chi_{k}(a)}\rvert}\longrightarrow 0\quad\text{as $k\rightarrow\infty$.}

When this is the case, it clearly follows that

\sup_{{p\hskip 1.0pt\nmid\hskip 1.0ptm}}\big\lvert\chi_{k}\big(\mathbbm{1}_{(m)}\big)\big\rvert\longrightarrow 0\quad\text{as $k\rightarrow\infty$,}

i.e. that
\big(\big(\chi_{k}(\mathbbm{1}_{(m)})\big)_{{p\hskip 1.0pt\nmid\hskip 1.0ptm}}\big)_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}
converges to
0
in
c_{0}({{\mathbb{N}}_{p^{\prime}}},{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}^{r})
.

Conversely, let
\big(\lambda_{k}^{(m)}\big)_{p\hskip 1.0pt\nmid\hskip 1.0ptm}
be a zero sequence of elements of the right hand side corresponding to
\chi_{k}
, for
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
, and assume that
\big(\lambda_{k}^{(m)}\big)_{p\hskip 1.0pt\nmid\hskip 1.0ptm}
converges to
0
in
c_{0}({{\mathbb{N}}_{p^{\prime}}},{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})
.
Then

\displaystyle\sup_{a\in E_{K}}\lvert\chi_{k}(a)\rvert

\displaystyle=\sup_{a\in E_{K}}\bigg\lvert\sum_{p\hskip 1.0pt\nmid\hskip 1.0ptm}\sum_{i=1}^{r}a_{(m,i)}\lambda_{k}^{(m,i)}\bigg\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{a\in E_{K}}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}{p\hskip 1.0pt\nmid\hskip 1.0ptm}\\
i=1,\ldots,r\end{subarray}}\lvert a_{(m,i)}\rvert\big\lvert\lambda_{k}^{(m,i)}\big\rvert\leq\sup_{{p\hskip 1.0pt\nmid\hskip 1.0ptm}}\big\lvert\lambda_{k}^{(m)}\big\rvert\longrightarrow 0\quad\text{as $k\rightarrow\infty$.}

Hence
(\chi_{k})_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}
converges to
0
in
{\mathrm{Hom}}_{\mathrm{cts}}(E_{K},{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})
.
∎

∎

B.2.
Locally Analytic Characters

In contrast to the case of a
p
-adic field, for a local field of positive characteristic, there are significantly less locally analytic characters than continuous characters when compared in a reasonable way.
Furthermore these locally analytic characters behave more rigidly than their
p
-adic counterparts.
The reason for this is the presence of the Frobenius endomorphism
z\mapsto z^{p}
on
K
.

Let
A
be a commutative unital
K
-Banach algebra which is an integral domain.
We now consider locally
K
-analytic characters of
U_{K}^{(1)}
with values in
A^{\times}
, i.e. group homomorphisms
\chi\colon U_{K}^{(1)}\rightarrow A^{\times}
such that
U_{K}^{(1)}\xrightarrow{\chi}A^{\times}\hookrightarrow A
is locally
K
-analytic in the sense of
Definition

1.1.13
(i).
We recall the following (see
[
42
]
for example):

Lemma B.2.1

(Lucas’s theorem)
.

Let
m
and
n
be non-negative integers and
p
a prime.
Then

\binom{m}{n}\equiv\prod_{i=0}^{k}\binom{m_{i}}{n_{i}}{\,\,\mathrm{mod}\,\,}(p),

where
m=\sum_{i=0}^{k}m_{i}p^{i}
and
n=\sum_{i=0}^{k}n_{i}p^{i}
are the
p
-adic expansions, and we use the convention that
\binom{a}{b}=0
if
a<b
, for
a,b\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
.

In particular, we can canonically extend the definition of the binomial coefficient
\binom{c}{n}
modulo
(p)
to
c\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}
and
n\in{\mathbb{Z}}
via

\binom{c}{n}\vcentcolon=\binom{c_{0}}{n_{0}}\cdots\binom{c_{k}}{n_{k}}{\,\,\mathrm{mod}\,\,}(p)

where
c=\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}c_{i}p^{i}
and
n=\sum_{i=0}^{k}n_{i}p^{i}
are the
p
-adic expansions.

Furthermore, we will use hyperderivatives which were originally introduced by Hasse and Teichmüller, and whose properties are recollected in
[
42
, §2]
:
Let
f(z)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}a_{n}(z-z_{0})^{n}
be a formal power series with values in
A
and centred at some
z_{0}\in K
.
Then the
k
-th hyperderivative of
f
is defined as the formal power series

D^{(k)}f(z)=\sum_{n=k}^{\infty}\binom{n}{k}\,a_{n}\,(z-z_{0})^{n-k}.

If
f
is strictly convergent on
B_{r}^{1}(z_{0})\subset K
, for some
r>0
, then
D^{(k)}f
is strictly convergent with the same radius of convergence around
z_{0}
.
Taking
k
-th hyperderivatives is
K
-linear.
We will also use a special instance of the chain rule
[
42
, §2]
(or
[
37
]
) for power series with values in
K
:

D^{(1)}(f\circ g)=\big((D^{(1)}f)\circ g\big)\cdot D^{(1)}g.

Theorem B.2.2
.

Let
\chi\colon U_{K}^{(1)}\rightarrow A^{\times}
be a locally
K
-analytic character.
Then
\chi
factors over the inclusion
U_{K}^{(1)}\hookrightarrow A^{\times}
, and there exists
c\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}
such that
\chi=\chi_{c}
where

(B.5)

\chi_{c}(z)=z^{c}\vcentcolon=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\binom{c}{n}(z-1)^{n}\quad\text{, for all $z\in U_{K}^{(1)}$.}

Moreover, the values of all
p^{i}
-th hyperderivatives of
\chi
at
1
are in fact contained in
{\mathbb{F}}_{p}\subset A
and
c
is uniquely determined by
c_{i}\equiv D^{(p^{i})}\chi(1){\,\,\mathrm{mod}\,\,}(p)
, for the
p
-adic expansion
c=\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}c_{i}p^{i}
.

Proof.

First note that every function
\chi_{c}
as in (
B.5
) defines a (locally)
K
-analytic character from
U_{K}^{(1)}
to itself.
Indeed, for
c\in{\mathbb{N}}
, the equation
\chi_{c}(zw)=\chi_{c}(z)\chi_{c}(w)
follows from the identity of formal power series in
{\mathbb{Z}}\mkern-3.0mu\left[\mkern-2.5mu\left[z,w\right]\mkern-2.5mu\right]
.
Approximation of
c\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}
then yields

\chi_{c}(zw)\equiv\chi_{c}(z)\chi_{c}(w){\,\,\mathrm{mod}\,\,}(t^{n})\quad\text{, for all $n\in{\mathbb{N}}$, $z,w\in U_{K}^{(1)}$,}

where
t
is a uniformizer of
{\mathcal{O}}_{K}={\mathbb{F}}_{q}\mkern-3.0mu\left[\mkern-2.5mu\left[t\right]\mkern-2.5mu\right]
.
This shows the sought functional equation.

Now, let
\chi\colon U_{K}^{(1)}\rightarrow A^{\times}
be a locally
K
-analytic character.
Let
N\geq 1
such that on
U_{K}^{(N)}=1+t^{N}{\mathcal{O}}_{K}
the character
\chi
is given by the strictly convergent power series

\chi(z)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}a_{n}(z-1)^{n}\quad\text{, with $a_{n}\in A$.}

It follows from
\chi(z^{p})=\chi(z)^{p}
that

\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}a_{n}(z^{p}-1)^{n}=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}a_{n}^{p}(z^{p}-1)^{n}\quad\text{, for all $z\in U_{K}^{(N)}$.}

Hence by the identity theorem
1.1.1
, we have
a_{n}=a_{n}^{p}
.
Since
A
is an integral domain by assumption, this implies that
a_{n}
already is contained in
{\mathbb{F}}_{p}\subset A
.
As
{\lvert{a_{n}}\rvert}=1
, for all
n\geq 0
, the power series
\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}a_{n}(z-1)^{n}
strictly converges on the whole of
U_{K}^{(1)}
.
For
z\in U_{K}^{(1)}
, let
j\geq 0
such that
z^{p^{j}}\in U_{K}^{(N)}
.
Then

\chi(z)^{p^{j}}=\chi(z^{p^{j}})=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}a_{n}(z^{p^{j}}-1)^{n}=\bigg(\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}a_{n}(z-1)^{n}\bigg)^{p^{j}}.

Hence it follows that
\chi(z)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}a_{n}(z-1)^{n}
, for all
z\in U_{K}^{(1)}
, using that
A
is an integral domain of characteristic
p
.

Now consider, for all
w\in U_{K}^{(1)}
, the chain rule applied to the hyperderivative with respect to
z
:

\displaystyle\chi(w)\,D^{(1)}\chi(z)=D^{(1)}\big(\chi(w)\chi(z)\big)=D^{(1)}\chi(wz)=D^{(1)}\big(\chi\circ(w{\,\_\,})\big)(z)=D^{(1)}\chi(wz)\cdot w.

Setting
z=1
and noting that
D^{(1)}\chi(1)=a_{1}
, we conclude that

\chi(w)\,a_{1}=w\,D^{(1)}\chi(w)=(w-1)D^{(1)}\chi(w)+D^{(1)}\chi(w)\,.

By the identity theorem for power series
1.1.1
, as
w\in U_{K}^{(1)}
was arbitrary, this is an identity between power series.
It follows that, for all
n\geq 1
,

(B.6)

\displaystyle a_{1}a_{n}=\binom{n+1}{1}a_{n+1}+\binom{n}{1}a_{n}.

For all
l\geq 0
, we want to show by induction on
j
that

(B.7)

\displaystyle a_{lp+j}=\binom{a_{1}}{j}a_{lp}\quad\text{, for $j=0,\ldots,p-1$,}

where we identify
a_{1}\in{\mathbb{F}}_{p}
with its representative in
\{0,\ldots,p-1\}\subset{\mathbb{Z}}
.
For
j=0
, (
B.7
) holds trivially as
\binom{a_{1}}{0}=1
.
Now assume (
B.7
) holds for
j\in\{0,\ldots,p-2\}
.
Then (
B.6
), for
n=lp+j
, together with the induction hypothesis gives

(j+1)a_{lp+j+1}=\binom{lp+j+1}{1}a_{lp+j+1}=\left(a_{1}-\binom{lp+j}{1}\right)a_{lp+j}=(a_{1}-j)\binom{a_{1}}{j}a_{lp}\,.

Hence

a_{lp+j+1}=\frac{a_{1}-j}{j+1}\binom{a_{1}}{j}a_{lp}=\binom{a_{1}}{j+1}a_{lp}

showing (
B.7
) for
j+1
.

Observe that

z^{a_{1}}=\sum_{j=0}^{p-1}\binom{a_{1}}{j}(z-1)^{j},

and therefore

\displaystyle\chi(z)

\displaystyle=\sum_{l=0}^{\infty}\bigg(\sum_{j=0}^{p-1}a_{lp+j}(z-1)^{j}\bigg)(z-1)^{lp}

\displaystyle=\sum_{l=0}^{\infty}\bigg(\sum_{j=0}^{p-1}\binom{a_{1}}{j}(z-1)^{j}\bigg)a_{lp}(z-1)^{lp}=z^{a_{1}}\sum_{l=0}^{\infty}a_{lp}(z^{p}-1)^{l}.

We define, for
z\in U_{K}^{(1)}
and
i\geq 0
,

\chi^{(i)}(z)\vcentcolon=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}a^{(i)}_{n}(z-1)^{n}\quad\text{, where $a^{(i)}_{n}\vcentcolon=a_{np^{i}}$.}

Then
\chi(z)=z^{a_{1}}\chi^{(1)}(z^{p})
.
Using the injectivity of the Frobenius endomorphism this implies that
\chi^{(1)}
is a locally
K
-analytic character on
U_{K}^{(1)}
, too.
Recursively it follows that
\chi^{(i)}
is a locally
K
-analytic character on
U_{K}^{(1)}
, for all
i\geq 0
.
Moreover, (
B.7
) applied to
\chi^{(i)}
gives, for all
l\geq 0
,

(B.8)

\displaystyle a_{lp^{i+1}+jp^{i}}=a^{(i)}_{lp+j}=\binom{a_{1}^{(i)}}{j}a_{lp}^{(i)}=\binom{a_{p^{i}}}{j}a_{lp^{i+1}}\quad\text{, for $j=0,\ldots,p-1$.}

To finish the proof we show that, for all
n\geq 0
,
a_{n}=\prod_{i=0}^{k}\binom{a_{p^{i}}}{n_{i}}
where
n=\sum_{i=0}^{k}n_{i}p^{i}
is the
p
-adic expansion of
n
.
Indeed, fix
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
with such a
p
-adic expansion.
Then (
B.8
), for
i=0,\ldots,k
, gives

\displaystyle a_{n}

\displaystyle=a_{\left(\sum_{i=1}^{k}n_{i}p^{i-1}\right)p+n_{0}}=\binom{a_{1}}{n_{0}}\,a_{\sum_{i=1}^{k}n_{i}p^{i}}

\displaystyle=\binom{a_{1}}{n_{0}}\,a_{\left(\sum_{i=2}^{k}n_{i}p^{i-2}\right)p^{2}+n_{1}p}=\binom{a_{1}}{n_{0}}\binom{a_{p}}{n_{1}}\,a_{\sum_{i=2}^{k}n_{i}p^{i}}

\displaystyle=\ldots=\binom{a_{1}}{n_{0}}\cdots\binom{a_{p^{k}}}{n_{k}}.

Lastly note that
a_{p^{i}}=D^{(p^{i})}\chi(1)
, for all
i\geq 0
.
∎

Corollary B.2.3
.

The locally analytic characters
{\mathrm{End}}_{\mathrm{la}}\big(U_{K}^{(1)}\big)\subset{\mathrm{End}}_{\mathrm{cts}}\big(U_{K}^{(1)}\big)
constitute a closed subring with multiplication given by composition.
Moreover, with the induced subspace topology, the description in (
B.5
) yields an isomorphism

{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}\overset{\cong}{\longrightarrow}{\mathrm{End}}_{\mathrm{la}}\big(U_{K}^{(1)}\big)\,,\quad c\longmapsto\chi_{c},

of topological rings, and the above embedding
{\mathrm{End}}_{\mathrm{la}}\big(U_{K}^{(1)}\big)\subset{\mathrm{End}}_{\mathrm{cts}}\big(U_{K}^{(1)}\big)
corresponds to

(B.9)

\displaystyle\mathrm{diag}\colon{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}\longrightarrow\prod_{p\hskip 1.0pt\nmid\hskip 1.0ptn}\prod_{j=1}^{r}c_{0}({{\mathbb{N}}_{p^{\prime}}},{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}^{r})\,,\quad c

\displaystyle\longmapsto\mathrm{diag}(c)\vcentcolon=\Big({\underline{c}}_{({n,j})}=\big(c_{({n,j})}^{({m,i})}\big)\Big),

where
c_{({n,j})}^{({m,i})}\vcentcolon=\begin{cases}c&,\text{if $m=n$ and $i=j$,}\\
0&,\text{else,}\end{cases}

under this isomorphism and the identification (
B.3
).

Proof.

One readily computes that the image
\mathrm{diag}({\mathbb{Z}}_{p})
is a closed subgroup.
To show that the injective homomorphism (
B.9
) of additive groups is a topological embedding, we show that a sequence
(c_{k})_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}\subset{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}
converges to
0
if and only if
\big(\mathrm{diag}(c_{k})\big)_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}
converges to
0
.
Indeed, the latter is equivalent to convergence of
\big(({\underline{c_{k}}})_{(n,j)}\big)_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}
to
0
in
c_{0}({{\mathbb{N}}_{p^{\prime}}},{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})
, for all
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
with
{p\hskip 1.0pt\nmid\hskip 1.0ptn}
and
j=1,\ldots,r
.
But for the supremum-norm
{\lvert{{\,\_\,}}\rvert}_{\infty}
of
c_{0}({{\mathbb{N}}_{p^{\prime}}},{\mathbb{Z}}_{p})
we compute

\lvert({\underline{c_{k}}})_{(n,j)}\rvert_{\infty}\vcentcolon=\sup_{{p\hskip 1.0pt\nmid\hskip 1.0ptm}}\big\lvert({\underline{c_{k}}})_{(n,j)}^{(m,i)}\big\rvert={\lvert{c_{k}}\rvert}.

The only assertion left to verify is that the isomorphism
{\mathrm{End}}_{\mathrm{la}}\big(U_{K}^{(1)}\big)\cong{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}
is compatible with the multiplication, i.e. that
\chi_{c}\circ\chi_{d}=\chi_{cd}
, for
c,d\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}
.
For
c,d\in{\mathbb{N}}
, this holds because
\chi_{c}(z)
is the usual exponentiation
z^{c}
in this case.
The general case then follows by approximation and continuity. ∎
